LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
Action type: Approval
Application designation: Priority review
Priovant Therapeutics, Inc. submitted a new drug application (NDA 220106) for brepocitinib
30 mg once daily for the treatment of dermatomyositis in adults. NDA 220106 was reviewed by
the multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval, and the signatory
authority concurs with these recommendations.
To support the NDA, the Applicant submitted data from a clinical program consisting of one
pivotal phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial (PVT-2201-301) in
dermatomyositis. The safety package was supplemented by one phase 2b, randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled trial in systemic lupus erythematosus (B7931028), and one phase 2a,
open-label pilot study in skin-predominant dermatomyositis (PVT-2201-202), along with
integrated safety data from ten completed phase 2 studies across multiple autoimmune
indications.
A key methodological issue arose during the review regarding the Applicant’s use of last
observation carried forward (LOCF) imputation to handle missing data in the primary and key
secondary efficacy analyses, which the Agency determined was not appropriate. Following the
Agency’s February 25, 2026, “Filing Review Issues Identified” letter, the Applicant submitted
updated results using multiple imputation, and the Agency’s primary efficacy evaluations are
based on these updated results. The comparison between brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo was
statistically significant and clinically meaningful for the primary endpoint of Total Improvement
Score (TIS) at Week 52 (least squares [LS] mean difference: 14.2 points; 95% CI: 5.52, 22.88; p
=0.0013) and for the first five key secondary endpoints, including change from baseline in
Cutaneous Dermatomyositis Disease Area and Severity Index (CDASI) Activity Score at Week
52 (although improvements in the overall CDASI-A score at Week 52 were likely primarily
driven by improvements in erythema, refer to Section 6.4), Dermatomyositis Outcomes for
Muscle and Skin (DMOMS) score at Week 52, proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points
at Week 52, time to consecutive TIS ≥40 points by Week 52, and proportion of subjects
achieving TIS ≥40 points with daily oral prednisone ≤2.5 mg at both Week 48 and Week 52.
These results contribute to the demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness.
The safety profile of brepocitinib 30 mg is consistent with the known class effects of Janus
kinase (JAK) inhibitors, with the most clinically notable findings including a numerically higher
incidence of serious infections compared to placebo, liver enzyme elevation including cases of
probable drug-induced liver injury, dose-dependent increases in serum creatinine and decreases
in estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) attributable to the drug’s inhibition of organic
cation transporters rather than true renal impairment, decreases in hemoglobin and neutrophils,
3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
and hypoglycemia. Identified risks can be mitigated through labeling, including a Boxed
Warning consistent with the JAK inhibitor class, and further evaluated during routine
pharmacovigilance.
Given the statistically significant and clinically meaningful efficacy demonstrated across the
primary and key secondary endpoints, the robustness of the primary endpoint results to missing
data assumptions, and a safety profile that is manageable through labeling and routine
pharmacovigilance, the overall benefit-risk for brepocitinib 30 mg is favorable for the treatment
of adults with dermatomyositis, as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework. The action for this
application will be Approval. For detailed information supporting this decision, refer to the
detailed reviews included below.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
SEE for brepocitinib was established through one pivotal phase 3, randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled study (PVT-2201-301) that evaluated two dose levels of brepocitinib (15 mg
and 30 mg once daily) in adults with dermatomyositis over a 52-week blinded treatment period
as well as confirmatory evidence from dose response relationship observed in Trial PVT-2201301, a small open-label phase 2 study in skin-predominant dermatomyositis, and
pharmacodynamic and pharmacological data that support the mechanism of action of
brepocitinib.
The pivotal study demonstrated statistically significant efficacy for brepocitinib 30 mg compared
to placebo on the primary endpoint of TIS at Week 52 (LS mean difference of 14.2 points; 95%
CI: 5.52, 22.88; p =0.0013). The study also demonstrated statistically significant improvements
across the first five key secondary endpoints when comparing brepocitinib 30 mg to placebo,
including change from baseline in Cutaneous Dermatomyositis Disease Area and Severity IndexActivity (CDASI-A) Score at Week 52 (LS mean difference: −4.4 points; 95% CI: −6.8, −1.9; p
=0.0005), DMOMS score at Week 52 (LS mean difference: 15.3 points; 95% CI: 4.8, 25.7; p
=0.0043), proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points at Week 52 (adjusted risk difference:
20.2%; 95% CI: 5.1%, 35.3%; p =0.0088), time to consecutive TIS ≥40 points by Week 52
(hazard ratio: 1.5; 95% CI: 1.1, 2.2; p =0.0193), and proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40
points with daily oral prednisone ≤2.5 mg at both Week 48 and Week 52 (adjusted risk
difference: 23.3%; 95% CI: 8.3%, 38.2%; p =0.0022). Formal hypothesis testing stopped at the
sixth key secondary endpoint, proportion of subjects achieving ≥40% improvement with a ≥4point improvement from baseline in CDASI-A score at Week 52, which did not reach statistical
significance (adjusted risk difference: 13.9%; 95% CI: −1.7%, 29.4%; p =0.0805). Note that
improvements in the overall CDASI-A score at Week 52 were likely primarily driven by
improvements in erythema (refer to Section 6.4).
Five of the six individual TIS core set measures favored brepocitinib 30 mg over placebo, with
statistically significant improvements in Physician Global Activity–Visual Analogue Scale
(PhGA-VAS), Patient Global Disease Activity-Visual Analogue Scale (PtGA-VAS),
Extramuscular Global Assessment-Visual Analog Scale (EMGA-VAS), Manual Muscle Testing
4
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
8 (MMT-8), and Health Assessment Questionnaire-Disability Index (HAQ-DI), and the
exception of the most abnormal muscle enzyme assessment, which did not reflect improvement.
Subgroup analyses across age, race, sex, and ethnicity were generally consistent with the overall
population results.
While the primary endpoint yielded statistically significant results, a Clinical Outcome
Assessment (COA) review of the TIS identified challenges regarding data interpretability. The
review confirmed that the individual TIS core set measures are well understood and capture
clinically relevant aspects of dermatomyositis from the patient perspective. However, there is
currently insufficient qualitative and quantitative evidence to fully justify the instrument’s
composite scoring methodology. Specifically, it remains unclear how the thresholds defining the
levels of improvement were derived and assigned. Furthermore, qualitative patient feedback
indicated that patient-reported outcomes (such as the HAQ-DI and PtGA) should carry greater
weight than currently assigned, suggesting the current weighting does not fully reflect the patient
experience. Due to these limitations, a definitive conclusion on the fitness-for-purpose of the
composite TIS could not be reached from a COA perspective. Nevertheless, the clinical review
team considers the observed improvements in the TIS to be clinically meaningful, as the
composite score’s success is driven by robust, concurrent improvements across five of its six
individual core set measures. Because these individual components encompass both objective
clinician assessments and patient-reported outcomes, the overall TIS results, supported by
consistent efficacy across multiple secondary endpoints, demonstrate a clinically significant
treatment effect on the underlying muscle and skin manifestations of the disease.
Confirmatory evidence was provided by the dose response relationship observed in the pivotal
study PVT-2201-301 via placebo, 15 mg QD, and 30 mg QD based on primary and secondary
efficacy endpoints, and the open-label pilot study PVT-2201-202, which demonstrated
improvements in skin disease activity in 5 adults with skin-predominant dermatomyositis, with a
mean reduction in CDASI-A score by Week 12 and a universal rebound in disease activity upon
treatment discontinuation, further supporting the drug-mediated nature of the observed benefit.
The Applicant provided further confirmatory/supportive evidence which includes in vivo
demonstration of brepocitinib pharmacodynamic effect on Type I interferon (IFN) gene
expression over time from participants in study PVT-2201-301 with 30 mg dosing;
pharmacological effect based on ex vivo demonstration of its inhibitory effect on key cytokine
signaling (STAT phosphorylation) in PBMCs isolated from dermatomyositis patients; and in
vitro demonstration of brepocitinib’s ability to protect muscle and skin cells from damage caused
by Type I IFN signaling.
The statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvements across the primary endpoint
and multiple key secondary endpoints, the robustness of the primary endpoint results to missing
data assumptions, and the consistency of findings across multiple efficacy measures and
subgroups in the pivotal trial, further supported by confirmatory clinical data in the phase 2
study, as well as mechanistic and pharmacodynamic data, collectively establish SEE for
brepocitinib 30 mg in the treatment of adults with dermatomyositis.

condition
(DeWane et

•

Dermatomyositis is an idiopathic inflammatory myopathy
characterized by cutaneous manifestations, symmetric and
progressive muscle weakness, and systemic
manifestations involving the pulmonary, cardiac,
gastrointestinal, and vascular systems.
– The prevalence of DM in the United States is 1-6 per
100,000 adults.
– DM predominately affects women at a 2:1 ratio.
– The average age of diagnosis is between 40-60 years.
– Skin manifestations can lead to decreased quality of
life due to severe puritis.
– Myositis and GI involvement can lead to progressive
proximal muscle weakness, dysphagia, dysphonia, and
aspiration.
– Systemic manifestations can be severe and/or lifethreatening, including ILD, pulmonary hypertension,
myocarditis, and arrhythmias.

The totality of the efficacy evidence from Trial PVT-2201-301
demonstrates that brepocitinib 30 mg once daily provides
statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvements
in overall disease activity, physical function, and steroid-sparing
in adults with dermatomyositis, as measured across the primary
endpoint and multiple key secondary endpoints, as well as
improvements in cutaneous manifestations of dermatomyositis,
driven primarily by reductions in erythema. However, based on
COA analyses, the statistically significant changes in the
CDASI-A score do not represent a clinically meaningful
improvement in cutaneous disease severity from the patient's
(b) (4)
perspective
.The robustness of the primary endpoint results to missing
data assumptions, the consistency of findings across multiple
efficacy measures and subgroups, and the statistically
significant improvements observed in five of six individual TIS
core set measures collectively support a favorable benefit
profile for brepocitinib 30 mg. Although uncertainties exist
regarding the TIS composite scoring methodology, inlcuding its
weighting and thresholds, the clinical team considers the robust
improvements across its individual, clinically relevant
components to be meaningful. While methodological issues
related to missing data handling and ICE management were
identified during the review, these were resolved through the
Applicant’s submission of updated analyses and the review
team’s conduct of alternative analyses, neither of which altered
the overall efficacy conclusions. Brepocitinib 30 mg
demonstrated efficacy in a randomized, placebo-controlled
phase 3 trial in dermatomyositis, and may address a significant
unmet medical need in a rare and debilitating autoimmune
disease with limited approved treatment options.

A key methodological issue identified during the review was
the Applicant;s use of LOCF imputation to handle missing

September 5, 2025, only 1 day after SAP v4.0 was

generally consistent with those from the Applicant’s LOCFbased analyses, a key distinction is that under the Agency’s
approach, formal hypothesis testing stopped at the sixth
key secondary endpoint, whereas under the Applicant’s

LOCF approach, all primary and key secondary endpoints
were statistically significant. The evolving nature of the SAP
across multiple versions — with the missing data handling
method changing from multiple imputation in SAP v2.0 to
LOCF in SAP v3.0 and v4.0 — raises questions regarding

• An additional methodological concern was identified

strategy for handling the ICEs of receipt of rescue

from baseline at Week 52 was assigned to subjects
experiencing these ICEs. The adaptive nature of this
approach raises questions regarding the operating
characteristics of the resulting analyses. The review team
conducted two alternative analyses using uniform and
truncated normal distributions, and the conclusions were
consistent across all three approaches, mitigating this
concern. The brepocitinib 15 mg arm did not meet formal
statistical significance for the primary or any key secondary
endpoint per the multiplicity control procedure, and
therefore the evidence of effectiveness is limited to the 30mg dose. Additionally, the study population was
predominantly White (71.8%) and female (77.6%), which
may limit the generalizability of the efficacy findings to more
diverse patient populations, though subgroup analyses did
not identify concerning findings in non-majority subgroups.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Risk and risk
management

•

The safety evaluation of brepocitinib at the proposed dose
of 30 mg by mouth daily was evaluated in the pivotal trial,
Study PVT-2201-301, and pooled safety data across 11
completed phase 2 and 3 studies encompassing various
indications. The identified risks are largely consistent with
those known for the JAK inhibitor class.
Key Risks
• Serious Infections and Viral Reactivation: A higher
incidence of serious infections (e.g., pneumonia, sepsis)
was observed in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm compared to
placebo in the pooled analysis. Viral reactivation,
predominantly herpes zoster (including serious
disseminated cases), was also reported.
• Hepatotoxicity: Liver enzyme elevations and cases of
probable DILI were identified. The underlying disease
complicates detection due to muscle-derived
aminotransferase shedding.
• Renal Laboratory Changes: Dose-dependent increases in
serum creatinine and corresponding decreases in
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) were observed.
These are attributed to the drug’s inhibition of organic
cation transporters (reduced tubular secretion) rather than
true glomerular impairment, as cystatin C levels remained
stable.
• Hematologic Abnormalities: Dose-dependent decreases in
hemoglobin and neutrophil counts were observed in
brepocitinib-treated patients.
• Hypoglycemia: Low glucose events occurred at higher
frequencies in brepocitinib-treated subjects compared to
placebo.
• MACE, Thrombosis, and Malignancy: While MACE,
thromboembolic events, and malignancies were rare in the
clinical program, these remain established risks for the JAK
inhibitor class.
• Embryo-Fetal Toxicity: Nonclinical reproductive and
developmental toxicity studies in animals demonstrated
skeletal malformations, indicating a potential risk to a
developing fetus.

10
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

The safety profile of brepocitinib 30 mg is well-characterized
and consistent with the established risks of the JAK inhibitor
class. The most clinically significant risks identified in the
development program include serious infections, viral
reactivation, hepatotoxicity, hematologic abnormalities, and
hypoglycemia. While major class-related risks such as MACE,
thrombosis, and malignancy were rare in the clinical trials, they
remain important safety considerations. Notably, observed
changes in renal laboratories (creatinine/eGFR) were
determined to be a benign effect of transporter inhibition rather
than true renal toxicity.
The review team concludes that the risks associated with
brepocitinib are acceptable and manageable for the treatment
of adults with dermatomyositis when mitigated through
appropriate labeling. Risk management will rely on classstandard Boxed Warnings (for serious infections, mortality,
malignancies, MACE, and thromboses) and specific Warnings
and Precautions. This includes targeted laboratory monitoring
requirements, such as the addition of GGT to standard liver
panels, to accurately distinguish drug-induced hepatotoxicity
from disease-related muscle enzyme shedding in this specific
patient population, and addition of hypoglycemia to the
Warnings and Precautions to reflect the class-wide Safety
Labeling Change for JAK inhibitors and the hypoglycemia
observed in the brepocitinib clinical development program.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
Boxed Warnings
• Consistent with the JAK inhibitor class, the label will include
Boxed Warnings for serious infections, higher rate of allcause mortality, malignancies, MACE, and thromboses.
Warnings and Precautions and Monitoring
• The label will include warnings and recommended
laboratory monitoring for hepatotoxicity (specifically adding
GGT to distinguish hepatic from muscle-derived enzyme
elevations), hematologic changes (hemoglobin,
lymphocytes, and neutrophils), lipids, and hypoglycemia.
• A Warning and Precaution for hypoglycemia will be
included in the labeling. This aligns brepocitinib with the
Safety Labeling Changes for the JAK inhibitor class and is
supported by observations of hypoglycemia during the
brepocitinib clinical development program.
Patient Selection
• Brepocitinib is not recommended in patients with severe
renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²) due to
uncharacterized pharmacokinetics and safety in this
population.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Brepocitinib 30 mg demonstrates a favorable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of
dermatomyositis in adults. The effectiveness of brepocitinib 30 mg was established through one
adequate and well-controlled phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study (PVT2201-301) that demonstrated statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvements in
overall dermatomyositis disease activity, with confirmatory evidence provided by a phase 2 pilot
study (PVT-2201-202) demonstrating rapid and drug-dependent improvements in
dermatomyositis skin disease, and in vitro and in vivo pharmacological data demonstrating that
brepocitinib, a JAK inhibitor, effectively suppresses key inflammatory cytokines pathways
implicated in dermatomyositis.
In Trial PVT-2201-301, brepocitinib 30 mg achieved a statistically significant improvement in
the primary endpoint of TIS at Week 52, with LS mean TIS values of 47.5 and 33.3 points for
the brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo groups, respectively, yielding an LS mean difference of 14.2
points (95% CI: 5.52, 22.88; p =0.0013). Statistically significant improvements were also
observed for the first five key secondary endpoints, including change from baseline in CDASI-A
Score at Week 52 (LS mean difference: −4.4 points; 95% CI: −6.8, −1.9; p =0.0005) [although
improvements the overall CDASI-A score at Week 52 was likely primarily driven by
improvements in erythema, see Section 6.4], DMOMS score at Week 52 (LS mean difference:
15.3 points; 95% CI: 4.8, 25.7; p =0.0043), proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points at
Week 52 (adjusted risk difference: 20.2%; 95% CI: 5.1%, 35.3%; p =0.0088), time to
consecutive TIS ≥40 points by Week 52 (hazard ratio: 1.5; 95% CI: 1.1, 2.2; p =0.0193), and
proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points with daily oral prednisone ≤2.5 mg at both
Week 48 and Week 52 (adjusted risk difference: 23.3%; 95% CI: 8.3%, 38.2%; p =0.0022). The
steroid-sparing portion of this endpoint represents an important clinical benefit, given the
significant morbidity associated with long-term corticosteroid use in this population. Five of the
six individual TIS core set measures (PhGA-VAS, PtGA-VAS, EMGA-VAS, MMT-8, and
HAQ-DI) demonstrated statistically significant improvements favoring brepocitinib 30 mg over
placebo, with the exception of the most abnormal muscle enzyme assessment. While a COA
review identified methodological uncertainties regarding the TIS composite scoring
methodology and component weighting (particularly suggesting that patient-reported outcomes
may be underweighted), the clinical review team considers the observed improvements to be
clinically meaningful. This conclusion is driven by the robust, concurrent improvements across
the individual core set measures, which include both objective clinician assessments and patientreported outcomes, demonstrating a treatment effect on the underlying muscle and skin
manifestations of the disease. The primary endpoint results were robust to missing data
assumptions, and subgroup analyses were generally consistent with the overall population
findings.
The observed safety profile of brepocitinib 30 mg is consistent with the known class effects of
JAK inhibitors. Key safety findings include a numerically higher incidence of serious infections
(e.g., pneumonia, sepsis) and viral reactivation (predominantly herpes zoster) compared to
placebo. Hepatotoxicity was also identified; while no Hy’s Law cases occurred, cases of
probable drug-induced liver injury (DILI) were noted. Additional findings include dosedependent decreases in hemoglobin and neutrophils, as well as laboratory-based hypoglycemia.
12
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Dose-dependent increases in serum creatinine and corresponding decreases in eGFR were
observed, but these are attributable to the drug’s benign inhibition of organic cation transporters
rather than true glomerular filtration impairment, as cystatin C levels remained stable. Common
treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) included headache, fatigue, nausea, and acne.
Nonclinical studies also identified a potential risk for embryo-fetal toxicity (skeletal
malformations). While major class-related risks such as major adverse cardiovascular events
(MACE), thrombosis, and malignancy were rare in the clinical program, they remain established
risks for this pharmacologic class. There were four deaths in the pivotal trial, PVT-2201-301,
which occurred in the open-label period after the initial data cut and were captured in the 90Day-Safety-Update; cardiogenic shock following coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG),
dermatomyositis flare with worsening interstitial lung disease (ILD), worsening ILD with new
pulmonary hypertension, and post-double valve replacement myocardial rupture.
Overall, the identified risks are generally consistent with the known safety profiles of approved
JAK inhibitors and appear manageable with appropriate risk mitigation strategies. Labeling
should include a Boxed Warning consistent with the JAK inhibitor class, covering increased
risks of serious infections, malignancy, major adverse cardiovascular events, thrombosis, and allcause mortality. Warnings and Precautions should address serious infections, viral reactivation,
hypoglycemia, and specific laboratory monitoring requirements. Notably, liver enzyme
monitoring must include gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) to accurately distinguish druginduced hepatotoxicity from disease-related muscle enzyme elevations in this specific patient
population. Monitoring for hematologic changes (hemoglobin, lymphocytes, neutrophils) and
lipids is also required. Furthermore, brepocitinib is not recommended in patients with severe
renal or hepatic impairment.
The overall benefit-risk profile for brepocitinib 30 mg is favorable for the treatment of adults
with dermatomyositis, and the identified risks can be mitigated through labeling and further
evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Dermatomyositis (DM) is an immune-mediated myopathy characterized by skin rash and muscle
inflammation, which results in progressive, symmetrical proximal muscle weakness. Cutaneous
manifestations include “heliotrope rash” (pink-violaceous periorbital erythema),
“Gottron’s papules” (pink-violaceous papules overlying interphalangeal and
metacarpophalangeal joints), “Gottron’s sign” (pink-violaceous erythema overlying the elbows,
knees and other joints), periungual telangiectasias, “mechanic’s hands” (scaling, fissuring and
ulceration over the distal fingers), scalp erythema, and “Shawl sign” (papulosquamous eruption
of the neck and trunk characterized by erythema and scaling), and are often associated with
pruritus. Additional clinical manifestations include ILD, dysphagia, cardiac involvement, and
association with underlying malignancy. While various therapies are utilized as standard of care
for dermatomyositis management, including topical and systemic corticosteroids, methotrexate,
azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, cyclosporine, and hydroxychloroquine, only one FDAapproved treatment exists: intravenous immunoglobulin, or OCTAGAM 10%, which received
approval for DM in 2022. A substantial unmet medical need persists for therapeutic interventions
in this serious condition.
Brepocitinib for the treatment of dermatomyositis in adults has undergone regulatory review as
NDA 220106. The proposed dosing regimen consists of 30 mg administered orally once daily as
a single immediate-release tablet. Given the serious nature of dermatomyositis, brepocitinib
received Priority Review designation for the review of NDA 220106 based on the potential for
enhanced patient adherence that may lead to improved clinical outcomes. The oral once-daily
administration of brepocitinib offers advantages over intravenous immunoglobulin, which
requires monthly infusions administered over 2−5 consecutive days.
Brepocitinib belongs to the pharmacologic class of JAK inhibitors. By inhibiting JAK-mediated
signal transduction, brepocitinib may attenuate the downstream inflammatory cascade
responsible for muscle and skin injury in patients with dermatomyositis. Although the Applicant
contends that brepocitinib is a potent and selective inhibitor of JAK1 and tyrosine kinase 2
(TYK2), two members of the JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine kinases that mediate cytokine
receptor signal transduction, the nonclinical team’s assessment does not support this
characterization. Specifically, at the proposed clinical dose of 30 mg daily, brepocitinib has been
determined to inhibit TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 in vitro.
Given this broader inhibitory profile, brepocitinib cannot be considered highly selective for
TYK2/JAK1; rather, the demonstrated affinity across JAK family kinases supports its
classification as a JAK inhibitor and TYK2 inhibitor. Therefore, the mechanism of action is
described as a “Janus Kinase (JAK) and Tyrosine Kinase 2 (TYK2) inhibitor” in the brepocitinib
USPI.
To support the proposed indication of treatment of adults with dermatomyositis, the Applicant
has provided data from one adequate and well-controlled pivotal trial, PVT-2201-301. This is an
ongoing, phase 3, multicenter, randomized, parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled
study with a 52-week double-blind treatment period followed by a 52-week open-label treatment
14
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
period in which 241 subjects were enrolled and randomized 1:1:1 to receive either brepocitinib
15 mg orally once daily (81 subjects), brepocitinib 30 mg orally once daily (81 subjects), or
placebo for 52 weeks (79 subjects). Following completion of the blinded treatment period,
eligible subjects who continued into the open-label treatment period received open-label
brepocitinib 30 mg orally once daily for 52 weeks. The primary endpoint was the TIS at Week
52, a composite endpoint based on six Disease Activity Core Set Measures: PhGA-VAS, PtGAVAS, MMT8, HAQ Disability Index, Muscle Enzyme Assessment, and Extramuscular Global
Assessment-VAS. SEE is further supported by the dose-response relationship observed in this
trial, alongside pharmacodynamic and pharmacological data supporting brepocitinib's
mechanism of action. Additional limited supportive efficacy and safety data are derived from a
small, phase 2, open-label study in skin-predominant DM (PVT-2201-202) involving five
subjects. Because brepocitinib is a new molecular entity, primary supportive safety data are
provided via an Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS), which characterizes the overall safety
profile by pooling data from 11 completed phase 2 and 3 studies across various autoimmune
indications into three main safety pools (S1, S2, and S3).
In conclusion, brepocitinib, a JAK and TYK2 inhibitor proposed at 30 mg orally once daily, was
reviewed under Priority Review as NDA 220106 for the treatment of dermatomyositis in adults,
a serious immune-mediated myopathy with substantial unmet medical need. The efficacy of
brepocitinib for the proposed indication is primarily supported by a single pivotal phase 3 trial,
PVT-2201-301, evaluating the primary endpoint of TIS at Week 52, and is supplemented by
dose-response data, pharmacodynamic evidence, and a supportive phase 2 study. Additionally, as
a new molecular entity, brepocitinib's safety profile is characterized through an Integrated
Summary of Safety that pools data from 11 phase 2 and 3 trials across multiple autoimmune
indications.

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The review of this application will focus on establishing the efficacy and safety of brepocitinib
for the treatment of adults with DM. The clinical evidence is centered on a single pivotal phase 3
trial and an integrated safety analysis, and is supported by a small phase 2 study in skinpredominant DM.
The submitted evidence for efficacy and safety is stratified into primary and supportive
categories. The primary evidence for the efficacy and safety of brepocitinib in the proposed
indication of DM is derived from Study PVT-2201-301. This is a pivotal phase 3, randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled trial that evaluated the efficacy and safety of brepocitinib
15 mg and 30 mg over a 52-week treatment period. There also was a small, phase 2, open-label
study in DM (PVT-2201-202) involving five subjects providing limited supportive data in the
proposed indication. The primary source of supportive safety data is presented with the ISS. As
brepocitinib is a new molecular entity, this integrated analysis was utilized to characterize its
overall safety profile. The ISS pools data from 11 completed phase 2 and 3 studies across various
autoimmune indications. The pooling strategy is organized into three main safety pools (S1, S2,
and S3).
Pool S1 is the most relevant for the safety assessment in DM. It integrates 52-week, placebocontrolled data from the pivotal DM study (PVT-2201-301) and a methodologically similar study
in systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) (B7931028). The Applicant justifies this integration
based on shared clinicopathological features, patient populations, and background therapies
between DM and SLE. This approach increases the size of the long-term, placebo-controlled
16
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
safety database, allowing for more precise estimation of event rates for known risks of JAK
inhibition.
Safety Pool S2 is designed to provide the largest possible dataset with a placebo comparator for
safety analysis. This pool includes data from seven completed, randomized studies that had at
least 8 weeks of double-blind, placebo-controlled exposure to a single-treatment arm of oral
brepocitinib; studies PVT-2201-301 in DM, the phase 2b study B7931028 in SLE, the phase 2a
study B7981007 Crohn’s disease, the phase 2b study B7931030 in psoriatic arthritis (PsA), the
phase 2b study B7981005 in ulcerative colitis, the phase 2a study C2501007 in hidradenitis
suppurativa, and the phase 2a study B7931004 in plaque psoriasis. The doses of brepocitinib
included in this safety pool range from 10 mg to 60 mg. A key feature of this pool is that the
safety analysis is restricted to the data collected only during the placebo-controlled portion of
these studies. The primary objective of Pool S2 is to enable a robust comparison of adverse event
rates between various brepocitinib dose groups and a placebo group across a broad range of
subject populations.
Safety Pool S3 represents the collection of safety data intended to inform the overall and longterm safety profile of brepocitinib. This pool includes data from all 11 completed Phase 2 and
Phase 3 clinical trials of oral brepocitinib. Unlike Pool S2, Pool S3 is not limited to placebocontrolled periods; it also incorporates data from open-label extension (OLE) periods and other
non-placebo-controlled phases of the trials. This provides the greatest number of total exposures
to brepocitinib and allows for the evaluation of long-term adverse effects. The only specified
exclusion from this pool is the data from the crossover period of the alopecia areata study
(B7931005).
The review will incorporate all available safety data, including information submitted in safety
updates after the initial application filing. The ISS document itself reflects this process, noting an
update on February 12, 2026, to include exposure-adjusted incidence rates (EAIRs) in response
to an FDA Information Request (ISS, Section 1.3.1). The review will consider all data up to the
most recent data cut-off date to ensure the most current understanding of the safety profile.
The submitted data package appears adequate for a full review of the efficacy and safety of
brepocitinib. The single, adequate, and well-controlled Phase 3 trial, PVT-2201-301, is designed
to provide the primary evidence of efficacy in the target population. The ISS, with its prespecified pooling strategy, provides an adequate framework for evaluating the safety profile of
this new molecular entity, supplementing the primary safety data from the pivotal trial.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Brepocitinib is a small molecule inhibitor of the JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine kinases.
The JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine kinases, which include JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and TYK2,
mediates signal transduction of cytokine receptors. Upon binding of a cytokine to its receptor,
the associated JAKs are activated and participate in the regulation of numerous inflammatory
cascades, including cytokine signaling and production.
In cell-free in vitro assays, brepocitinib inhibited human recombinant TYK2, JAK1, JAK2, and
JAK3 activity with half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values of 22.7, 16.8, 76.6, and
6490nM, respectively. Therefore, brepocitinib was found to be relatively selective for TYK2,
JAK1, and JAK2 compared to JAK3. The IC50 values of M1 major metabolite for all four
enzymes were >14,000nM, indicating no pharmacological activity of the major metabolite
against JAKs. The Applicant claims that brepocitinib has the highest affinity for JAK1 and
TYK2 and that the most potent effects were observed with inhibition of TYK2/JAK1 signaling.
Therefore, the Applicant’s proposed established pharmacologic class is selective TYK2/JAK1
inhibitor.
In cell-based in vitro studies, brepocitinib inhibited interferon (IFN)α-induced STAT3
phosphorylation mediated by the TYK2/JAK1 signaling complex with an IC50 of 10nM in
human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs), an IC50 of 1.7nM in DM subject PBMCs,
and an IC50 of 30nM in human whole blood. Similarly, in human whole blood, brepocitinib
inhibited IL-12-induced STAT4 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 65nM and IL-23-induced
STAT3 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 120nM, both of which are mediated by the TYK2/JAK2
signaling complex. In DM subject PBMCs, brepocitinib inhibited IL-6-induced STAT1 and
STAT3 phosphorylation (mediated by TYK2/JAK1/JAK2) with IC50 values of 8.8 and 30nM,
respectively. Furthermore, brepocitinib inhibited IL-4-induced STAT6 phosphorylation with an
IC50 of 119nM in CD3+ T cells and 111nM in CD14+ monocytes; in human PBMCs, it inhibited
IL-15-induced STAT5 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 65nM. Both of the latter effects are
mediated by the JAK1/JAK3 signaling complex.
Although the Applicant claims that the most potent effects were observed with inhibition of
TYK2/JAK1, the IC50 values for TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and
TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 signaling reported above are all below 123nM, which is the average unbound
plasma concentration observed in the clinical development program for DM at a 30 mg oncedaily (QD) dose. At 123nM, brepocitinib appears capable of inhibiting TYK2/JAK1,
TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 signaling. Thus, brepocitinib is not highly
selective for TYK2/JAK1; rather, it has affinity for multiple JAK kinases. Based on the totality
of the data, it was determined that the established pharmacologic class for brepocitinib is JAK
and TYK2 inhibitor.
Cytokines implicated in DM in cell-based systems include IFNγ, IL-6, IL-12, and IL-23. In in
vitro studies, brepocitinib inhibited various TYK2- and JAK-dependent functions across
23
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
different cell types, including cytokine-induced phosphorylation of STAT proteins in human
PBMCs (including those from DM subjects) and whole blood; differentiation of T helper1, Th17,
and B cells; and IL-12-induced IFNγ production in human PBMCs. In human skeletal muscle
myoblasts, brepocitinib reduced IFN-I-induced myosin surface expression, and in human dermal
microvascular endothelial cells, it attenuated IFN-I-induced alterations to the structure of the
microvascular network. In normal human keratinocytes, brepocitinib decreased IFN-I- or IFNγinduced STAT3 and STAT1 phosphorylation and reduced expression of cytokines such as IL-6,
CCL2, and CXCL10.
While no animal models fully recapitulate all aspects of DM, the Applicant evaluated the antiinflammatory effects of brepocitinib using a rat adjuvant-induced arthritis disease model, which
is broadly used in musculoskeletal inflammation research. In the rat adjuvant-induced arthritis
model, brepocitinib reduced disease severity and hind paw swelling. The totality of in vitro and
in vivo pharmacology data submitted was considered sufficient to support the Applicant’s
rationale for the use of brepocitinib as a treatment for DM.

Drug Information
In subjects with DM, 30 mg QD was the highest dose studied. In healthy subjects, single dose of 200 mg
followed by 175 mg QD for 10 days using brepocitinib oral solutions or suspensions were studied (Study
B7931001). Following 200 mg single dose, the mean Cmax and AUC0-inf of brepocitinib are 2460 ng/mL and
18410 ng*hr/mL, respectively. Following 175 mg QD, the mean Cmax and AUCtau of brepocitinib on Day 10 are
2091 ng/mL and 16180 ng*hr/mL, respectively. No relative bioavailability study was conducted between
brepocitinib oral solutions/ suspensions and oral tablets.
Dose proportionality
Following brepocitinib single oral doses of 3 mg to 200 mg under fasted condition using oral solutions or
suspensions, brepocitinib Cmax and AUC increased approximately dose-proportionally from 3 mg to 100 mg,
but more than dose proportionally from 100 mg to 200 mg (Study B7931001).
Accumulation
Based on post hoc predictions in patients with DM, following 30 mg QD administration, the accumulation ratio
is 1.1 and 1.2 for the mean Cmax and AUC, respectively.
Time to achieve steady state
Following QD administration of brepocitinib solution, steady state appeared to be reached by Day 8 (Study
B7931001).

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Characteristic
Mass balance results

Clearance

Half-life
Metabolic pathway(s)

Primary excretion pathways (%
dose)

Drug Information
Elimination
In the mass balance study (Study B7931014), following a single oral dose of 60 mg of [14C]-brepocitinib
containing approximately 300 nCi radioactivity, 96.7% of the administered radioactivity was recovered within
192 hours postdose, in which 88.0% (7.7% as unchanged form) was in urine and 8.7% (0.8% as unchanged
form) was in feces.
Based on circulating radioactivity in plasma, unchanged brepocitinib and metabolite M1 (the major circulating
metabolite, inactive) accounted for 47.8% and 37.1% of the total radioactivity, respectively. Each of
metabolites M3 and M4 accounted for 3.6% of the total radioactivity. All remaining metabolites accounted for
<1% of total radioactivity.
In urine, M1 and M6b accounted for 51.4% and 10.4% of administered dose, respectively. All other
components were minor (<10% of dose) to trace (<1% of dose) in abundance.
In feces, the predominant drug related compounds were M3 (2.7% of dose) and M4 (1.1% of dose). All other
metabolites were trace (<1% of dose) in abundance.
The apparent oral clearance of brepocitinib ranged from 14.8 to 22.9 L/hour in healthy subjects (Studies
B7931014, B7931019, B7931033, B7931048, VT-2201-101). Based on population PK analysis, the estimated
apparent oral clearance of brepocitinib in patients with DM is 20 L/h.
Following a single oral dose of brepocitinib at 30 mg, the half-life of brepocitinib ranged from 5.4 to 11.9 hours
in healthy subjects (Studies B7931033, B7931048, VT-2201-101).
Brepocitinib is mainly metabolized by CYP1A1 and CYP1A2 with minor contribution from CYP3A4 . Based on
the mass balance study (B7931014), all major metabolism pathways observed in circulation and excreta
involved oxidative modifications of the methyl-pyrazole motif with M1 being both the major inactive circulating
metabolite and the major excreted metabolite. No active metabolites were identified.
In the mass balance study (B7931014), following a single oral dose of 60 mg of [14C]-brepocitinib containing
approximately 300 nCi radioactivity, 96.7% of the administered radioactivity was recovered within 192 hours
postdose, in which 88.0% (7.7% as unchanged form and 51% as M1) was excreted in urine and 8.7% (0.8%
as unchanged form and 0.7% as M1) was excreted in feces.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Characteristic

Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
Based on population PK analysis, there is no clinically significant effect of body weight on the
pharmacokinetics of brepocitinib. A dosage adjustment based on body weight is not recommended.
Age
Based on population PK analysis, there is no clinically significant effect of age on the pharmacokinetics of
brepocitinib. A dosage adjustment based on age is not recommended.
Renal impairment
Following a single oral administration of 30 mg brepocitinib, brepocitinib Cmax and AUC decreased by 5% and
29%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC increased by 33% and 45%, respectively, in subjects with mild renal
impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR): 60 to 89 mL/min, based on Modification of Diet in
Renal Disease (MDRD) formula) compared to subjects with normal renal function. Brepocitinib C max and AUC
increased by 24% and 48%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC increased by 22% and 129%, respectively, in
subjects with moderate renal impairment (eGFR: 30 to 59 mL/min), compared to subjects with normal renal
function. Brepocitinib Cmax and AUC increased by 10% and 12%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC increased by
78% and 346%, respectively, in subjects with severe renal impairment (eGFR: 15 to 29 mL/min) compared to
subjects with normal renal function (Study B7931048). The observed exposure changes in mild and moderate
renal impairment are not considered clinically relevant, and no dosage adjustment is recommended in
subjects with mild and moderate renal impairment. However, brepocitinib is not recommended in subjects with
severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min).
Hepatic impairment
Following a single oral administration of 30 mg brepocitinib, compared to subjects with normal hepatic
function, brepocitinib Cmax decreased by 27% and AUC increased by 19%, M1 Cmax decreased by 19% and
AUC increased by 19%, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child Pugh 7−9) (Study VT-2201-101).
The observed changes in exposure are not considered clinically relevant, and no dosage adjustment is
recommended in subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment.
Smokers
Smoking causes induction of CYP1A1 and CYP1A2 levels. Following 30 mg brepocitinib once daily
administration in patients with dermatomyositis, brepocitinib AUC and Cmax at steady state was estimated to
be 45% and 33% lower, respectively, in current smokers compared to non-current smokers.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism At clinically relevant concentrations, brepocitinib does not inhibit CYPs 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6,
3A4/5, and also does not induce CYPs 3A4, 2B6, 1A2. M1 does not inhibit CYPs 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, and 3A4/5.
Inhibition/induction of transporter At clinically relevant concentrations, brepocitinib inhibits P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2K, OCT1, OCT2, does
systems
not inhibit OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3. M1 inhibits MATE2K, but not other transporters. However, the
impact of brepocitinib as inhibitors of P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2K, OCT1, OCT2 has not been assessed. In
the pivotal efficacy study (Study PVT-2201-301), narrow therapeutic index P-gp/BCRP and OCT1/OCT2
substrates, including colchicine, digoxin, fentanyl, quinidine, dofetilide, procainamide, were prohibited at least
2 weeks prior to randomization and during the study.

Impact on oral contraceptive

Pharmacodynamics

Drug Information
Coadministration of brepocitinib with PORTIA (ethinyl estradiol (EE) and levonorgestrel (LN)), an oral
contraceptive steroid, did not lead to clinically relevant change in PK exposure of EE (C max decreased by 3%,
AUC increased by 18%) and LN (Cmax and AUC decreased by 12% and 7%, respectively) (Study B7931018).
Transcriptome analysis using bulk ribonucleic acid sequencing was performed on whole blood samples from
patients in the phase 3 study (VT-2201-301) to assess the pharmacodynamic effect of brepocitinib on
dermatomyositis -relevant proinflammatory cytokines, such as Type I and II IFN.
The median percentage of baseline for the Type I IFN score was 40.9%, 68.3%, and 92.3% at Week 12,
49.0%, 68.4%, and 102.6% at Week 24, 57.4%, 71.7%, and 85.5% at Week 52 in brepocitinib 30 mg,
brepocitinib 15 mg, and placebo arms, respectively.

The median percentage of baseline for the Type II IFN score was 68.2%, 74.4%, and 100.8% at Week 12,
84.5%, 96.0%, and 114.7% at Week 24, 87.2%, 100.2%, and 105.5% at Week 52 in brepocitinib 30 mg,
brepocitinib 15 mg, and placebo arms, respectively.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Brepocitinib 30 mg and 15 mg QD were selected for the phase 3 study PVT-2201-301 based on
the ex vivo inhibition of cytokine signaling in PBMCs collected from DM subjects, and efficacy
and safety data from phase 2 studies in other indications. Following brepocitinib 30 mg QD
administration, the average free plasma concentration of brepocitinib (123nM) is expected to
result in >96% inhibition of IFNα and IFNβ signaling and >80% inhibition of IL-6 signaling
over a 24-hour dosing interval. The Applicant also relied on efficacy and safety data of
brepocitinib in phase 2 studies in other indications to support the dose selection of brepocitinib
30 mg and 15 mg QD in the phase 3 study.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Trial PVT-2201-301
6.2.1.1. Trial Design, Trial PVT-2201-301
General Study Design
Study PVT-2201-301 is a prospective, parallel-group, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, international, multicenter phase 3 clinical trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of
oral brepocitinib in adults with dermatomyositis. The study population comprises individuals
with definite or probable idiopathic inflammatory myopathy (IIM) who meet the 2017
EULAR/ACR subclassification criteria for classic dermatomyositis, defined as having both
muscle and skin disease involvement. Eligible subjects must have active diseases despite current
or prior corticosteroid and/or immunomodulatory/immunosuppressive therapy, or documented
intolerance to such therapy. A total of 241 subjects were randomized at 89 sites globally. The
total study duration is up to 116 weeks per subject, comprising an up-to-8-week Screening
Period, a 52-week double-blind Blinded Treatment Period, a 52-week OLE Period, and a 4-week
Off-Drug Follow-Up Period (see Figure 1).

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

Visit 1a is for subjects who had an onset of dermatomyositis symptoms <3 years prior to screening or if the timing of onset is
unknown; the screening window for these subjects is Week −8 to 0.
Visit 1b is for subjects who had an onset of dermatomyositis 3 years prior to screening; the screening window for these subjects is
Week −4 to 0.
All subjects who have not received rescue therapy who are taking oral corticosteroids will have a protocol defined steroid taper
beginning at Week 12 and continuing through Week 36 to an oral corticosteroid dose of 5 mg/day (tapering below 5 mg/day is
encouraged based on the investigator’s judgement).
The Off-Drug Follow-Up Period occurs directly after the Blinded Treatment Period for subjects who do not enter the Open-Label
Extension Period.
Abbreviations: PO, oral; QD, once daily; V, visit

The Primary Analysis was conducted after all subjects either completed the Week 52 visit or
prematurely discontinued the study prior to the Week 52 visit and clinical database lock was
declared by data management. Adverse events (AEs), disposition, and exposure data collected in
the OLE Period are reported up to and including March 1, 2025.
The 90 Day Safety Update (90 DSU) was submitted as a supplement to NDA 220106 on
December 29, 2025. The 90 DSU provides updated AE, disposition, and exposure data from the
ongoing OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301 using a data cut of December 1, 2025, representing
an additional 9 months of open-label brepocitinib 30 mg exposure beyond the March 1, 2025,
data cut included in the original submission.
Randomization Scheme
Following confirmation of eligibility during the Screening Period, subjects are centrally
randomized 1:1:1 at baseline (Day 1/Visit 2) to one of three intervention arms using an
interactive response technology system. Randomization is stratified by baseline PhGA-VAS
score, categorized as 0 to 4.9 cm or 5 to 10 cm. The study is blinded to subjects, site personnel,
investigators, and Applicant personnel throughout the Blinded Treatment Period. All subjects,
investigators, and Applicant personnel remain blinded to treatment assignment until after Week
52 database lock.

31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Dose and Duration
During the 52-week Blinded Treatment Period, subjects received one of the following regimens
administered orally QD: brepocitinib 30 mg (n=81), brepocitinib 15 mg (n=81), or matching
placebo (n=79). Two active dose levels were selected to characterize dose-response relationships
for efficacy and safety in this population, informed by prior phase 2 data across multiple
autoimmune indications. All subjects could continue permitted background dermatomyositis
therapies, including oral corticosteroids (at a dose of ≤20 mg/day prednisone equivalent at
screening) and/or up to one systemic nonsteroid immunomodulatory/immunosuppressive agent
at stable doses. A protocol-mandated corticosteroid taper to ≤5 mg/day prednisone equivalent is
required for all subjects receiving corticosteroids at baseline, beginning at Week 12 and
completing by Week 36, with the dose remaining stable through Week 52.
Open-Label Extension Period
Subjects who complete the Blinded Treatment Period and meet specified eligibility criteria—
including having received study drug at Week 52, demonstrating clinical benefit in the
investigator’s opinion, and having no ongoing protocol deviations that would pose safety risks—
are eligible to enter the 52-week OLE Period. During the OLE Period, all subjects, regardless of
their original treatment assignment, receive open-label brepocitinib 30 mg by mouth QD for up
to 52 weeks (Weeks 52 through 104). The OLE Period is designed to provide long-term safety
and efficacy data and to evaluate the durability of response. Study visits during the OLE Period
occur every 4 to 12 weeks. Following completion of either the Blinded Treatment Period (for
subjects not entering the OLE) or the OLE Period, subjects attend a single Off-Drug Follow-Up
Visit approximately 4 weeks after the last dose of study drug.
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint is the TIS at Week 52, a composite measure derived from the six
International Myositis Assessment and Clinical Studies Group disease activity core set measures
(CSMs): PhGA-VAS, PtGA-VAS, MMT-8, HAQ-DI, muscle enzyme assessment (most
abnormal at baseline), and EMGA-VAS. The TIS ranges from 0 to 100, with higher scores
indicating greater improvement; the Applicant labeled threshold scores of ≥20, ≥40, and ≥60 to
correspond to minimal, moderate, and major clinical response, respectively.
Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The key secondary endpoints are as follows:
•

Change from baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52

•

DMOMS score at Week 52, a composite endpoint incorporating MMT-8, CDASI, PhGAVAS, and PtGA-VAS, ranging from 0 to 100

•

Proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points at Week 52

•

Time to achievement of consecutive (≥2 visits) TIS ≥40 points by Week 52

•

Proportion of subjects, regardless of baseline corticosteroid use, achieving TIS ≥40 points at
Week 52 and an oral prednisone dose of 0 to ≤2.5 mg/day (or equivalent) at both Week 48
and Week 52

•

Proportion of subjects achieving ≥40% improvement with a ≥4-point improvement from

•

Clinical Outcome Assessment Description(s)
Total Improvement Score
The TIS is a composite tool designed to quantify treatment response for adult and juvenile DM
and polymyositis. It contains six disease activity CSMs that reflect myositis disease
activity. The six CSMs are described below:
•

Physician’s Global Disease Activity (PhGA) Visual Analog Scale (VAS): A single item
clinician reported outcome (ClinRO) measure assessing overall disease activity using a 0 to
10cm VAS ranging from 0 (“No evidence of disease activity”) to 10 (“Extremely active or
severe disease activity”). The recall period is momentary (at the time of assessment).

•

Patient’s Global Disease Activity (PtGA) VAS: A single item patient-reported
outcome (PRO) measure assessing overall disease activity using a 0 to 10cm VAS ranging
from 0 (“No evidence of disease activity”) to 10 (“Extremely active or severe disease
activity”). The recall period is momentary (at the time of assessment).

•

Extramuscular Global Assessment (EMGA)-VAS component from the Myositis Disease
Activity Assessment Tool: The EMGA-VAS is a single item ClinRO measure assessing
overall disease activity in extramuscular systems using a 0−10cm VAS ranging from 0
(“Absent”) to 10 (“Maximum”). The recall period is momentary (at the time of assessment).

•

HAQ-DI: The HAQ-DI is a 20-item PRO measure assessing functional ability / difficulty
with activities of daily living. The HAQ-DI consists of eight domains: Dressing and
Grooming, Hygiene, Arising, Reach, Eating, Grip, Walking, and Common Daily
Activities. Each item is rated on multiple response scales (e.g., 4-point verbal rating scale,
checklist). The recall period is the previous week (“over the past week”).

•

MMT-8: The MMT-8 is a ClinRO measure quantifying muscle weakness. A manual test
conducted by a clinician to assess muscle strength of eight muscle groups (Axial muscles:
neck flexors; Proximal muscles: deltoids, biceps, gluteus maximus, gluteus medius, and
quadriceps; Distal muscles: wrist extensors and ankle dorsiflexors).

•

Serum levels of muscle enzymes (aldolase, creatine kinase (CK), alanine aminotransferase
(ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)). The most elevated
muscle enzymes at baseline, relative to the upper limit of normal was the muscle enzyme
evaluated as a component of the TIS.

For copies of the COA-related CSM components, refer to the full COA Review.

33
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Scoring
To calculate the TIS, the absolute percent change for each CSM is first calculated using the
following formula:
The absolute percent change = (final value – baseline value)/range × 100
The instrument ranges used in the calculation are based on the scales of the individual
CSMs1. For the muscle enzyme CSM, the most abnormal serum muscle enzyme at baseline is
selected from CK, aldolase, ALT, AST, and LDH.
Next, an improvement level is assigned to each CSM according to the category of improvement
in which the absolute percent change falls. Each improvement category is then converted to an
improvement score with different weight applied to both within and across CSMs (Table 6).
Finally, the TIS is calculated as the sum of the improvement scores across all CSMs. The
resulting TIS ranges from 0 to 100, with higher scores indicating greater overall clinical
improvement.
A TIS is calculated only when all assessments are taken at the same study visit and there are nonmissing values for MMT-8 and PhGA and at least 2 other CSMs. For individual CSMs that are
missing when deriving TIS, a multiple imputation procedure was used to handle the missing
data. To identify the most abnormal baseline muscle enzyme, all five serum muscle enzyme
measurements must be available at baseline.
