Assessment Questionnaire-Disability Index; MMT-8, Manual Muscle Testing-8; PhGA-VAS, Physician Global Disease Activity-Visual
Analogue Scale; PtGA-VAS, Patient Global Disease Activity-Visual Analogue Scale; TIS, Total Improvement Score

CDASI-A Scale
The CDASI-A is one of two scales within the CDASI instrument, a ClinRO measure designed to
assess the extent of cutaneous disease activity and damage in patients with DM. The CDASI-A
assesses the extent of erythema, scale, and erosion/ulceration in 15 different anatomical locations.
The presence and severity of Gottron’s papules on hands, periungual changes and alopecia are
also captured.
Erythema is rated on a 4-point verbal rating scale ranging from 0 (“Absent”) to 3 (“Dark red”).
Scale is rated on a 3-point verbal rating scale ranging from 0 (“Absent”) to 2
(“Crust/Lichenification”). Erosion/ulceration is rated on a 2-point verbal rating scale ranging
34
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
from 0 (“Absent”) to 1 (“Present”). Gottron’s papules (on hands) are rated on a 4-point verbal
rating scale ranging from 0 (“Absent”) to 3 (“Dark red”). Periungal changes are rated on a 3point verbal rating scale ranging from 0 (“Absent”) to 2 (“Visible telangiectasias”). Recent hair
loss (reported by the patient) is rated on a Yes/No response scale. The recall period is current
state, except for the patient reported recent hair loss item in which the recall period is the
previous 30 days.
Scoring
•

The CDASI-A score is based on the assessment of erythema, scale, and erosion/ulceration
at 15 anatomical locations, as well as Gottron’s hands, periungual changes, and recent
alopecia (hair loss within last 30 days). It is calculated as the sum of all individual activity
item scores. If an individual activity item is missing, a value of 0 is imputed, reflecting the
absence of that clinical sign. The CDASI-A score ranges from 0 to 100, with higher
scores indicating greater cutaneous disease activity.
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial PVT-2201-301

Trial PVT-2201-301 enrolled male and female adults between 18 and 75 years of age (inclusive)
with a confirmed diagnosis of dermatomyositis meeting the 2017 EULAR/ACR Classification
Criteria for Idiopathic Inflammatory Myopathies, specifically subclassified as definite or
probable IIM with the dermatomyositis subtype. Subjects with possible IIM were not eligible.
For those with probable IIM, confirmation from an Independent Eligibility Adjudication
Committee was required prior to randomization. Notably, the classification criteria could be met
using historical signs, symptoms, and diagnostic findings, including prior muscle biopsy results.
Subjects were required to demonstrate active disease at both screening and randomization,
defined by two concurrent requirements: an MMT-8 score of ≥80 and ≤142 out of a possible
150, and an active cutaneous manifestation of dermatomyositis documented by a CDASI-A
Score of ≥6. Additionally, subjects were required to exhibit disease activity abnormalities in at
least two of the five following International Myositis Assessment and Clinical Studies Group
(IMACS) Disease Activity Core Set Measures at both screening and randomization (though the
same two measures did not need to be abnormal at both timepoints): PhGA-VAS ≥2 cm, PtGAVAS ≥2 cm, HAQ Disability Index ≥0.25, at least one elevated muscle enzyme (CK, aldolase,
ALT, AST, or LDH) greater than 1.5 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) at screening, or
EMGA-VAS ≥2 cm.
For subjects with onset of dermatomyositis symptoms within 3 years prior to screening, a
documented computed tomography or positron emission tomography – computed tomography
scan with contrast of the chest, abdomen, and pelvis—performed after symptom onset and within
1 year prior to screening—was required, with no findings suggestive of malignancy. If no
qualifying historical scan was available, one was required to be performed during the Screening
Period.
With respect to background therapy, subjects were required to be receiving a current
corticosteroid dose of ≤20 mg/day prednisone equivalent at screening, with a total duration of
prescribed corticosteroid therapy of at least 12 weeks prior to randomization and a stable dose
for at least 4 weeks prior to randomization. Subjects not currently receiving corticosteroids (i.e.,
a dose of 0 mg/day) were also eligible. Additionally, subjects could be receiving at most one
35
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
systemic nonsteroid immunomodulatory/immunosuppressive therapy at a stable dose for at least
12 weeks prior to randomization, at or below the maximally allowed doses specified in the
protocol (e.g., methotrexate ≤25 mg/week, mycophenolate mofetil ≤3000 mg/day, azathioprine
≤2.5 mg/kg/day). An antimalarial could be used in addition to one nonsteroid
immunomodulatory/immunosuppressive agent, provided the antimalarial dose had also been
stable for at least 12 weeks prior to randomization. Subjects not receiving any corticosteroid or
nonsteroid immunomodulatory/immunosuppressive therapy were required to have documented
failure of response or intolerance to at least one prior dermatomyositis-related therapy (an
antimalarial alone did not satisfy this criterion).
Subjects were required to weigh more than 40 kg and less than 130 kg, with a body mass index
(BMI) of less than 40 kg/m².
Notably, Trial PVT-2201-301 excluded subjects if they had dermatomyositis with end-stage
organ involvement that would have posed additional risk or confounded study assessments—
such as advanced symptomatic ILD with abnormal forced vital capacity or requiring oxygen
therapy, or severe dysphagia impacting compliance with oral therapy. Similarly, subjects with
irreversible muscle involvement or severe atrophy that would have confounded disease activity
assessment—including a Muscle Damage-VAS score of ≥7 cm, documented severe atrophy on
magnetic resonance imaging, or wheelchair-bound status—were excluded. Subjects with motionlimiting calcinosis that would have prevented accurate MMT-8 assessment were also ineligible.
The trial also excluded subjects with a history of any lymphoproliferative disorder (e.g., EpsteinBarr virus-related lymphoproliferative disorder, lymphoma, or leukemia), active malignancy,
history of cancer within 5 years prior to randomization (except completely resected basal cell
carcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, ductal carcinoma in situ of the breast, carcinoma in situ of
the uterine cervix, or thyroid carcinoma with no evidence of recurrence for at least 1 year), or
cancer-associated dermatomyositis defined as a myositis diagnosis or symptom onset within 2
years before or after a cancer diagnosis. Subjects with overlap myositis, inclusion body myositis,
polymyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, juvenile dermatomyositis, or druginduced myopathy were not eligible, although secondary Sjögren’s syndrome was permitted.
Subjects with generalized symptomatic musculoskeletal or neuromuscular conditions other than
dermatomyositis that would have prevented adequate investigator assessment were excluded, as
were those with severe liver disease defined as Child-Pugh Class B or C. Subjects were excluded
if they had a history of thrombosis or cerebrovascular ischemic event within the last 12 months
prior to randomization, a history of recurrent (≥2) venous thrombosis or arterial
thromboembolism, or a known hypercoagulable state placing them at high risk for thrombosis.
Additional cardiovascular exclusions included New York Heart Association Class III or IV
cardiac insufficiency, clinically significant cardiac dysrhythmia, a confirmed corrected QT
interval using Fridericia’s formula (QTcF) >450 msec, unstable angina within 3 months prior to
randomization, myocardial infarction within 1 year prior to randomization, or coronary artery
bypass graft surgery within 1 year prior to screening.
Subjects who were recipients of a solid organ transplant currently receiving systemic
immunosuppressive therapy were excluded, as were those with any condition that may have
affected oral drug absorption (e.g., gastrectomy or gastric bypass surgery; gastric banding or
sleeve procedures were not exclusionary). Treatment-naïve subjects (i.e., those with no prior
dermatomyositis therapy with corticosteroids or immunosuppressive agents) were not eligible.
Prior exposure to brepocitinib or participation in a brepocitinib clinical trial was exclusionary, as
36
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
was a history of hypersensitivity to any constituent of the study drug formulation or to other JAK
inhibitors.
With respect to vaccinations, subjects who had received a live vaccine within 6 weeks prior to
randomization, or who were expected to require a live vaccine during the study, were excluded.
BCG vaccination within 52 weeks prior to randomization was also exclusionary. Regarding
infections, subjects with active bacterial, viral, fungal, mycobacterial, or other infections
(excluding those requiring only topical therapy) that would have substantially increased risk
were excluded, as were those with a history of recurrent serious infections. Subjects currently on
suppressive therapy for chronic infections (with the exception of stable herpes simplex virus
suppression), those hospitalized for infection within 60 days prior to randomization, those who
had received intravenous or intramuscular antibacterials, antivirals, antifungals, or antiparasitic
agents within 60 days prior to randomization, or those who had used oral antibiotics for an active
infection within 14 days prior to randomization were also excluded. A history of disseminated
herpes zoster, disseminated herpes simplex, or recurrent (≥2 episodes within the last 5 years)
localized dermatomal herpes zoster was exclusionary.
Subjects with a positive interferon gamma release for tuberculosis at screening or within 12
weeks prior to screening were excluded, with specific provisions allowing enrollment of subjects
with adequately treated latent tuberculosis under defined conditions and with infectious disease
specialist consultation. Subjects with chest X-ray findings suggestive of active tuberculosis were
also excluded. From a laboratory standpoint, subjects were excluded for confirmed positive
hepatitis B surface antigen, certain hepatitis B core antibody positive patterns, hepatitis C
antibody with confirmatory RNA positivity, or HIV infection. Renal exclusions included a serum
creatinine-based eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m², proteinuria defined as a urine protein/creatinine
ratio >1 g/g, or symptomatic nephrolithiasis within 6 months prior to screening. Hematologic and
chemistry exclusions included hemoglobin <10 g/dL, platelet count <100 × 10⁹/L, absolute
neutrophil count <1.5 × 10⁹/L, absolute lymphocyte count <0.75 × 10⁹/L, AST or ALT ≥3× ULN
(or ≥6× ULN if deemed related to myositis disease activity with concomitant CK elevation ≥2×
ULN), and total bilirubin ≥1.5× ULN (with an exception for Gilbert’s syndrome if direct
bilirubin was < ULN).
Additional exclusions included initiation of a new or changed physical therapy or supervised
exercise regimen within 4 weeks prior to randomization or plans to do so during the Blinded
Treatment Period, significant trauma or invasive surgery within 8 weeks prior to randomization,
a history of alcohol or drug abuse unless in full remission for more than 12 months, and
pregnancy, breastfeeding, or unwillingness to use highly effective contraception for the required
duration in women of childbearing potential.
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial PVT-2201-301
Analysis Sets
Intent-To-Treat Set
This analysis set included all randomized subjects. This was the primary analysis set for all
efficacy analyses. Subjects were analyzed based on the randomized treatment assignment.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Safety Analysis Set
This analysis set included all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of study drug.
Subjects were analyzed based on the treatment received. This analysis set was used for safety
analyses.
Safety Extension Analysis Set
This analysis set included all subjects who entered the OLE Period and received at least one dose
of study drug in the OLE Period. Subjects were analyzed based on the treatment or treatment
sequence received.
Sample Size Determination
Subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to receive brepocitinib 30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg, or
placebo. Under the assumptions of mean TIS score at Week 52 is 50 points for brepocitinib and
35 points for placebo with a comment standard deviation of 23 points, the Applicant stated that a
sample size of 75 subjects per treatment group would yield at least 98% power at a two-sided
significance level of 0.05 for comparing the brepocitinib group to the placebo group. Based on
that, the Applicant planned to enroll 225 subjects (75 subjects per treatment group) in the study.
Primary Endpoint Analysis
Primary Analysis
An analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model was used for the primary analysis. The ANCOVA
model included covariates for treatment (3-levels: brepocitinib 15 mg, brepocitinib 30 mg, and
placebo), baseline PhGA-VAS (randomization stratum), and dermatomyositis background
therapy at baseline. Dermatomyositis background therapy at baseline was defined as the
following: 1) corticosteroids only; 2) one systemic nonsteroid immunomodulatory /
immunosuppressant therapy and/or an antimalarial; 3) corticosteroids and one nonsteroid
immunomodulatory/immunosuppressant therapy and/or an antimalarial; 4) none. Primary
inference for a dose level was drawn from the 95% CI of the least squares mean treatment
difference (brepocitinib – placebo) along with the associated two-sided p-value. The intercurrent
event (ICE) of premature treatment discontinuation was handled using the treatment policy
strategy. The ICEs of receipt of rescue medication and death were handled using a composite
strategy where a TIS value of 0 was assigned to subjects who experienced the intercurrent
events.
In statistical analysis plan (SAP) v2.0, the Applicant proposed using a multiple imputation
method to handle missing data for the continuous and binary primary and key secondary
endpoints. In SAP v3.0 submitted on July 24, 2025, and SAP v4.0 submitted on September 4,
2025, the Applicant changed the missing handling approach to a LOCF method. Because the
database lock occurred on September 5, 2025, there was insufficient time for the Division to
review and provide recommendations regarding the final SAP including missing data handling
approaches. During the NDA review cycle, the Agency communicated a “Filing Review Issues
Identified” letter to the Applicant on February 25, 2026. It stated the LOCF method is not
appropriate, as the underlying missingness mechanisms are generally not scientifically justified;
the Agency’s primary evaluation of the efficacy data would utilize multiple imputations to
handle missing data and requested the analyses accordingly. The Applicant submitted the
38
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
updated results using the multiple imputation method on March 11, 2026. In this review, the
study results using multiple imputations, when applicable, are presented.
For the primary endpoint, the multiple imputations were conducted separately for the 6 core set
measures, and then the TIS score is calculated.
Multiple Imputation Steps
1. The SAP reflects that any dependent variable with missing values were imputed using the
Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods. This was done 1000 times, using a noninformative
Jeffreys prior to derive the posterior mode from the expectation-maximization algorithm as
the starting values, assuming a multivariate normal distribution over all variables included in
the imputation model, which includes the dependent variable data by visit. The seed of the
pseudorandom number generator used to randomly generate imputations for the missing
values was 3216549. Additionally, this was conducted by treatment group.
2. Using a standard missing at random-based multiple imputation (MI) approach, the missing
data were imputed using monotone regression. The number 9482038 was used as a seed for
this step. This step was conducted separately within data from each treatment group. At the
end of this step, any imputed data that were out of range for the given dependent variable
were corrected. For example, a PhGA-VAS score which came back either as below 0 or
above 10 was re-set to 0 or 10, respectively.
3. ANCOVA was performed separately for each of the 1000 complete analysis datasets, and the
results were combined into one multiple imputation inference using the MIANALYZE
procedure in SAS (PROC MIANALYZE). The treatment difference was tested at the 2-sided
0.05 level and corresponding 95% CIs were calculated.
Sensitivity Analyses
Based on a request conveyed by the Agency in “Filing Review Issues Identified” letter sent on
February 25, 2026, a tipping point analysis for the primary endpoint was conducted with the shift
𝛿𝑏 in TIS of the imputed values for the brepocitinib group varying independently from the shift
𝛿𝑝 in TIS of imputed values for the placebo group. If the resulting shifts led to certain imputed
values being greater than 100 or less than 0, then the imputed values were set to 100 or 0,
respectively. The two-dimensional sensitivity parameter space to be evaluated included scenarios
where subjects in the brepocitinib group with missing data tended to have worse outcomes than
subjects in the placebo group with missing data. The tipping point analysis results for the twodimensional space were provided as reflected for the following values for the sensitivity
parameters: 𝛿𝑏 = −45, −40, −35, … ,0 and 𝛿𝑝 = 0, 5, 10, … ,40. For each combination of values
for the two sensitivity parameters, the point estimate for the comparison between treatment
groups, the corresponding 95% confidence interval, and the corresponding two-sided p-value
were provided.
Multiplicity Control
The hypothesis testing commenced from the primary endpoint and then the key secondary
endpoints in the order listed in Section 6.2.1.1 at two-sided alpha of 0.05. If the null hypothesis
for an endpoint in the testing hierarchy was not rejected, formal testing was to be stopped for all
subsequent endpoints. All the primary and key secondary endpoints for the brepocitinib 30 mg

39
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
group were to be tested first, followed by testing these endpoints in the brepocitinib 15 mg
group.
Key Secondary Endpoint Analysis Change From Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52
An ANCOVA model with covariates for treatment (3-levels: brepocitinib 15 mg, brepocitinib
30 mg, and placebo), baseline CDASI-A score, baseline PhGA-VAS (randomization stratum),
and dermatomyositis background therapy category at baseline was used for analysis. The ICE of
premature treatment discontinuation was handled using the treatment policy strategy. The ICEs
of receipt of rescue medication and death were handled using a composite strategy. The
Applicant’s prespecified analyses reflected that the observed 5th percentile of clinical
improvement from baseline value at Week 52 was to be assigned to subjects who experienced
these ICEs. To handle missing data, a similar multiple imputation method as that described for
the primary endpoint was used by the Agency.
In the “Mid-Cycle Communication Agenda” provided to the Applicant on April 20, 2026, the
Agency conveyed that the method to handle ICEs of receipt of rescue medication and death
defines the estimand as a function of the data observed within the same study. Due to the
adaptive nature of the estimand definition, the interpretability of results produced using this datadependent method would be a review issue. Refer to Section 6.3.2 for a detailed discussion.
Change From Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 4
This endpoint was analyzed in the same manner as that for the change from baseline in CDASIA Score at Week 52.
Continuous Secondary Endpoints
For all other key continuous secondary endpoints ANCOVA models were used. The ICEs and
missing data were handled in a manner similar to that for the primary endpoint.
Binary Secondary Endpoints
A Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel test was used, stratifying by baseline PhGA-VAS (randomization
stratum), and dermatomyositis background therapy at baseline. The proportion of responders and
Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel risk difference at Week 52 was reported, along with the subject counts
and associated two-sided p-value and 95% CI between each treatment group versus placebo. The
ICE of treatment discontinuation was handled using the treatment policy strategy. The ICEs of
receipt of rescue medication and death were handled using a composite strategy where subjects
were considered nonresponders if they experienced the ICEs. For missing data handling, multiple
imputation described for the primary endpoint was implemented, except a Cochran-MantelHaenszel Test was used for the testing each of the 1000 completed analysis datasets.
Time-to-Event Endpoint
For the time to achievement of consecutive (≥2 visits) TIS ≥40 points by Week 52, a Cox
proportional hazards model was used for analysis. The Cox proportional hazards model included
terms for treatment (3-levels: brepocitinib 15 mg, brepocitinib 30 mg, placebo), baseline PhGAVAS (randomization stratum), and dermatomyositis background therapy at baseline. The
exponentiated model coefficient for treatment was reported, along with the associated two-sided
40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
p-value and 95% CI. Subjects who received rescue medication and subjects who died were
censored at Week 52. The treatment policy strategy was used to handle the ICE of treatment
discontinuation. Subjects who permanently discontinued from the study early for any reason
before achieving consecutive response were censored on the date of their study discontinuation.
Subjects who reached Week 52 without achieving the endpoint were censored in Week 52.
Subgroup Analysis
Analyses were conducted for the primary endpoint using the subgroups defined by the following
factors:
•

Age group: ≤45 versus >45 years

•

Age group: ≤65 versus >65 years

•

Race: White versus Black versus Other

•

Ethnicity: Hispanic or Latino versus Not Hispanic or Latino

•

Sex: Female versus Male

•

Region: U.S. versus Ex-U.S.

•

Region: North America versus Europe versus Asia-Pacific versus Latin America versus Rest
of World

Safety Analysis
All safety analyses for the blinded treatment period were based on the safety analysis set analysis
set when using the while on treatment strategy.
All adverse events were coded using Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities version 27.0.
The incidence of TEAE by primary system organ class and preferred term were presented for all
TEAEs, treatment-related TEAEs, treatment-emergent serious adverse events (SAEs), TEAEs
leading to discontinuation from the study, TEAEs leading to discontinuation of study drug,
TEAEs adverse events of special interests (AESIs), and all TEAEs by maximum intensity. An
adverse event was considered a TEAE if it first occurred, increased in severity, or became
serious on or after the date of the first dose of study drug and within 28 days after the date of last
dose.
All continuous safety laboratory parameters were summarized descriptively by observed value
for each visit by treatment group, together with the corresponding change from baseline.
Laboratory reference ranges were used to identify abnormalities with low, normal, or high
values.
Treatment exposure was calculated for each subject exposed to study drug during the study and
summarized descriptively. This analysis was conducted for the Blinded Treatment Period and
OLE Period separately based on the appropriate safety analysis set (i.e., safety analysis set and
safety extension analysis set, respectively).
Analysis for Six Core Set Measures of TIS
To understand the contribution of individual core set measures to the overall TIS, the following
analyses were conducted. For each of the five CSMs, PhGA-VAS, PtGA-VAS, MMT-8, EMGA41
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
VAS and HAQ-DI, of disease activity used to compute the TIS, analysis was conducted
evaluating the difference in means in change from baseline on the absolute scale, comparing the
brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. For the most abnormal muscle enzyme CSM,
analysis was conducted evaluating the difference in percent change from baseline comparing the
brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. Each CSM analysis used an ANCOVA model
with the following covariates: treatment (3 levels: brepocitinib 30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg, and
placebo), baseline PhGA-VAS (randomization stratum), dermatomyositis background therapy at
baseline (4 levels: corticosteroids only, one systemic nonsteroid
immunomodulatory/immunosuppressant therapy and/or an antimalarial, corticosteroids plus one
nonsteroid immunomodulatory / immunosuppressant therapy and / or an antimalarial/ none) and
baseline value of the CSM being analyzed.
Robust standard errors were used for the analyses. Composite strategy was used to handle the
intercurrent event of death and receipt of rescue medication. Premature treatment
discontinuations were handled with a treatment policy strategy. The multiple imputation
procedure described previously was used to handle missing data.
6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Trial PVT-2201-301
Enrollment and Subject Disposition
A total of 241 subjects were randomized globally, and all of them received at least 1 dose of
study drug. The majority of the subjects (87.1%) completed the Blinded Treatment Period (52
weeks). Fourteen (17.7%) placebo subjects, 11 (13.6%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects and 6 (7.4%)
brepocitinib 30 mg subjects discontinued from the Blinded Treatment Period. Similarly, 20
(25.3%) placebo subjects, 15 (18.5%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects and 9 (11.1%) brepocitinib
30 mg subjects discontinued treatment prematurely (Table 9).
The clinical study report states that at the time of the data cut off for the primary analysis (March
1, 2025), approximately half (n=121) of the subjects who entered the study chose (and were
eligible) to continue into the optional OLE Period to receive brepocitinib 30 mg QD, including
36 subjects initially in the placebo group, 41 subjects initially in the brepocitinib 15 mg group,
and 44 subjects initially in the brepocitinib 30 mg group.

Demographic and Other Baseline Characteristics
The majority of subjects were white (71.8%) and female (77.6%), with a mean age of 50.6 years
(range: 20 to 77 years). The proportion of subjects who are female was slightly lower for the
placebo group compared to the two brepocitinib groups. Other than this, the demographic
characteristics were comparable across the three treatment groups.
Most subjects (58.9%) had a PhGA-VAS of moderate severity at baseline. Baseline disease
activity measures showed highly active disease in skin and muscle, as reflected by the PhGAVAS and CDASI-A. Regarding disease duration, 43.6% of subjects had disease onset fewer than
three years prior to screening, while 56.4% had onset three or more years prior.
Nearly all subjects were receiving at least one permitted background DM therapy at baseline.
The most common regimen was a corticosteroid combined with a nonsteroid immunosuppressant
and/or antimalarial agent, used by 63.9% of subjects overall, while 7.1% received no background
therapy. Among subjects receiving oral corticosteroids at baseline, the mean daily prednisoneequivalent dose across all treatment groups was 11.4 mg, with individual group means of
11.3 mg (SD 5.94) for placebo, 10.7 mg (SD 6.18) for brepocitinib 15 mg, and 12.2 mg (SD
5.73) for brepocitinib 30 mg. Overall, 54.8% of all subjects were receiving a prednisoneequivalent dose greater than 5 mg/day at baseline, 49.0% were receiving greater than 7.5 mg/day,
and 31.5% were receiving greater than 10 mg/day. The baseline corticosteroid data indicates that

43

Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
subjects enrolled in the pivotal trial had a substantial steroid burden, making the steroid-sparing
endpoints meaningful from a clinical standpoint.

6.2.1.5. Endpoint Results, Trial PVT-2201-301
6.2.1.5.1. Primary Endpoint, Trial PVT-2201-301
The comparison between the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group provided
statistically significant results for the primary endpoint of TIS at Week 52. The LS mean (SE)
TIS values were 47.5 (3.38) and 33.3 (3.69) points, for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the
placebo group, respectively. This yielded an LS mean difference (SE) of 14.2 (4.43) points when
comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg to placebo (95% CI: 5.52, 22.88); p =0.0013. There was an
observed numeric increase with the brepocitinib 15 mg compared to placebo.

As shown in Table 12, the only combination of values that tipped the results to statistical
nonsignificance was (𝛿𝐵30 ,𝛿𝑝 ) = (−45, 40). As this combination appeared implausible from the
review team’s perspective, it was concluded that the primary endpoint results were robust to
missing data assumptions. For values of 𝛿𝐵30 in (0, −5, −10,−35), all the p-values were less than
0.05. We do not report them in Table 12.
6.2.1.5.2. Key Secondary Endpoints, Trial PVT-2201-301
Per the multiplicity control strategy (see Section 6.2.1.3), the primary and key secondary
endpoints in the brepocitinib 30 mg group were tested first, followed by testing these endpoints
in the brepocitinib 15 mg group. In the presentation of this section, p-values are provided only
for those endpoints for which formal hypothesis tests were carried out according to this testing
procedure. Since not all key secondary endpoints in the brepocitinib 30 mg group demonstrated
statistical significance, the endpoints in the brepocitinib 15 mg group were not tested. Hence, the
p-values are not presented for brepocitinib 15 mg in this section.

Change From Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52
The comparison between the brepocitinib 30 mg and the placebo provided statistically significant
results for change from baseline (CFB) in CDASI-A score at Week 52, with LS mean (SE) CFB
scores of −12.1 (0.96) and −7.7 (1.12), for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group,
respectively. This yielded an LS mean difference (95% CI) of −4.4 (−6.8, −1.9); p =0.0005. The
brepocitinib 15 mg group displayed a smaller numeric decrease when compared to placebo, with
a dose-response relationship observed.

In this main analysis above in Table 13, the ICEs of receipt of rescue medication and death were
handled using a composite strategy. The observed 5th percentile of clinical improvement from
baseline value at Week 52 was assigned to subjects who experienced these ICEs. As this method
is data-dependent, the review team explored two alternative methods to analyze this endpoint.
The details of the alternative methods and the corresponding results are provided in Section 6.3.
DMOMS Score at Week 52
At Week 52, the comparison between the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group
provided statistically significant results for the DMOMS endpoint. LS mean (SE) DMOMS
scores were 58.6 (4.21) for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and 43.4 (4.70) for the placebo group,
yielding an LS mean difference of 15.3 [95% CI: (4.8, 25.7); p =0.0043] when comparing the
brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. The brepocitinib 15 mg group showed a
numerically smaller improvement relative to placebo.

Proportion of Subjects Achieving TIS ≥40 Points at Week 52
For the proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥40 points at Week 52, the comparison between the

Proportion of Subjects Achieving TIS ≥60 Points at Week 52
Among the subjects who could achieve a maximum possible TIS ≥ 60 points given the baseline
CSMs, there was an observed improvement when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the
placebo group for the proportion of subjects achieving TIS ≥60 points at Week 52, with
responder rates of 50.8% and 29.0%, for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group,
respectively. This yielded an adjusted risk difference (95% CI) of 21.8% (6.1%, 37.6%) when
comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. There was a smaller numeric

difference when comparing the brepocitinib 15 mg group to the placebo group, with an adjusted
risk difference (95% CI) of 7.7% (−7.9%, 23.2%). For the ITT set, an adjusted risk difference
(95% CI) was 21.8% (6.7%, 36.9%) when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the
placebo group. There was a smaller numeric difference when comparing the brepocitinib 15 mg
group to the placebo group, with an adjusted risk difference (95% CI) of 7.8% (−7.0%, 22.5%).
Change From Baseline in HAQ-DI at Week 52
There was an observed improvement when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the
placebo group for CFB in HAQ-DI at Week 52, with LS mean (SE) CFB scores of −0.4 (0.1) and
−0.1 (0.1), for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group, respectively. This yielded a
LS mean difference (95% CI) of −0.3 (−0.5, −0.1) when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group
to the placebo group. The observed improvement for the brepocitinib 15 mg group compared to
the placebo group was smaller than that for the brepocitinib 30 mg group.
Change From Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 4
There was an observed improvement when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the
placebo group, with LS mean (SE) CFB scores of −6.5 (0.7) and −3.5 (0.7), for the brepocitinib
30 mg group and the placebo group, respectively. This yielded an LS mean difference versus
placebo (95% CI) of −2.9 (−4.6, −1.3). The observed improvement for the brepocitinib 15 mg
group compared to the placebo group was smaller than that for the brepocitinib 30 mg group.

6.2.1.5.5. Interpretation of Clinical Meaningfulness of TIS
Endpoint Results, Trial PVT-2201-301
This section details how clinically meaningful change (improvement) in the TIS at Week 52 was
determined using data from Study PVT-2201-301. Refer to Section 6.5 for a discussion of the
TIS.
The Applicant proposed “TIS ≥40 points is meaningful to patients as moderate improvement,
while TIS ≥60 points corresponds to much and very much improved, or a major level of clinical
improvement,” as reported in the literature (Aggarwal et al. 2017). To support the clinical
meaningfulness of the threshold values of 40 and 60, the Applicant submitted result of anchorbased analysis in the COA evidence dossier: “Clinical Outcome Assessments Used in Phase 3
Clinical Trial of Oral Brepocitinib in Adults with Dermatomyositis” dated December 5, 2025.
FDA identified the following limitations with the approach the Applicant used for their anchorbased analysis:
1. A range of clinically meaningful change thresholds was not clearly defined.
2. Primary and supportive anchor measures were not prespecified.
3. Changes or responses on the anchors that represented a meaningful improvement from
patients’ perspectives were not specified for the Patient Global Impression of Severity (PtGIS), Physician Global Impression of Severity (PhGI-S), and Physician Global Impression of
Change (PhGI-C) anchors.
4. The anchor-based analyses were performed using collapsed anchor change categories,
precluding a clear evaluation of how TIS values map to changes in the anchor measures.
5. Subjects’ baseline DM symptom severity, as measured by the PtGI-S, was not incorporated
into the determination of clinically meaningful change.
As such, FDA requested additional anchor-based analysis using un-collapsed anchor change
categories for all the four anchor scales. The following sections discuss the additional analysis
results included in the Applicant’s response to the FDA information request (IR) dated April 24,
2026.
Anchor Assessments in Study PVT-2201-301
Table 21 summarizes the global scales used to support the quantitative anchor-based analyses.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
The PtGI-S, Patient Global Impression of Change (PtGI-C), PhGI-S, and PhGI-C anchors used to
derive meaningful change thresholds for the TIS have limitations that may impact the
interpretability of the results of the anchor-based analyses. Specifically:
1. The concepts measured in the PtGI-S (DM severity) and PtGI-C (DM status) items do not
fully align with the concept measured in the TIS-based endpoint (DM disease activity). To
provide the most direct evidence, we generally recommend that an anchor measure the same
concept as the target instrument (i.e., the specific aspect of disease captured by the endpoint,
rather than overall disease status) to the extent possible. We acknowledge, however, that DM
severity and DM status are closely related to DM disease activity.
2. The recall period of the HAQ-DI (“past week”), a component of the TIS, does not align with
the momentary assessment time period of the prespecified TIS endpoint. We generally
recommend that the anchor scale’s recall period should be consistent with the assessment
time period of the prespecified endpoint to the extent possible. We acknowledge that the
remaining TIS components align with this assessment period.
3. The PtGI-C and PhGI-C have recall periods of up to 52 weeks, which may be prone to recall
bias.
Despite these limitations, the PtGI-S, PtGI-C, PhGI-S, and PhGI-C items can be informative to
help inform the meaningful change thresholds for the TIS-based endpoint score.
For the FDA’s assessment of anchor-based analyses, the PtGI-S was considered the primary
anchor with the PtGI-C, PhGI-S, and PhGI-C as supportive anchors.
Target Anchor Change Category
The Applicant proposed a 1-category to 2-category improvement on the PtGI-C as clinically
meaningful improvement with justification from a qualitative study (refer to Section 5.5.6.1.1 of
the Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment review). However, they did not specify a target
change category on the PtGI-S, PhGI-S, and PhGI-C measures that represent clinically
meaningful improvement to patients to support anchor-based meaningful change analyses.
Ideally, a target anchor change category should be pre-specified by the Applicant and agreed
upon by FDA during the IND phase. As the Applicant did not provide data to support what
constitutes a meaningful change to patients on three of the four anchors, after internal discussion,
FDA consider it acceptable to use a 1-category to 2-category improvement on each of the four
anchors to support further anchor-based analyses.
Meaningful Change Threshold Range
Figure 2 below shows the empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) of TIS by changing
baseline in PtGI-S at Week 52 in Study PVT-2201-301. The eCDF plot demonstrates clear and
consistent separation between the target anchor change category of a 1-category improvement
and the “no change” curve. The median TIS among subjects who reported a 1-category
improvement in PtGI-S was −55.0. However, to maintain a misclassification rate of no more than
20% of subjects who reported no change on the anchor but would be classified as having
experienced meaningful change, the corresponding TIS threshold would be −57.5.

associated TIS thresholds were −56.3, −52.5, and −45.0 for PhGI-S, PtGI-C, and PhGI-C,
respectively.

The amount of change that subjects consider a meaningful improvement may depend on their
baseline symptom severity. Table 23 shows the distribution of TIS by baseline PtGI-S for
subjects who experienced the target 1-category improvement in PtGI-S. As shown in Table 23,
the median change scores for subjects who reported “Mild,” “Moderate,” and “Severe” DM
symptoms at baseline are 55.0, 52.5, and 55.0 points, respectively. As such, a 55.0-point
improvement in TIS would be needed to ensure that at least 50% of subjects who reported
“Mild,” “Moderate,” or “Severe” DM symptoms at baseline experienced a meaningful

improvement in TIS. Based on the analysis results above, FDA determined that a range of 45 to
57.5 points’ improvement represents a clinically meaningful improvement in TIS from subjects’
perspective.

Based on the anchor-based analyses described above, supplemented with eCDF curves,
minimizing the misclassification of subjects who reported no improvement on the anchor scales,
and taking into account subjects’ baseline DM symptom severity, FDA determined that a range
of 45 to 57.5 points’ improvement represents a clinically meaningful improvement in TIS from
subjects’ perspectives.
Conclusion
To determine how much more likely average subjects receiving treatment over placebo would be
to experience meaningful improvement in TIS, the review team examined the eCDF plot of
within-subject changes in TIS at Week 52 by treatment arm. Figure 6 shows a consistent
separation between treatment arms based on visual inspection in the FDA-determined
meaningful change range of 45-point to 57.5-point improvement. A greater proportion of
subjects treated with brepocitinib 30 mg experienced meaningful change compared to subjects
who received placebo in the FDA-determined range.

6.2.1.5.6. Interpretation of the CDASI-A Endpoint Results,
Trial PVT-2201-301
This section details how clinically meaningful change (improvement) in change from baseline in
the CDASI-A at Week 52 was determined using data from Study PVT-2201-301.
The Applicant proposed an improvement range of 6 to 10 points as “perceptible reduction”
(improvement) and 10 to 15 or more points as “much and very much improved.” These values
were established via triangulation of results of the anchor-based analyses using PtGI-S, PhGI-S,
PtGI-C, and PhGI-C as anchors. To support the clinical meaningfulness of the proposed values,
the Applicant submitted result of their anchor-based analyses in the COA evidence dossier:
“Clinical Outcome Assessments Used in Phase 3 Clinical Trial of Oral Brepocitinib in Adults
with Dermatomyositis” dated December 5, 2025.
FDA identified the following limitations with the approach the Applicant used for their anchorbased analysis:
1. Primary and supportive anchor measures were not prespecified.
2. Changes or responses on the anchors that represented a meaningful improvement from
subjects’ perspectives were not specified for the PtGI-S, PhGI-S, and PhGI-C anchors.
3. The anchor-based analyses were performed using collapsed anchor change categories,
precluding a clear evaluation of how CDASI-A values map to changes in the anchor scales.
4. Subjects’ baseline DM symptom severity, as measured by the PtGI-S, was not incorporated
into the determination of clinically meaningful change.
59
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
See Section 6.2.1.5.5 for a discussion of the anchors and target anchor categories.
Meaningful Change Threshold Range of CDASI-A Score
Figure 7 below shows the eCDF of change from baseline to Week 52 in CDASI-A score by
change from baseline in PtGI-S at Week 52 in Study PVT-2201-301. The eCDF plot shows clear
and consistent separation between the target anchor change category of a 1-category
improvement and “no change” curves. The median change score for subjects who reported a 1category improvement in PtGI-S was −12.0. To maintain a misclassification rate of no more than
20% of subjects who reported no change on the anchor but would be classified as having
experienced meaningful change, the corresponding CDASI-A threshold would be −10.0.

Conclusion
To determine how much more likely average subjects receiving treatment over placebo would be
to experience a meaningful improvement in CDASI-A, the review team examined the eCDF plot
of within-subject changes in CDASI-A at Week 52 by treatment arm (Figure 11). Based on
visual inspection of the eCDF, the two treatment curves overlap substantially across the entire
range of change from baseline. In the FDA-determined meaningful change range of a 9- to 13point improvement, there is little to no separation between the brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo
arms. As such, the anchor-based analysis results do not support that subjects receiving
brepocitinib 30 mg experienced a clinically meaningful improvement, as measured by change
from baseline in CDASI-A at Week 52.

6.2.1.5.7. Overall Summary of Efficacy Results, Trial PVT2201-301
Following the multiplicity control procedure prespecified by the Applicant, analyses comparing
brepocitinib 30 mg to placebo provided statistically significant results for the primary endpoint
of TIS, as well as for the following key secondary endpoints:
•

CFB in CDASI-A Score at Week 52

•

DMOMS at Week 52

•

Proportion of Subjects Achieving TIS ≥40 Points at Week 52

•

Time to Consecutive TIS ≥40 Points by Week 52

•

Proportion of Subjects Achieving TIS ≥40 Points at Week 52 With Daily Dose of Oral
Prednisone of 0 to ≤2.5 mg at Both Week 48 and Week 52

Since the testing for the endpoint of “Proportion of Subjects Achieving ≥40% Improvement with
a ≥4-Point Improvement from Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52” in the comparison
between brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo did not provide statistically significant results, the
comparisons between brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo for the subsequent endpoints and all
comparisons between brepocitinib 15 mg and placebo were not formally tested. In general, the
primary and key secondary endpoints result in brepocitinib 15 mg were numerically favorable
compared to placebo.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Missing Data Handling Approach for Efficacy
Analyses
Issue
The Applicant used the LOCF method to handle missing data in the analysis for the continuous
and binary primary and key secondary endpoints, as specified in the SAP v4.0. The
appropriateness of this missing data handling approach is in question.
Background
Missing data handling is a critical methodological consideration in the analysis of clinical trial
efficacy outcomes, as the chosen approach can materially influence treatment effect estimates.
From the review team’s perspective, using LOCF imputation to handle missing data is generally
not appropriate, as the underlying missingness mechanisms are typically not scientifically
justified. LOCF imputation makes the strong assumption that the outcome of a subject does not
change after dropping out {National Research Council Panel on Handling Missing Data in
Clinical, 2010 #30. Even if one could justify the weaker assumption that the true underlying
mean outcome at the timepoint of interest is equal to the true underlying mean outcome at the
time of drop out within a given subject, LOCF is a single imputation approach, which generally
results in underestimation of the uncertainty of the estimated treatment effect due to the failure to
64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
specifically account for the lack of knowledge of the values that are missing. In general, the
Division recommends against the use of single imputation approaches.
In SAP v2.0, the Applicant proposed using a multiple imputation method to handle missing data
for the continuous and binary primary and key secondary endpoints. In SAP v3.0 submitted on
July 24, 2025, and SAP v4.0 submitted on September 4, 2025, the Applicant changed the missing
handling approach to a LOCF method. Because the database lock occurred on September 5,
2025, there was insufficient time for the Division to review and provide recommendations
regarding the final SAP including missing data handling approaches. During the NDA review
cycle, the Agency communicated a “Filing Review Issues Identified” letter to the Applicant on
February 25, 2026. It stated the LOCF method is not appropriate, as the underlying missingness
mechanisms are generally not scientifically justified; the Agency’s primary evaluation of the
efficacy data would utilize multiple imputations to handle missing data and requested the
analyses accordingly.
Assessment
The Applicant submitted updated results using the MI method on March 11, 2026. The review
team based its primary efficacy evaluations on these updated results. We present a side-by-side
comparison between the results obtained using the LOCF method and using the MI method for
handling missing data in Table 25.

Conclusion
The conclusions drawn from the Agency’s main efficacy evaluations are generally consistent
with those drawn from the Applicant’s main efficacy evaluations. However, with the LOCF
imputation method, all the primary and key secondary endpoints were statistically significant
when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. With the MI method used
by the review team to handle missing data, only the first five key secondary endpoints provided
statistically significant results when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo
group. Per the review team’s analysis approach, the testing formally stopped at the endpoint
“Proportion of Participants Achieving ≥40% Improvement with a ≥4-Point Improvement from
Baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52.” The remaining key secondary endpoints were not
formally evaluated via comparisons between the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo
group, via hypothesis tests. Furthermore, the primary and key secondary endpoints were not
formally evaluated via comparisons between the brepocitinib 15 mg group and the placebo
group, via hypothesis tests.

6.3.2. Composite Variable Strategy for the Analysis of
Change From Baseline in CDASI-A Score at
Week 52
Issue
In the analysis of the first key secondary efficacy endpoint, change from baseline in CDASI-A
Score at Week 52, the Applicant’s definition of the estimand via the implementation of a
composite variable strategy for handling the ICEs of receipt of rescue medication and death prior
to Week 52 was data-dependent, in that the 5th percentile of clinical improvement scores was to
be assigned to all subjects who experienced these ICEs. Given the adaptive nature of defining the
estimand, it is unclear whether the resulting analyses have good operating characteristics.

66
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Background
The choice of how to handle such intercurrent events is a critical regulatory and statistical
consideration, as different methods may possibly yield materially different conclusions regarding
the investigational product’s effect based on the endpoint. Given that the Applicant defined the
estimand using a composite variable strategy assigning the observed 5th percentile of clinical
improvement to subjects experiencing these events, the review team assessed whether this
approach was appropriate and whether alternative analyses may lead to different conclusions.
Assessment
The Applicant’s analysis (Table 13) using the observed 5th percentile of clinical improvement for
the composite variable strategy provided statistically significant results when comparing the
brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group, for CFB in CDASI-A Score at Week 52, with LS
mean (SE) CFB scores of −12.1 (0.96) and −7.7 (1.12), for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the
placebo group, respectively. This yielded an LS mean difference (95% CI) of −4.4 (−6.8, −1.9);
p =0.0005. The brepocitinib 15 mg group displayed a smaller numeric decrease when compared
to placebo, with a dose-response relationship observed.
The review team explored the alternative methods listed below to handle the ICEs of receipt of
rescue medication and death as alternative analyses. On April 21, 2026, in response to the
comments by the Agency, the Applicant stated that an 8-point increase in CDASI-A score
represented a significant increase in cutaneous dermatomyositis activity {Dan, 2023 #5}. For the
subjects receiving rescue medication and for the subjects who died, the Week 52 value was
assigned using a random sampling procedure to reflect uncertainty in the subject’s specific level
of utility. The possible values to be assigned were at least the maximum of the baseline value +8
and the Week 52 value. The assigned value was at most the worst possible value of 100. In the
first alternative analysis, a Uniform distribution was used for each subject, with the subjectspecific range of possible values as described above. In a second alternative analysis, the Week
52 value was chosen randomly from a Truncated Normal distribution using the same subjectspecific range of possible values.
Alternative Analysis 1: Review Team Method 1 (Uniform Distribution)
The possible values at Week 52 to be assigned to the subjects who experienced the intercurrent
events of receipt of rescue medications or death were at least the maximum of the baseline value
+8 and the Week 52 value, and at most the worst possible value of 100. Since no subjects died
during the trial, this approach was used for subjects who received rescue medication only. A
Uniform distribution was used for each subject. The change of baseline was then calculated
subtracting the baseline value from the assigned Week 52 value. The use of a subject-specific
Uniform distribution in effect assumed that for the subjects who received rescue medication, any
value in the possible range was equally likely.
For the CFB in CDASI-A score at Week 52, the LS means (SE) were −4.6 (3.2) and 4.6 (3.9), for
the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group, respectively. The comparison between
treatment groups provided statistically persuasive results, with an LS mean difference (95% CI)
of −9.2 (−17.8, −0.7) when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. The
comparison of the brepocitinib 15 mg group to the placebo group provided a smaller numeric
difference, with a dose-response relationship observed. For each brepocitinib group, the amount
67
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
of clinical improvement when compared to placebo was lower compared to the analysis results
obtained using the Applicant’s method. Furthermore, the placebo group results reflect clinical
deterioration for this group. Overall, the conclusions based on the results for this alternative
analysis were consistent with those based on the Applicant’s analysis.

Alternative Analysis 2: Review Team Method 2 (Truncated Normal Distribution)
The maximum observed value at Week 52 was 31, with a standard deviation of 7.6 across all
observed Week 52 values. The review team used these two summary statistics to parameterize a
truncated normal distribution for imputation, specifying a mean of 31 and a standard deviation of
7.6. For each subject, the lower truncation bound was set to the greater of the baseline value +8
or the observed Week 52 value, and the upper truncation bound was fixed at 100. This approach
reflected the assumption that imputed Week 52 values are most likely to cluster near the
observed data, with diminishing probability toward the distributional extremes, whether near the
ceiling of 100 or near the lower bound.
For the CFB in CDASI-A score at Week 52, the LS means (SE) were −8.9 (1.6) and −3.2 (1.9),
for the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group, respectively. The comparison between
treatment groups provided statistically persuasive results, with an LS mean difference (95% CI)
of −5.7 (−9.9, −1.4) when comparing the brepocitinib 30 mg group to the placebo group. The
comparison of the brepocitinib 15 mg group to the placebo group provided a smaller numeric
difference, with a dose-response relationship observed. The results obtained with this method
appear closer to the results obtained using the Applicant’s main analysis method.

The conclusions regarding the evidence of an effect were consistent across both methods and the
Applicant’s primary analysis method. In each case, the 95% CI for the comparison between the
68
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo group did not cover the null value of 0, while the 95%
CI for the comparison between the brepocitinib 15 mg group and the placebo group covered the
null value of 0.
For each of the three approaches (the Applicant’s analysis and both of the review team’s
alternative analyses) the comparison between the brepocitinib 30 mg group and the placebo
group provided statistically persuasive results. This consistency across intercurrent eventhandling methods provides increased confidence of an effect for brepocitinib 30 mg compared to
the placebo with respect to this endpoint.
Conclusion
The comparisons between brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo provided statistically persuasive
results across the different analyses for change from baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52.
Therefore, the review team concludes that there is evidence of an effect with the use of
brepocitinib 30 mg on the change from baseline in CDASI-A Score at Week 52, when compared
to placebo.

6.4. Division of Dermatology and Dentistry
Clinical Review
Overview of Cutaneous Manifestations in Dermatomyositis
Dermatomyositis (DM) is an immune-mediated inflammatory myopathy characterized by both
muscle inflammation and a constellation of distinctive cutaneous findings. The pathognomonic
skin manifestations include the heliotrope rash, Gottron's papules, Gottron's sign, periungual
telangiectasias, mechanic's hands, scalp erythema, non-scarring alopecia, and the shawl sign or
V-sign (erythematous and papulosquamous eruptions of the upper chest and posterior neck and
back (UpToDate 2025)). Photosensitivity is common, and pruritus is a prevalent but often underrecognized symptom that can significantly impair sleep and daily functioning (Hundley et al.
2006). Skin manifestations contribute substantially to disease burden and quality-of-life
impairment, and in a subset of patients may predominate in the relative absence of significant
muscle disease (clinically amyopathic dermatomyositis (Concha et al. 2020). As noted in
Section 3, DM carries significant systemic morbidity, and cutaneous disease represents an
important domain of patient impact.
The CDASI Instrument and the CDASI-Activity Subscore
The Cutaneous Dermatomyositis Disease Area and Severity Index (CDASI) is a clinicianreported outcome (ClinRO) measure designed to standardize the assessment of the cutaneous
manifestations of DM. As described in Section 6.2.1.1 (Clinical Outcome Assessments
Descriptions), the CDASI instrument evaluates erythema, scale, and erosion/ulceration across 15
anatomical locations, along with Gottron's papules on the hands, periungual changes, and
alopecia; it yields separate Activity (CDASI-A) and Damage (CDASI-D) subscores, with the
CDASI-A ranging from 0 to 100. Despite increasing interest in the CDASI-A as a clinical trial
endpoint, no consensus exists on thresholds for disease severity or clinically meaningful change.
Published cut-offs for disease severity vary (e.g. 14 points [University of Pennsylvania] to 19
points [Stanford University] for “mild” disease), and proposed thresholds for minimal clinically
69
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
meaningful change are not established, ranging from 4 to 5 points in some literature (Anyanwu et
al. 2015).
The inclusion criteria for Study PVT-2201-301 as pertains to cutaneous disease required a
CDASI-A score of ≥ 6 at screening and randomization. This threshold is low relative to
published cut-offs for disease severity, suggesting that participants may have had minimal
cutaneous disease activity at baseline. Additionally, the Applicant proposed an improvement
range of 6-10 points as “perceptible reduction” in cutaneous disease activity, based on
triangulation of results of their anchor-based analyses using the Patient Global Impression of
Severity (PtGI-S), Physician Global Impression of Severity (PhGI-S), Patient Global Impression
of Change (PtGI-C), and Physician Global Impression of Change (PhGI-C) as anchors. As
discussion in Section 6.2.1.5.6 (Meaningful Change Threshold Range of CDASI-A Score), the
FDA identified several limitations with the Applicant's anchor-based analyses and suggest a
clinically meaningful threshold range of 9 to 13 points of improvement based on internal anchorbased analyses.
Statistical Results and Clinical Meaningfulness of the CDASI-A
The effect of brepocitinib on cutaneous manifestations of dermatomyositis was evaluated via
secondary endpoints in Study PVT-2201-301. Refer to Section 6.2.1.1 for a detailed summary of
the trial design. The comparison between the brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo groups was
statistically significant for change from baseline (CFB) in CDASI-A score at Week 52, the first
key secondary endpoint in the trial. The Least Squares (LS) mean (Standard Error [SE]) CFB
scores were -12.1 (0.96) and -7.7 (1.12) for the brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo groups,
respectively, yielding an LS mean difference (95% CI) of -4.4 (-6.8, -1.9); p = 0.0005. The
brepocitinib 15 mg group showed a smaller numeric decrease when compared to placebo (LS
mean difference [95% CI] of –1.9 [-4.7, 0.9]), with a dose-response relationship observed. This
statistical result was robust across alternative methods evaluated by the statistical review team, as
detailed in Section 6.3.2.
However, this review team shares significant concerns regarding whether the observed
improvement in CDASI-A represents a clinically meaningful change from the patient
perspective. At the FDA-determined meaningful change range of 9- to 13-point improvement,
there is little to no separation between the brepocitinib 30 mg and placebo treatment arms on
visual inspection of the empirical Cumulative Distribution Function (eCDF) curve (refer to
Figure 11). This result calls into question whether participants receiving brepocitinib 30 mg are
meaningfully more likely to achieve a perceptible improvement in their skin manifestations than
participants receiving placebo, despite the statistically significant mean difference.
Given the discrepancy between the statistical significance of the CDASI-A result and the data
supporting clinical meaningfulness, an Information Request (IR) was sent to the Applicant on
June 4, 2026 requesting the sum scores for erythema, scale, and erosion/ulceration across all
anatomic locations, as well as accompanying descriptive statistics for this data at baseline, at
Week 52, and for change from baseline at Week 52 by treatment arm. A follow-up IR was issued
on June 8, 2026, requesting the same data for the Gottron's papules - Hands, Periungual changes,
and Alopecia components of the CDASI-A, as well as a rationale for the lack of separation
observed between instrument components.

70

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
In response to the FDA IR, the Applicant provided their post-hoc analyses of the results, which
are summarized below. The LS mean difference in CFB in the erythema subscore at Week 52
was -2.7 points (brepocitinib 30 mg vs. placebo; 95% CI: -4.57, -0.77). Treatment differences
across the remaining subscores were notably smaller at Week 52:
1.
2.
3.
4.
5.

Scale: LS mean difference -1.0 point (95% CI: -1.70, -0.34)
Erosion/Ulceration: LS mean difference -0.1 point (95% CI: -0.25, 0.03)
Gottron's Papules - Hands: LS mean difference -0.5 point (95% CI: -0.83, -0.25)
Periungual: LS mean difference -0.2 point (95% CI: -0.39, -0.06)
Alopecia: LS mean difference approximately 0.0 points (95% CI: -0.15, 0.07)

These findings indicate that the improvement in the overall CDASI-A score at Week 52 was
likely primarily driven by improvement in erythema. While reduction in erythema may be a
clinically relevant finding, the apparent selectivity of the treatment effect for this single CDASIA component raises important questions about the breadth of brepocitinib's effect across the full
spectrum of the cutaneous manifestations intended to be captured by the instrument. This
observation is particularly consequential given the previously noted discrepancies in establishing
a clinically meaningful threshold, and uncertainty as to whether the observed changes were
meaningful to patients.
Additional Cutaneous Endpoints (CDA-IGA and PP-NRS)
Both the Cutaneous Dermatomyositis Activity Physician's Global Assessment (CDA-IGA) and
the Peak Pruritus–Numerical Rating Scale (PP-NRS) were exploratory endpoints in Study PVT2201-301. As exploratory endpoints, they were not part of the formal hypothesis-testing
hierarchy and were not controlled for multiplicity, nor were they fit-for-purpose in this context.
We additionally note a measurement limitation of the PP-NRS as administered in Study PVT2201-301: the instrument captures peak itch intensity at a single time point, rather than averaging
patient-reported itch severity over multiple time points in a week. This may not adequately
capture the fluctuating nature of pruritus over time and could introduce bias. While descriptive
results suggest improvement favoring brepocitinib 30 mg over placebo for these additional
cutaneous endpoints, formal conclusions about efficacy based on these results cannot be drawn.
Conclusions
Although brepocitinib 30 mg demonstrated a statistically significant improvement in CDASI-A
score at Week 52, there remains uncertainty as to whether these results represent a clinically
meaningful improvement in cutaneous disease activity. Further evaluation of individual subscore
data suggests that the statistically significant CDASI-A result is driven by improvement in
erythema, with the remaining subscores appearing either not statistically separable or negligible
in magnitude. Exploratory analyses of the CDA-IGA and PP-NRS endpoints suggest
improvement favoring brepocitinib 30 mg; however, given their exploratory nature and lack of
multiplicity control, these endpoints are not adequate to support formal efficacy conclusions.
(b) (4)

. However, in the
context of an already established efficacy on the systemic manifestations of dermatomyositis,
qualitative description of the effects of brepocitinib on the cutaneous manifestations (driven
primarily by reductions in erythema), is reasonable and supported by the available data. We
propose the following descriptive language for inclusion in the label: “Treatment with LISRAYA

6.5. Approach to Evaluation of Clinical Outcome
Assessment
The Applicant administered several COAs in Study/Trial PVT-2201-301, of which the FDA
review team evaluated the TIS-based endpoint for its adequacy to support a demonstration of
clinical benefit.
To support the proposed labeling claims, the Applicant submitted an evidence dossier as part of
the NDA submission. The TIS was reviewed for content validity, other measurement properties
(i.e., inter-rater, inter-rater reliability, etc.), and score interpretability (i.e., what constitutes
clinically meaningful change scores on the COA-based endpoint).

6.5.1. Total Improvement Score
6.5.1.1. Content Validity
The Applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content
validity of the TIS:
•

Literature review

•

Patient input (focus groups, individual interviews, online survey, patient listening session,
and externally led patient-focused drug development meeting)

A high-level summary of the results for each activity is described below. For a comprehensive
evaluation of the content validity of the TIS, refer to the full COA review.
6.5.1.1.1. Qualitative Evidence
The literature review identified patient-reported symptoms and impacts relevant to the TIS CSMs
and informed subsequent qualitative interview development; however, the Applicant provided
insufficient methodological detail to assess how concepts were identified and prioritized.
The Applicant conducted two qualitative studies with adult DM subjects:
•

Qualitative Study #1 (2021 to 2022): Involved 14 subjects across two focus groups and five
cognitive debriefing interviews. No final report was provided; results were extracted from a
subsequent online survey report. Key findings identified the following as the most
bothersome symptoms, with muscle weakness having the greatest impact on daily life:
–
–
–
–
–

Muscle weakness
Shortness of breath
Muscle pain
Fatigue
Skin symptoms

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
•

Qualitative Study #2 (2024): Consisted of 18 1-hour telephone interviews designed to assess
the:
– Relevance and directionality of the TIS CSMs
– Appropriateness of CSM weightings
– Comprehensibility of the HAQ-DI and PtGA-VAS patient-reported measures
Findings from Qualitative Study #2 confirmed that all TIS CSMs were relevant to subjects’
DM experience:
– Muscle weakness: All subjects reported experience of muscle weakness; 72% (n=13/18)
rated it as having a “large” or “very large” role in their disease experience, and 94%
(n=17/18) considered it “very important” to measure for assessing improvement.
– Non-muscle symptoms: All subjects reported experience of skin symptoms and fatigue,
which were considered important but secondary to muscle weakness.
– Muscle enzymes: Most subjects found this measure relevant, though two subjects
(n=2/18, 11%) noted their CK levels did not correlate with muscle weakness, limiting its
perceived importance.
– Disease activity: All subjects supported inclusion of both physician-reported (PhGA) and
patient-reported (PtGA) disease activity measures. Muscle weakness and/or skin activity
(n=13/18, 72%) were most frequently reported for assessing disease activity, followed by
ability to perform daily activities, fatigue, and pain. Most subjects (n=13/18, 72%) rated
the PtGA as “very important,” citing patients as best positioned to characterize their own
disease experience.
– Physical functioning and daily activities: All subjects agreed these were critically
important to capture and universally endorsed inclusion of a patient-reported measure
assessing this domain.
Subjects broadly endorsed a linear relationship among the CSMs, with the following notable
findings:
– Improvement in muscle weakness was consistently associated with improvement in
physical functioning (n=18/18, 100%) and muscle enzymes (n=16/18, 89%).
– The relationship between muscle weakness and non-muscle symptoms was less
consistent — approximately 28% of subjects (n=5/18) reported skin symptoms remained
unchanged with muscle improvement, and one subject (n=1/18, 6%) reported skin
worsening.
– Subjects defined overall DM improvement primarily in terms of:
▪
▪
▪

Reduced muscle weakness and pain
Improved skin symptoms
Enhanced ability to perform daily activities without excessive fatigue

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
There was subject disagreement regarding CSM weighting within the TIS:
– Most subjects agreed with the weightings for muscle weakness (n=14/18, 78%) and nonmuscle symptoms (n=14/18, 78%).
– Approximately 61% (n=11/18) felt the HAQ-DI and 50% (n=9/18) felt the PtGA-VAS
should be weighted more heavily than their current 10% contributions.
– 39% (n=7/18) of subjects disagreed with the PhGA weighting of 20%, with some
expressing concern that physician assessments reflect only a limited snapshot of the
patient experience.
Both patient-reported CSMs — the HAQ-DI and PtGA-VAS — were generally found to be
clear, relevant, and easy to use by subjects. HAQ-DI instructions, response options, and the oneweek recall period were rated as clear and acceptable, with the majority of subjects finding the
items relevant to their DM experience. The most relevant HAQ domains were Activities,
Dressing and Grooming, and Walking, while Grip was the least relevant. Most subjects found the
PtGA-VAS clear and easy to understand, generally considering muscle weakness, skin
symptoms, and associated pain when responding. It should be noted that the Applicant did not
provide frequency descriptive statistics for key comprehensibility variables for either instrument.
6.5.1.1.2. Mixed Methods Evidence
The Applicant conducted an online survey with 195 adults (aged 18 to 75 years) with DM to
further understand symptoms and impacts of DM. The survey covered two main areas:
demographic questions and dermatomyositis-focused questions. The DM-focused questions were
largely developed or derived from established PRO measures, addressing:
•

Symptoms and severity, informed by a targeted literature review and qualitative patient
interviews5

•

Sexual desire, adapted from the Female Sexual Function Index

•

Intimacy impact, adapted from the SLE-FAMILY questionnaire

•

Mental health, adapted from the PROMIS Anxiety and Depression Short Forms

•

Career impact, adapted from the WPAI-GH

•

Institutional/organizational support

•

Treatment preference

•

General impact on daily functioning

The majority of respondents reported mild-to-moderate DM severity, with concurrent muscle and
skin symptoms. The most frequently cited bothersome symptoms were muscle weakness, fatigue,
and muscle pain, which support prior qualitative research findings. Notably, symptom severity
did not consistently correlate with perceived bother, indicating that lower-severity symptoms
may still be perceived as very bothersome by the respondent. Respondents reported meaningful
limitations in activities of daily living and physical functioning (e.g., stair climbing, independent

ambulation), as well as negative impacts on social functioning, interpersonal and intimate
relationships, mental health (i.e., anxiety, depression, and worry), and occupational productivity.
A limitation of the survey was it did not capture if subjects were experiencing a DM flare, which
could have impacted the respondents’ responses. Generalizability of findings is limited by the
convenience sampling methodology and the underrepresentation of African American
respondents (9%), a population in which DM is known to be disproportionately prevalent,
particularly among women.
6.5.1.1.3. Other Evidence
The Applicant referenced a Patient Listening Session and an externally-led patient-focused drug
development (PFDD) Meeting to supplement their evidence generation activities. A brief
summary of these meetings is provided below:
•

On April 26, 2022, Myositis Support & Understanding organized a patient-led listening
session to convey to FDA the disease burden of DM. Seven patients and one caregiver
provided testimony spanning refractory disease requiring palliative care, delayed or incorrect
diagnoses, loss of employment and independence, severe chronic pain, and significant mental
health impacts.
Key clinical and regulatory themes from the session underscored the severity of DM,
including a 53% 10-year survival rate, with 62 to 67% of patients failing to achieve remission
on current standard of care and 73% experiencing disease flares despite therapy. Patients
described the substantial physical burden of muscle weakness, fatigue, pain, and skin
manifestations, as well as the emotional toll on patients and their families. The heterogeneity
of DM — reflected in diverse antibody profiles (MDA-5, NXP-2, Jo-1, Mi-2) with distinct
systemic manifestations — was highlighted as a critical consideration for treatment
development and clinical trial design.

•

On June 7, 2024, Myositis Support & Understanding and The Myositis Association coorganized an Externally-Led PFDD meeting for adult DM. The meeting featured testimonies
from ten patients representing varied disease forms and severities, moderated virtual panel
discussions, and real-time audience polling. A pre-meeting survey of 200 DM patients and
caregivers supplemented live discussions with quantitative and qualitative data on symptom
burden, treatment challenges, and therapy preferences.
Findings from the PFDD meeting reinforced and expanded upon the themes raised in the
2022 listening session. Patients reported relentless fatigue, muscle weakness, skin-related
pain, and pulmonary complications as primary burdens, accompanied by anxiety, depression,
and social isolation. Despite use of corticosteroids, immunosuppressants, and IVIG, many
patients reported suboptimal symptom control and significant side effects. Unmet needs
centered on disease-modifying therapies that address underlying disease mechanisms, with
patient priorities including personalized treatment approaches, simplified administration, and
reduced long-term toxicity.
