LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
6.5.1.2. Other Measurement Properties
Due to the complex scoring algorithm, the results of any psychometric analyses on the TIS are
difficult to interpret. Refer to Section 6.5.1.3, which describes limitations with interpretation of
the TIS.
6.5.1.3. Score Interpretability
As described in Section 6.2.1.1, the TIS scoring algorithm is complex, creating categorizations of
changes (i.e., “levels of improvement”) in the continuous percent change from baseline scores of
each CSM and differentially weighting those categories of changes both within and across
CSMs. Multi-component endpoints, such as the TIS, that bundle and differentially weight
multiple concepts are challenging to interpret because an improvement in the score could reflect
completely different combinations of improvements in different subjects. The Applicant
submitted limited evidence to support the appropriateness of the algorithm from the perspective
of patients. As discussed in Section 6.5.1.1.1, the Applicant conducted qualitative interviews
(n=18) to determine if the weights contributing to the TIS were understandable to patients. The
qualitative research findings indicated there was general agreement on the relative weighting of
the muscle weakness and non-muscle symptom CSMs; while subjects suggested the two PRO
measures (i.e., HAQ-DI and PtGA-VAS) should be weighed more and the physician-reported
PhGA-VAS should be weighed less or at least equally with the PROs. However, evidence to
support the appropriateness of the relative weighting of improvements within CSMs from the
perspectives of patients was not provided. For example, for the HAQ-DI an improvement
between >15% and 25% is weighted the same as improvement between >25% and 40%;
whereas the latter improvement category is given 50% more weight than the former for the
muscle enzyme and PtGA-VAS, 20% more for the EMGA-VAS, and 16.7% more weight for the
PhGA-VAS.
Additionally, evidence to support the appropriateness of the boundaries that define the levels of
improvement for the CSM change scores from the patient perspective was not provided (e.g., the
largest category of improvement for MMT-8 is >30% improvement, while the largest category
for the other 5 CSMs is >40% improvement). Finally, no penalty is applied for worsening on a
CSM as the lowest level of improvement on all CSMs except for MMT-8 is “Worsening to 5%
improvement” (“worsening to 2% improvement for MMT-8), which is given a weight of 0 across
all CSMs.
To aid in the interpretation of the complex TIS in the absence of additional evidence mentioned
above, the Agency explored the relationships between subjects’ CSM scores as well as subjects’
change from baseline in CSM scores at Week 52. In concept elicitation interviews, patients
reported a linear relationship across most CSMs (i.e., when one CSM improved, other CSMs also
improved). Correlation analyses among changes in CSM scores indicate a moderate relationship
among all non-muscle enzyme CSMs (see Table 28). Subject’s raw change from baseline to
Week 52 in CSM scores showed improvement from baseline to Week 52 for all non-muscle
enzyme CSMs (see Section 6.2.1.5.4). Taken together, these qualitative and quantitative data
suggest consistent and correlated improvements across individual CSMs in Study PVT-2201-301
and provide reassurance that the observed TIS may represent a meaningful composite of patient
improvement in DM despite the limitations in the interpretability of the TIS due to the complex
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
scoring algorithm. However, further research and evidence are needed to address the noted
limitations with the scoring algorithm of the TIS.

6.5.1.4. Conclusions
•

The COA-related components of the TIS measure clinically relevant and important aspects of
DM from the patient perspective. The qualitative findings generally support the components
of the TIS.

•

The COA-related components of the TIS are reasonably understood and interpreted
appropriately across respondents based on the submitted qualitative evidence.

•

The interpretability of the TIS may be limited by the weighting of each TIS component and
thresholds defining levels of improvement, which have not been adequately justified.

•

At this time, the COA review team cannot make a definitive conclusion on the fitness-forpurpose of the TIS for this specified context of use in the absence of qualitative and
quantitative evidence to support the scoring methodology – specifically, as it relates to the
levels of improvement and the corresponding improvement score, including the weighting of
the components. For recommendations for use of the TIS in future DM clinical trials, refer to
the full COA review.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
The nonclinical safety profile of brepocitinib was evaluated in support of this NDA through a
comprehensive nonclinical program, including safety pharmacology studies, repeat-dose toxicity
studies in rats and monkeys, reproductive and developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits,
and carcinogenicity studies in rats and transgenic mice. Safety pharmacology studies identified
potential effects in the cardiovascular system (potential QTc prolongation in monkeys) and
central nervous system (decreased activity). However, no effects were observed in the repeatdose toxicity studies in rats or monkeys. The effects identified in repeat-dose toxicology studies
are consistent with exaggerated primary pharmacology. Findings were observed in blood
(including reduced lymphocytes), lymphoid tissue (decreased cellularity in spleen, thymus,
lymph nodes, and sternal bone marrow), and lungs (increased alveolar foamy macrophages, type
II pneumocyte hyperplasia, and alveolar edema). Additionally, skeletal malformations were
observed in the reproductive and developmental toxicity studies. Similar findings of reduced
lymphocytes and decreased cellularity in lymphoid tissue, as well as embryo-fetal toxicity
including skeletal malformations, have been observed with other JAK inhibitors. The potential
risks based on the nonclinical findings are summarized below (refer to (DARRTS ID: 5839260)
for additional details).
Cardiovascular System
Brepocitinib inhibited human ether-à-go-go-related gene currents in vitro with an IC50 of
33.4 μM (equivalent to 18,760 ng/mL). In male monkeys, QTc prolongation was observed at a
dose of 30 mg/kg. This is likely a consequence of the apparent treatment-related decrease in
heart rate rather than blockade of cardiac potassium channel currents. Potential cardiovascular
effects were monitored in human clinical trials.
Central Nervous System
In a respiratory and Central Nervous System safety pharmacology study in male rats, lower mean
locomotor activity was noted at doses ≥20 mg/kg/day. Notably, no clinical signs such as lethargy
or decreased activity were observed in any of the rat toxicology studies, which tested PF06700841 doses up to 45 mg/kg/day for up to 6 months.
Blood
A decrease in leukocytes, red blood cells, reticulocytes, hemoglobin, and hematocrit was
observed in the 6-month rat study. In the 9-month monkey study, decreased total lymphocytes (T
cells, T helper cells, and cytotoxic T cells), natural killer cells, and eosinophils, as well as
decreased IgM and IgG responses to Keyhole Limpet Hemocyanin, were observed.

78
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Lymphoid Tissue
Decreased lymphoid cellularity of the thymus, spleen, lymph nodes, and sternal bone marrow
was observed across the toxicology studies, which correlated with decreased spleen and thymus
weights.
Lungs
In the 9-month monkey study, increased alveolar foamy macrophages, type II pneumocyte
hyperplasia, and alveolar edema were observed, correlating with macroscopic findings of
multifocal, pale, tan foci and suggestive of phospholipidosis.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
In male rats, brepocitinib had no effect on male reproductive performance (mating, fertility, or
copulation indices, or pre-coital interval) at any dose up to 55 mg/kg/day. In female rats at
55 mg/kg/day, reduced fecundity, fewer corpora lutea, fewer implantation sites, and increased
pre-implantation loss were observed. Post-implantation loss was observed at ≥10 mg/kg/day. In
an embryo-fetal development study in pregnant rats, fetal skeletal malformations were observed
at 2.0 mg/kg/day. Skeletal malformations induced by brepocitinib included bent long bones
(humerus, femur, radius, ulna, fibula, and tibia) and bent scapula bodies. Additional
malformations included split sternebrae, a bent scapula, and a cleft palate. Skeletal variations
included misshapen cervical arches, nodulated ribs, delayed skeletal ossification, and structural
rib and sternebrae changes. In a rabbit embryo-fetal development study, post-implantation loss
with fewer viable fetuses was observed at 7 mg/kg/day, and skeletal malformations were
observed at ≥3 mg/kg/day. Skeletal variations included unossified forepaw phalanges, misshapen
sternebrae, unossified hindpaw phalanges, unossified talus, misshapen scapulae, and abnormal
cervical centra ossification. In a pre- and post-natal developmental toxicity study in rats, no
adverse brepocitinib-related effects were observed in maternal rats or offspring at doses up to 5.5

79
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
times the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) on area under the concentration-time
curve (AUC) basis.
Carcinogenicity
No brepocitinib-related neoplastic findings were observed in the 6-month carcinogenicity study
in hemizygous rasH2 transgenic mice. In a 2-year carcinogenicity study, Leydig cell tumors were
observed in male rats at 30 mg/kg/day. The relevance of this finding to human subjects is
unknown.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Several FDA-approved JAK inhibitors for rheumatologic indications (i.e., tofacitinib,
upadacitinib, baricitinib) may inform the potential risks of brepocitinib. These JAK inhibitors
share the same Box Warnings and Warnings and Precautions within their respective United
States Prescribing Information (USPI) labels. The Box Warnings include: 1) increased risk of
serious bacterial, fungal, and opportunistic infections, including tuberculosis, leading to
hospitalization or death, 2) higher rate of all-cause mortality, including sudden cardiovascular
death with [JAK inhibitor] versus tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-inhibitor in rheumatoid arthritis
(RA) patients, 3) malignancies have occurred and higher rates of lymphoma and lung cancer
with [JAK inhibitor] versus TNF-inhibitors in RA patients, 4) higher rate of major adverse
cardiovascular events (defined as cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, and stroke) with
[JAK-inhibitor] versus TNF-inhibitor in RA patients, and 5) thrombosis has occurred in patients
treated with [JAK-inhibitor]. Increased incidence of pulmonary embolism, venous and arterial
thrombosis with [JAK inhibitor] versus TNF-inhibitors in RA patients. Labeled Warnings and
Precautions include: 1) serious infections, 2) gastrointestinal perforation, 3) laboratory
monitoring for potential changes to lymphocytes, neutrophils, hemoglobin, liver enzymes, and
lipids, 4) embryo-fetal toxicity (upadacitinib only), and 5) avoid use of live vaccines.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. Brepocitinib is a New Molecular Entity and is not approved in any country or
region; therefore, no postmarketing adverse event experience is available. Section 7.2 contains
safety information derived from related drug products.

Experiences

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database is adequate for a safety assessment of brepocitinib for the proposed dosage
regimen and duration. According to the ICH guidance for industry E1A The Extent of Population
Exposure to Assess Clinical Safety: For Drugs Intended for Long-term Treatment of Non-LifeThreatening Conditions (March 1995), in conjunction with the FDA guidance for industry
Premarketing Risk Assessment (March 2005), a minimum of 1500 subjects should be exposed to
the investigational drug, with at least 300 to 600 subjects treated for 6 months and at least 100
subjects treated for 1 year. Exposure to brepocitinib across all phase 1, 2, and 3 studies meets
these requirements. The NDA safety database comprises the pivotal dermatomyositis trial (PVT2201-301) and all phase 2 and phase 3 studies conducted across indications, as presented in
Safety Pool S3. At doses relevant to this application (≥30 mg daily), 742 subjects were exposed
to brepocitinib for at least 6 months, and 471 subjects were exposed for at least 1 year (Table
32).
In the pivotal trial PVT-2201-301, 71 subjects with dermatomyositis were exposed to
brepocitinib 30 mg for 1 year (Table 30). In addition, 185 subjects were enrolled into the OpenLabel Extension period of trial PVT-2201-301, with a mean exposure of approximately 39 weeks
across all treatment sequences. To supplement the dermatomyositis-specific exposure data and
provide additional safety characterization, Safety Pool S1 integrates data from Trial PVT-2201301 in DM and Study B7931028 in SLE. At doses relevant to this application (≥30 mg daily),
219 subjects were exposed to brepocitinib for 1 year in Pool S1 (Table 31). Both studies in Pool
S1 included 52 weeks of blinded treatment with brepocitinib 30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg, or
placebo; Study B7931028 additionally included a brepocitinib 45-mg dose arm. Integration of
DM and SLE data is reasonable for characterizing the brepocitinib safety profile given the shared
clinical features, standard background therapies, and risks between these conditions. Both are
chronic, systemic autoimmune connective tissue diseases characterized by Type I interferon
signaling dysregulation (Hile and Werth 2025) and relapsing-remitting courses, with similar
demographic patterns disproportionately affecting women and certain racial/ethnic minorities.
Clinical overlap includes photosensitive dermatologic manifestations, inflammatory skin lesions
with comparable histopathology (Magro et al. 2010), and Raynaud’s. Both populations face
comparable risks of serious disease- and treatment-related complications, including increased allcause mortality, serious infections, cardiovascular events, opportunistic infections, and viral
reactivations (Alarcón 2006; Robinson et al. 2016; Hsu et al. 2019; Kronzer et al. 2023; Lee et
al. 2023).
In summary, the safety database is adequate for safety assessment of brepocitinib for the
proposed indication and patient population, with exposure exceeding ICH E1A (March 1995)
and FDA guidance (March 2005) thresholds and supplemented by clinically relevant data from

the phase 2 study in SLE, a disease sharing key pathophysiologic, demographic, and safety risk
characteristics with dermatomyositis.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Results, Trial PVT-2201-301
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Trial PVT-2201-301
The demonstrated safety profile of brepocitinib administered at 30 mg by mouth daily is
acceptable for the population of adults with dermatomyositis. There were no deaths in Trial
PVT-2201-301 during the double-blind treatment period. The incidence of any adverse event
was similar across treatment arms; however, the incidence of SAEs was higher in the
brepocitinib 30 mg arm (16%) compared to the placebo arm (12.7%) and the brepocitinib 15 mg
arm (8.6%). Discontinuation of treatment due to an AE was higher in the placebo arm (11.4%)
compared to brepocitinib 15 mg (7.4%) and brepocitinib 30 mg (6.2%).
Based on data from the 90 DSU, brepocitinib 30 mg was generally well tolerated in the OLE
Period, with 71.9% of subjects experiencing at least one TEAE; the majority (93.3%) were mild
or moderate in severity, and rates were similar across all treatment sequences with no consistent
exposure-related trends. Four deaths occurred in the OLE Period (cardiogenic shock,
dermatomyositis, respiratory failure, and myocardial rupture), all assessed as not related to
brepocitinib by the Applicant (see Section 7.6.1.2). SAEs occurred in 11.4% of subjects overall
and at similar rates across treatment sequences, with Infections and Infestations being the most
common System Organ Class (SOC), accounting for 13 of 34 total SAEs in 12 subjects (6.5%).
TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation were infrequent (5.4% overall) and similar across
treatment sequences, with Cardiac Disorders and Respiratory, Thoracic, and Mediastinal
Disorders being the only SOCs in which more than one subject discontinued.

Of note, a clinical site inspection identified four previously unreported adverse events in subjects
receiving brepocitinib (one subject on 30 mg with a mild fungal skin infection, and three subjects
on 15 mg with moderate constipation, acute bronchitis, and mild radiculopathy, respectively);
these events were reviewed and determined to be either expected based on the known safety
profile of the JAK inhibitor class, or non-significant, with no impact on the overall safety
conclusions. Refer to Section 10 for further details of the clinical site inspection.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial PVT-2201-301
There were no deaths in the double-blind treatment period or the open-label treatment period of
Trial PVT-2201-301 through the data cut for the application (March 1, 2025). After the data cut,
four deaths occurred in the ongoing open-label treatment period, which are summarized below.
1. A fatal case of cardiogenic shock was reported in a 68-year-old subject assigned to the
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg sequence. This subject presented at baseline with multiple
cardiovascular risk factors and an abnormal electrocardiogram (ECG). During the OLE
Period, the subject received a diagnosis of coronary artery disease requiring urgent CABG.
Fatal cardiogenic shock occurred on Day 617 (Day 250 of the OLE Period) as a
postoperative complication of the CABG procedure. Notably, brepocitinib had been
discontinued more than 2 months prior to this event, on Day 550 (Day 183 of the OLE
Period), due to varicella zoster pneumonia and associated complications, after which the
subject received multiple administrations of intravenous immunoglobulin. The Applicant
assessed the event as not related to brepocitinib, while the Investigator assessed it as possibly
related; the event met adjudication criteria for cardiovascular death.
2. A fatal case of dermatomyositis was reported in a 52-year-old subject assigned to the
placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg sequence. This subject had MDA-5 positive DM and ILD
diagnosed approximately 3 months prior to randomization, with associated severe restriction
(forced vital capacity [FVC] of 42% at screening), as well as chronic bronchitis,
lymphopenia, and diabetes. On Day 396 (Day 27 of the OLE Period), the subject experienced
a dermatomyositis flare following a TEAE of herpes ophthalmicus, which had improved
from moderate to mild severity, that had led to a temporary discontinuation of brepocitinib.
The dermatomyositis flare was accompanied by worsening ILD in the absence of evidence of
infection, and the subject subsequently died under palliative care. Both the Investigator and
the Applicant assessed the event as not related to brepocitinib, and the event met adjudication
criteria for death.
3. A fatal case of respiratory failure was reported in a 42-year-old subject assigned to the
placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg sequence. This subject had DM and ILD with moderate
restriction (FVC of 50% at screening) associated with PL-7 and Ro52 autoantibodies, a
history of prior cyclophosphamide treatment, and obesity. Over the course of 52 weeks of
placebo treatment during the Blinded Treatment Period, the subject experienced worsening
lung function, a new diagnosis of pulmonary hypertension (estimated pulmonary artery
systolic pressure of 61 mm Hg), and a developing requirement for continuous supplemental
oxygen. On Day 463 (Day 99 of the OLE Period), following an adverse event of herpes
zoster that subsequently resolved and discontinuation of brepocitinib (reason not specified),
the subject was hospitalized for worsening respiratory symptoms and later died under
palliative care. Infectious workup was negative. Both the Investigator and the Applicant
assessed the event as not related to brepocitinib, and the event met adjudication criteria for
death.
4. A fatal case of myocardial rupture was reported in a 58-year-old subject assigned to the
brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequence. Prior to study enrollment, this subject had a
documented history of mitral and tricuspid regurgitation and mitral valve calcification, along
with additional comorbidities including lung fibrosis, pulmonary hypertension, calcinosis
cutis, chondrocalcinosis, and diabetes. Progressive dyspnea on exertion was observed
86
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
approximately 6 months into study participation. Transesophageal echocardiogram
performed on Day 607 revealed severe mitral valve calcification with a large calcific nodule
and chordal rupture; severe mitral valve regurgitation had been worsening over the preceding
two years. On Day 624, the subject underwent planned double valve replacement (mitral and
aortic) for severe mitral valve regurgitation and calcification. Postoperatively, the subject
was admitted to the intensive care unit, where bradycardia was identified and an external
pacemaker was placed. On Day 625, the subject experienced sudden hemodynamic collapse
and asystole unresponsive to ventricular pacing. Emergency thoracotomy was performed;
however, the subject died secondary to atrioventricular groove rupture. Both the Investigator
and the Applicant assessed the event as not related to brepocitinib, and the event met
adjudication criteria for cardiovascular death.
In conclusion, no deaths occurred during the blinded treatment period or the OLE period of Trial
PVT-2201-301 through March 1, 2025, data cut for this application. Four deaths were reported
after the data cut in the ongoing OLE period; in each case, the available clinical information,
including the presence of significant pre-existing comorbidities, the temporal relationship
between brepocitinib exposure and the fatal event, and the results of adjudication, supports the
assessment that the deaths were likely not related to brepocitinib.
7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
PVT-2201-301
There was a higher incidence of SAEs in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (16%) compared to the
brepocitinib 15 mg arm (8.6%) or the placebo arm (12.7%) during the double-blind treatment
period. The majority (8 out of 13 subjects) of the SAEs in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm were under
the SOC of Infections and Infestations.
In the 90 DSU, SAEs occurred in 11.4% subjects overall and at similar rates across treatment
sequences, with Infections and Infestations being the most common SOC, accounting for 13 of
34 total SAEs in 12 subjects (6.5%).

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and OND Custom Medical Queries
Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial PVT2201-301
Overall, the rate of adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation was lower in both
brepocitinib arms compared to placebo during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of
Trial PVT-2201-301. Treatment discontinuation due to an AE occurred in 9 (11.4%) placebo
subjects, 6 (7.4%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 5 (6.2%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with
risk differences of −4.0% (95% CI: −13.9, 5.5) and −5.2% (95% CI: −15.0, 3.9) for brepocitinib
15 mg and 30 mg versus placebo, respectively. No major differences in the overall rate of
treatment discontinuation due to AEs were identified between either brepocitinib arm and
placebo.
Across SOCs, the pattern of AEs leading to discontinuation was generally distributed and low in
frequency, with no single preferred term predominating in the brepocitinib arms. Within the
Cardiac Disorders SOC, 1 (1.3%) placebo subject discontinued due to cardiac failure congestive,
while no subjects in either brepocitinib arm discontinued for cardiac reasons. Within the
Gastrointestinal Disorders SOC, discontinuations were more frequent in the brepocitinib 15 mg
arm (two subjects, 2.5%) compared to placebo (0 subjects) and brepocitinib 30 mg (1 subject,
1.2%), driven by abdominal pain, diarrhoea, and nausea in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm and
gastrointestinal pain in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm. Within the Infections and Infestations SOC,
discontinuations occurred in 2 (2.5%) placebo subjects, 1 (1.2%) brepocitinib 15 mg subject, and
1 (1.2%) brepocitinib 30 mg subject; preferred terms in the brepocitinib arms included
pneumonia (15 mg), and arthritis bacterial and osteomyelitis (30 mg), while placebo
discontinuations were attributed to coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) and infected cyst.
Within the Investigations SOC, discontinuations were reported in 1 (1.3%) placebo subject, 2
(2.5%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 1 (1.2%) brepocitinib 30 mg subject. Preferred terms in
the brepocitinib 15 mg arm included hepatic enzyme increased and weight decreased, while
blood creatinine increased was reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm and blood creatine
phosphokinase increased in the placebo arm. Within the Nervous System Disorders SOC, 1
(1.3%) placebo subject discontinued due to central nervous system lesion, and 1 (1.2%)
brepocitinib 30 mg subject discontinued due to cerebrovascular accident; no discontinuations in
this SOC were reported in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm. One subject in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm
discontinued due to insomnia under the Psychiatric Disorders SOC, while no discontinuations in
this category were observed in the other arms. Within the Respiratory, Thoracic and Mediastinal
Disorders SOC, 1 (1.3%) placebo subject discontinued due to pulmonary embolism and 1 (1.2%)
brepocitinib 15 mg subject discontinued due to dyspnoea; no discontinuations in this SOC were
reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm. Discontinuations due to dermatomyositis under the Skin
and Subcutaneous Tissue Disorders SOC and nasopharyngeal cancer metastatic under the
Neoplasms SOC were each reported in 1 (1.3%) placebo subject only, with no corresponding
events in either brepocitinib arm.
In summary, AEs leading to treatment discontinuation were infrequent across all arms and
occurred at a numerically lower rate in both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo. The
preferred terms associated with discontinuation in the brepocitinib arms were generally
consistent with the known safety profile of JAK inhibitors and the underlying disease population,
91
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
and no single AE category drove a meaningful excess of discontinuations in the brepocitinibtreated subjects relative to placebo.
In the 90 DSU, TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation were infrequent (5.4% overall) and
similar across treatment sequences, with Cardiac Disorders and Respiratory, Thoracic, and
Mediastinal Disorders being the only SOCs in which more than one subject discontinued
treatment.

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial PVT2201-301
Table 36 presents TEAEs occurring at a frequency of at least 5% in any treatment arm during the
52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301. The overall incidence of any
TEAE was comparable across treatment arms, reported in 91.1% of placebo subjects, 86.4% of
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 90.1% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects.
Among the preferred terms meeting the ≥5.0% threshold in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, several
notable patterns emerged. PTs that were ≥5.0% in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm and greater than
the placebo arm included headache (14.8% brepocitinib 30 mg arm versus 2.5% placebo arm),
upper respiratory tract infection (14.8% versus 11.4%), fatigue (12.3% versus 2.5%), nausea
(11.1% versus 3.8%), urinary tract infection (11.1% versus 5.1%), bronchitis (9.9% versus
3.8%), arthralgia (9.9% versus 7.6%), COVID-19 (9.9% versus 8.9%), diarrhoea (8.6% versus
5.1%), back pain (8.6% versus 6.3%), fall (7.4% versus 2.5%), acne (6.2% versus 0%), and
influenza (6.2% versus 3.8%).
In the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as reported in the 90 DSU, the most frequently
reported TEAEs (>5% in any treatment group) were predominantly infections, including upper
respiratory tract infection (7.6%), nasopharyngitis (5.9%), bronchitis (5.4%), herpes zoster
(5.4%), and urinary tract infection (5.4%), consistent with the known class risk of JAK
inhibitors. Most events were mild to moderate in severity, resolved without permanent treatment
discontinuation, and occurred at similar rates across all treatment sequences.
In addition, TEAEs by SOC and PT occurring in at least 1.5% of subjects in any treatment arm
during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301 were evaluated by the
review team. The TEAE profile of brepocitinib 30 mg was characterized by increases in
headache, fatigue, and dizziness relative to placebo, while brepocitinib 15 mg was associated
with increases in nausea, dizziness, and sinusitis. Acne was increased in both brepocitinib arms.
Overall, the common TEAE profile of brepocitinib 30 mg in Trial PVT-2201-301 was
characterized by a higher frequency of headache and fatigue relative to placebo, while the
brepocitinib 15 mg arm was associated with a higher frequency of nausea. Evaluation of less
frequent events (occurring in ≥1.5% but ≤5% of subjects) identified additional imbalances,
notably dizziness in both brepocitinib arms and sinusitis in the 15 mg arm. Additionally, acne
was increased across both brepocitinib dose groups. These findings are consistent with the
known pharmacological profile of JAK inhibitors and are reflected in the current labeling. No
unexpected safety signals were identified from the common TEAE analysis.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Organ System by OND Custom Medical Query (Narrow)
Table 37 presents TEAEs organized by organ system and OND Custom Medical Query (OCMQ)
Narrow OCMQ during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301. The
overall incidence of any TEAE was 91.1%, 86.4%, and 90.1% in the placebo, brepocitinib
15 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg arms, respectively.
The most clinically notable finding was within the Nervous System Disorders organ system,
where headache occurred at a higher rate in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (18.1%) compared to
placebo (6.7%), with a risk difference of 12.3% (95% CI: 4.0, 22.0). Dizziness was also higher in
both brepocitinib arms (6.1% each) compared to placebo (2.1%). Within the Infections and
Infestations organ system, bacterial infection was higher in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (27.2%)
compared to placebo (11.4%), with a risk difference of 15.8% (95% CI: 3.6, 27.9). Within the
General Disorders organ system, fatigue was higher in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (13.6%)
compared to placebo (3.8%), with a risk difference of 9.8% (95% CI: 1.1, 19.5). Within the
Gastrointestinal Disorders organ system, nausea was higher in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm
(13.6%) compared to placebo (3.8%), with a risk difference of 9.8% (95% CI: 1.1, 19.5). Within
the Immune System Disorders organ system, hypersensitivity higher in the brepocitinib 30 mg
arm (6.2%) compared to placebo (0%), with a risk difference of 6.2% (95% CI: 1.4, 13.7).
Within the Renal and Urinary Disorders organ system, renal and urinary tract infections were
higher in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (14.8%) compared to placebo (5.1%), with a risk difference
of 9.8% (95% CI: 0.5, 19.8). No notable differences were identified for the remaining organ
systems, including cardiac, hematologic, hepatobiliary, metabolic, musculoskeletal, neoplasm,
psychiatric, reproductive, respiratory, skin, or vascular categories.
Overall, the Narrow OCMQ analysis identified increases in headache, dizziness, bacterial
infection, fatigue, nausea, hypersensitivity, and renal and urinary tract infections in one or both
brepocitinib arms relative to placebo, consistent with the known JAK inhibitor class profile. No
unexpected safety signals were identified.

102
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Organ System by broad OCMQ
The overall pattern of findings and the direction of differences between treatment arms remain
similar between the Narrow OCMQ and the broad OCMQ (bOCMQ), the latter is presented in
Table 38.
The most notable differences between the two analyses are as follows. Within the Nervous
System Disorders organ system, the bOCMQ analysis identifies increases in syncope for both
brepocitinib arms (4.9% brepocitinib 15 mg and 8.6% brepocitinib 30 mg versus 0% placebo)
and somnolence for the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (13.6% versus 3.8% placebo), neither of which
were notable in the Narrow OCMQ analysis. Within the General Disorders organ system, the
bOCMQ captures an increase in falls for the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (17.3% versus 3.8%
placebo) and volume depletion (4.9% versus 0% placebo), which were not notable in the Narrow
analysis. Within the Infections and Infestations organ system, the bOCMQ identifies an increase
in bacterial infection for the brepocitinib 30 mg arm that is numerically larger than in the Narrow
analysis (37.0% versus 21.5% placebo), reflecting the inclusion of additional bacterial infection
preferred terms. Within the Gastrointestinal Disorders organ system, the bOCMQ captures
higher overall incidence rates for nausea and vomiting by grouping them together, though the
pattern is similar to the Narrow analysis.
Overall, the two analyses are concordant in their key findings and do not reveal meaningfully
different safety conclusions. The significant findings identified in the Narrow analysis are
preserved in the Broad analysis, with the Broad analysis additionally flagging syncope,
somnolence, falls, and volume depletion as higher in one or more brepocitinib arms.
On review of the bOCMQ listings for syncope, somnolence, falls, and volume depletion,
significant overlap in subjects was identified across these categories. The majority of subjects
contributing to the bOCMQ categories of volume depletion and syncope were also captured
under other broad query terms, although the timing of the individual adverse events was not
always concordant across categories. Notably, the majority of events captured under the
somnolence category were coded to the preferred term of fatigue rather than somnolence, which
likely reflects the broad scope of the bOCMQ search strategy and suggests that the somnolence
signal identified in the Broad analysis may overestimate the incidence of somnolence as a
distinct adverse event. These findings suggest that the additional signals in the bOCMQ analysis
may largely reflect subject overlap and broad preferred term capture rather than distinct safety
signals beyond those identified in the Narrow analysis.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial PVT-2201-301
Chemistry
Table 39 presents the proportions of subjects with general chemistry analyte values exceeding
pre-specified threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT2201-301. Threshold Levels 1, 2, and 3 are defined per the Standard Safety Tables and Figures
Integrated Guide (FDA 2025), with subject counts cumulative for each abnormality threshold.
Risk differences with 95% confidence intervals are estimated using the Miettinen and Nurminen
method.
Sodium, chloride, calcium, potassium, and albumin abnormalities were generally low in
frequency and comparable across arms, with no notable differences identified.
Low bicarbonate at Level 1 (<20 mEq/L) was reported in 5.1% of placebo subjects, 6.2% of
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 12.3% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with a risk difference of
7.2% (95% CI: −1.8, 16.9) for brepocitinib 30 mg versus placebo. At Level 2 (<18 mEq/L), low
bicarbonate was reported in 0% of placebo subjects, 0% of brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and
2.5% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with a risk difference of 2.5% (95% CI: −2.3, 8.6). The
review team also analyzed mean change from baseline in bicarbonate for further evaluation of
these findings. Mean baseline serum bicarbonate levels were comparable across all three
treatment arms and within the normal reference range. Over the 52-week treatment period, small
decreases from baseline were observed across all arms at most time points, with the pattern of
change generally consistent between brepocitinib-treated and placebo-treated subjects. At Week
52, the mean change from baseline was −0.7 mEq/L (95% CI: −1.3, −0.1) for placebo, −0.3
mEq/L (95% CI: −0.8, 0.3) for brepocitinib 15 mg, and −0.6 mEq/L (95% CI: −1.2, 0.0) for
brepocitinib 30 mg. Absolute bicarbonate values at Week 52 remained within or near the normal
reference range across all arms (placebo: 25.7 mEq/L; brepocitinib 15 mg: 25.8 mEq/L;
brepocitinib 30 mg: 25.5 mEq/L). Also see Section 7.6.2.6 for a description of bicarbonate
changes in Pool S1. The observed bicarbonate decreases at the labeled 30 mg dose are modest
and did not result in clinically significant metabolic acidosis in any subject during the study.
Although the mechanism behind the observed bicarbonate decreases in subjects treated with
brepocitinib is unknown, the findings do not appear to represent a safety issue at the proposed
labeled dose of 30 mg based on the available data.
Low glucose at Level 1 (<70 mg/dL) was higher in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm (24.7%)
compared to placebo (9.0%), with a risk difference of 15.7% (95% CI: 4.2, 27.4). Low glucose at
Level 2 (<54 mg/dL) was higher in both brepocitinib arms, 4.9% in the 15 mg arm and 6.2% in
the 30 mg arm, compared to 0% in the placebo arm, with risk differences of 4.9% (95% CI: 0.1,
12.1) and 6.2% (95% CI: 1.3, 13.7), respectively. See Section 7.6.2.7 for further discussion
regarding the hypoglycemia events in the clinical development program. High fasting and
random glucose values were generally comparable across arms.
Elevations in creatine phosphokinase (CPK) were common across all arms, likely reflecting the
underlying dermatomyositis disease population. At Level 1 (>3× ULN), CPK elevations were
reported in 29.5% of placebo, 24.7% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 33.3% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects. At Level 2 (>5× ULN) and Level 3 (>10× ULN), rates were numerically higher in the

108
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
placebo arm compared to both brepocitinib arms. The high baseline CPK elevations in this
population are consistent with active dermatomyositis.
Refer to Kidney Function for a detailed description of the creatinine and eGFR findings from the
pivotal trial.
In summary, the most clinically significant general chemistry finding in Trial PVT-2201-301,
apart from renal function, which is described separately, was increases in low glucose values in
the brepocitinib arms (see Section 7.6.2.7 for further discussion). CPK elevations were common
across all arms, consistent with the underlying dermatomyositis disease population, and did not
differ significantly between brepocitinib and placebo. These laboratory findings, particularly the
renal function changes, are important considerations for the overall benefit-risk assessment of
brepocitinib and should be reflected in product labeling.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Kidney Function
Table 40 presents the proportion of subjects in each treatment arm (placebo N=79, brepocitinib
15 mg N=81, and brepocitinib 30 mg N=81) whose serum creatinine and eGFR values exceeded
pre-specified abnormality threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period,
with eGFR calculated from serum creatinine using the chronic kidney disease epidemiology
collaboration (CKD-EPI) equation and risk differences estimated using the Miettinen and
Nurminen method.
Creatinine elevations at Level 1 (≥1.5× baseline) were higher in both brepocitinib arms, 21.0% in
the 15 mg arm and 37.0% in the 30 mg arm, compared to 2.6% in the placebo arm, with risk
differences of 18.4% (95% CI: 9.3, 28.9) and 34.5% (95% CI: 23.6, 45.8), respectively.
Creatinine elevations at Level 2 (≥2× baseline) were also higher in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm
(8.6%) compared to placebo (0%), with a risk difference of 8.6% (95% CI: 3.7, 16.8). Similarly,
eGFR decreases at Level 1 (≥25% decrease) were higher in both brepocitinib arms, 35.8% in the
15 mg arm and 61.7% in the 30 mg arm, compared to 10.3% in the placebo arm, with risk
differences of 25.5% (95% CI: 12.9, 37.9) and 51.5% (95% CI: 38.0, 63.0), respectively. These
creatinine and eGFR changes are likely based on brepocitinib’s inhibition of organic cation
transporters and reflect reduced tubular secretion of creatinine rather than true glomerular
filtration impairment, a conclusion supported by the absence of corresponding decreases in
cystatin C-based eGFR (see Figure 14). Unlike creatinine, cystatin C is freely filtered at the
glomerulus and is not subject to tubular secretion, making it a more reliable biomarker of true
glomerular filtration rate (GFR) independent of transporter-mediated mechanisms. The mean
change from baseline in cystatin C-based eGFR over the 52-week blinded treatment period did
not demonstrate clinically meaningful or sustained declines across treatment arms, suggesting
that brepocitinib does not exert a genuine nephrotoxic effect on glomerular filtration function.
Additionally, an analysis by the review team evaluating eGFR following treatment
discontinuation for any reason demonstrated an overall trend where eGFR improved or remained
stable in brepocitinib-treated subjects. This reversibility upon discontinuation provides further
evidence that the observed eGFR changes do not represent true renal toxicity and are consistent
with brepocitinib's inhibition of organic cation transporters. Refer also to the Clinical
Pharmacology discussion of the effect of brepocitinib on creatinine in Section 8.2.
Review of data from the OLE period, as presented in the 90 DSU, found that clinically
meaningful reductions in renal function (defined as concurrent reductions of >50% in both
cystatin C-based and creatinine-based eGFR) were rare. Isolated increases in serum creatininebased eGFR were observed with no concurrent reductions in cystatin C-based eGFR.
The United States Prescribing Information (USPI) states that brepocitinib is not recommended in
patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²), which is reasonable as the
pharmacokinetic and safety profile of brepocitinib in this population has not been adequately
characterized.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug Induced Liver Injury, Trial PVT2201-301
Liver Biochemistry
Table 41 presents the proportions of subjects with liver biochemistry analyte values exceeding
pre-specified threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT2201-301. Threshold Levels 1, 2, and 3 are defined per the Standard Safety Tables and Figures
Integrated Guide (FDA 2025), with subject counts cumulative for each abnormality threshold.
Risk differences with 95% confidence intervals are estimated using the Miettinen and Nurminen
method. An important interpretive consideration for all liver biochemistry findings in this trial is
the underlying dermatomyositis disease population, in which chronic and recurrent myopathy
results in ongoing shedding of ALT and AST from skeletal muscle, independent of any hepatic
injury. This creates a unique challenge for the detection of drug-induced liver injury in this
population, as discussed further in this section.
Liver Biochemistry Analytes
Alkaline Phosphatase (ALP) elevations were infrequent and comparable across all arms at all
threshold levels, with no notable differences identified. At Level 1 (>1.5× ULN), ALP elevations
117
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
were reported in 3.8% of placebo, 1.2% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 6.2% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects.
ALT elevations at Level 1 (>3× ULN) were reported in 9.0% of placebo, 7.4% of brepocitinib
15 mg, and 9.9% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with no notable differences identified. At Level
2 (>5× ULN), elevations were reported in 1.3% of placebo, 2.5% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 0%
of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects. At Level 3 (>10× ULN), only 1 subject in the brepocitinib 15 mg
arm (1.2%) met this threshold, compared to 0% in both other arms.
AST elevations at Level 1 (>3× ULN) were reported in 3.8% of placebo, 7.4% of brepocitinib
15 mg, and 4.9% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with no notable differences identified. At Level
2 (>5× ULN), elevations were reported in 0% of placebo, 2.5% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 1.2%
of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects. No subjects in any arm met the Level 3 threshold (>10× ULN).
The reviewer notes a discrepancy between the ALT and AST >3× ULN rates presented in Table
41 and those reported in the clinical data summary outputs for Trial PVT-2201-301 and in the
(b) (4)
proposed labeling in Section 16. The Applicant reported
(b) (4)

However, Table 41 reports any subjects who have >3× ULN for any post-baseline
value. The Applicant was asked to reconcile these figures (see Section 16).
(b) (4)

Total bilirubin elevations were infrequent across all arms. At Level 1 (>1.5× ULN), elevations
were reported in 3.8% of placebo, 2.5% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 3.7% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects. No subjects in any arm met the Level 2 (>2× ULN) or Level 3 (>3× ULN) thresholds.
No notable differences were identified at any total bilirubin threshold level.

review the hepatic safety data from Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028 (SLE).

Drug-Induced Liver Injury Tables
Table 42 presents the results of a hepatocellular DILI screening analysis conducted during the
52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301. A potential Hy’s Law case was
defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin ≥2× ULN within 30 days after a postbaseline
ALT or AST ≥3× ULN, with the 30-day analysis window rule not applying to cholestasis and
Temple’s corollary cases.
No potential Hy’s Law cases were identified in any treatment arm. One subject in the placebo
arm (1.3%) and one subject in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (1.2%) met the cholestasis criterion.
Temple’s corollary cases, defined as subjects with elevated ALT or AST ≥3× ULN in the
absence of elevated bilirubin, were reported in 7/78 (9.0%) placebo subjects, 6/81 (7.4%)
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 11/81 (13.6%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, numerically higher
proportion in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm compared to placebo and brepocitinib 15 mg
warranting further evaluation. The total number of subjects meeting any quadrant of interest
criterion was 8/78 (10.3%), 6/81 (7.4%), and 12/81 (14.8%) for placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, and
brepocitinib 30 mg, respectively.

DHN DILI Team Consultation
The DHN DILI Team confirmed that no cases met Hy’s Law criteria in Trial PVT-2201-301. Of
the subjects with AT elevations >5× ULN, the DILI Team assessed three cases for likelihood of
attribution to brepocitinib using U.S. drug-induced liver injury network (DILIN) causality
scoring. One case from Trial PVT-2201-301 was assessed as probable DILI due to brepocitinib,
a 37-year-old female in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm who developed elevated liver enzymes
approximately 27 days after starting brepocitinib, with ALT peaking at 444 U/L, AST peaking at
268 U/L, ALP peaking at 474 U/L, and GGT peaking at 1,515 U/L on Study Day 57, with all
values returning to near-normal range by Study Day 90 following permanent discontinuation of
124
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
brepocitinib on Study Day 63. CK levels were consistently low (<100 U/L) throughout the event
and GGT exceeded 1,000 U/L, a pattern more consistent with hepatocellular injury than with
muscle-derived aminotransferase elevations, which do not produce GGT elevation. The latency
and dechallenge washout were consistent with DILI, though the fall in ALT had begun even
before brepocitinib was stopped. Total bilirubin remained within the normal range throughout,
abdominal ultrasound was normal, and the event was asymptomatic and resolved completely.
The remaining two cases assessed by the DILI Team were considered unlikely DILI, with
myopathy favored as the more likely explanation for the AT elevations based on concurrent CK
elevations.
The DILI Team also noted that the numerically higher proportion of subjects in the Temple’s
Corollary Quadrant in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (13.6%) compared to placebo (9.0%) on the
eDISH plot suggests a possible dose-response relationship for AT elevations starting at the
labeled 30-mg dose, though the subject with the highest AT elevation was in the brepocitinib
15 mg arm. The DILI Team further highlighted the challenge of detecting hepatocellular injury
in the dermatomyositis population, as ongoing muscle inflammation results in shedding of ALT
and AST from muscle, which does not shed GGT, potentially obscuring early recognition of
drug-induced liver injury. Accordingly, the DILI Team recommended that GGT be added to the
routine liver blood test monitoring panel for brepocitinib to help clinicians distinguish liverderived from muscle-derived aminotransferase elevations in this population (see Section 16).
Overall, the DILI Team concluded that the DILI risk is manageable with appropriate labeling
and does not preclude approval and did not recommend a postmarketing requirement or
commitment for this safety finding.
Open-Label Extension Period
During the OLE period, as described in the 90 DSU, two Grade 3 elevations (AST or ALT ≥10×
ULN) occurred; both were accompanied by marked concurrent CK, LDH, and aldolase
elevations most consistent with active myositis rather than drug-induced hepatotoxicity, bilirubin
remained within the normal range in both cases, and no subjects met Hy’s Law criteria. These
findings are consistent with the blinded treatment period data and do not suggest an emerging
hepatotoxicity signal with longer-term brepocitinib use.
In summary, no potential Hy’s Law cases were identified in any treatment arm, and one case of
probable DILI was identified by the DILI Team in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm, characterized by
a hepatocellular pattern with marked GGT elevation and low concurrent CK, which resolved
completely following drug discontinuation. The numerically higher proportion of Temple’s
corollary cases in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm compared to placebo suggests a possible doserelated hepatotoxicity signal at the labeled dose, though the absence of hyperbilirubinemia in any
case and the high background rate of muscle-derived aminotransferase elevations in the
dermatomyositis population limit the interpretability of these findings. The addition of GGT to
the recommended monitoring panel, as recommended by the DHN DILI Team, is considered
appropriate and is reflected in the labeling. Overall, the hepatic safety profile of brepocitinib is
considered manageable through appropriate baseline testing, enhanced monitoring, and labeling,
consistent with the DILI Team’s conclusion that the identified hepatotoxicity signal does not
preclude approval.

Lipids
Table 44 presents the proportions of subjects with lipid analyte values exceeding pre-specified
threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301
(placebo N=79, brepocitinib 15 mg N=81, brepocitinib 30 mg N=81). Threshold Levels 1, 2, and
3 are defined per the Standard Safety Tables and Figures Integrated Guide (FDA 2025), with
subject counts cumulative for each abnormality threshold. Risk differences with 95% confidence
intervals are estimated using the Miettinen and Nurminen method.
Elevations in total cholesterol were common across all arms, reflecting the underlying disease
population and background immunosuppressive therapies. At Level 1 (>200 mg/dL), elevations
were reported in 66.7% of placebo, 77.8% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 74.1% of brepocitinib
30 mg subjects. At Level 2 (>210 mg/dL) and Level 3 (>225 mg/dL), rates were numerically
higher in both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo.
Among female subjects, low HDL at Level 2 (<40 mg/dL) was lower in both brepocitinib arms
(16.4% in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm and 20.0% in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, compared to
37.0% in the placebo arm), with risk differences of −20.6% (95% CI: −36.2, −4.9) and −17.0%
(95% CI: −33.0, −0.8). Among male subjects, HDL abnormalities were numerically lower in
both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo at all threshold levels; however, there were few
male subjects in the study.
LDL elevations were common across all arms. At Level 1 (>130 mg/dL), elevations were
reported in 67.9% of placebo, 65.4% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 74.1% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects. At Level 2 (>160 mg/dL) and Level 3 (>190 mg/dL), rates were numerically higher in
both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo.
Triglyceride elevations were highly prevalent across all arms, again consistent with the
underlying disease population and background therapies. At Level 1 (>150 mg/dL), elevations
were reported in 78.2% of placebo, 66.7% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 70.4% of brepocitinib
30 mg subjects, with rates numerically lower in both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo. At
Level 2 (>300 mg/dL) and Level 3 (>500 mg/dL), rates were also numerically lower in both
brepocitinib arms compared to placebo.
During the OLE period, as presented in the 90 DSU, mean changes in lipid parameters, including
total cholesterol, LDL, HDL, and triglycerides, were small in magnitude across all treatment
sequences at Week 104, with stable LDL/HDL ratios and no mean increases in triglycerides;
Grade 4 lipid elevations were not observed.
In summary, the lipid findings from Trial PVT-2201-301 did not identify any major increases in
total cholesterol, LDL, or triglyceride elevations in either brepocitinib arm relative to placebo.
Low HDL cholesterol at Level 2 was less frequent in both brepocitinib arms compared to
placebo among female subjects. The high prevalence of lipid abnormalities across all arms is
consistent with the underlying dermatomyositis disease population and the use of background
corticosteroids and immunosuppressive therapies. Overall, the lipid findings do not indicate an
adverse lipid effect associated with brepocitinib at either dose studied in Trial PVT-2201-301,
with the labeling accounting for known lipid effects seen in the broader JAK inhibitor class.

Hematology
Table 45 presents the proportions of subjects with hematology analyte values exceeding prespecified threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201301. Threshold Levels 1, 2, and 3 are defined per the Standard Safety Tables and Figures
Integrated Guide (FDA 2025), with subject counts cumulative for each abnormality threshold.
Risk differences with 95% confidence intervals are estimated using the Miettinen and Nurminen
method.
Hemoglobin decreases were more frequent in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm compared to placebo at
both threshold levels assessed. At Level 2 (>1.5 g/dL decrease from baseline), decreases were
reported in 16.7% of placebo, 22.2% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 40.7% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects, with a risk difference of 24.1% (95% CI: 10.2, 37.3) for brepocitinib 30 mg versus
placebo. At Level 3 (>2 g/dL decrease from baseline), decreases were reported in 9.0% of
placebo, 11.1% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 28.4% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with a risk
difference of 19.4% (95% CI: 7.6, 31.3) for brepocitinib 30 mg versus placebo. No notable
differences were identified for the brepocitinib 15 mg arm at either hemoglobin decrease
threshold. Hemoglobin increases were infrequent and comparable across arms, with no notable
differences identified.
Low lymphocyte counts were common across all arms, consistent with the underlying
dermatomyositis disease population and background immunosuppressive therapies. At Level 1
(<1 × 10³ cells/uL), low lymphocyte counts were reported in 57.7% of placebo, 50.6% of
brepocitinib 15 mg, and 59.3% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with no major differences
identified. At Level 2 (<0.75 × 10³ cells/uL) and Level 3 (<0.5 × 10³ cells/uL), rates were also
comparable across arms with no major differences identified. High lymphocyte counts were
infrequent and comparable across arms.
Low neutrophil counts were numerically higher in both brepocitinib arms compared to placebo,
though no major differences were identified at any threshold level. At Level 1 (<2 × 10³
cells/uL), low neutrophil counts were reported in 12.8% of placebo, 19.8% of brepocitinib
15 mg, and 22.2% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects. At Level 2 (<1 × 10³ cells/uL), low neutrophil
counts were reported in 0% of placebo, 2.5% of brepocitinib 15 mg, and 1.2% of brepocitinib
30 mg subjects, with no subjects in any arm meeting the Level 3 threshold (<0.5 × 10³ cells/uL).
Low white blood cells counts, high white blood cells counts, eosinophil counts, and platelet
counts were comparable across all arms at all threshold levels, with no notable differences
identified. Prothrombin time and partial thromboplastin time data were available for Trial PVT2201-301 and were without notable differences at any threshold level.
During the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as presented in the 90 DSU, hematologic
changes were generally mild and infrequent; Grade 3 neutropenia (defined as neutrophil count
<1.0 to 0.5 × 10⁹/L) occurred in only 1 subject (1.8%) in the placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg
sequence, Grade 3 lymphocyte reductions (defined as lymphocyte count <0.5 to 0.2 × 10⁹/L)
occurred in 1 to 2 subjects per treatment sequence, and Grade 3 hemoglobin reductions (defined
as hemoglobin <80 g/L) occurred in 1 subject each in the placebo-to-30 mg (1.8%) and 30 mgto-30 mg (1.5%) sequences; no Grade 4 reductions in any hematologic parameter occurred, and
platelet parameters showed no shifts of ≥2 Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events

128
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
(CTCAE) grades from OLE baseline and no Grade 3 or Grade 4 reductions, suggesting no
clinically significant thrombocytopenic risk associated with brepocitinib 30 mg.
In summary, the notable hematology findings in Trial PVT-2201-301 include a dose-dependent
increase in hemoglobin decreases in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm relative to placebo, observed at
both the Level 2 and Level 3 thresholds, as well as numerically higher rates of low neutrophil
counts in both brepocitinib arms relative to placebo. These findings are consistent with the
known hematologic effects of JAK inhibitors; accordingly, the proposed brepocitinib USPI
includes recommendations under Warnings and Precautions to monitor hemoglobin and
neutrophil counts due to the potential for clinically meaningful changes. The high prevalence of
lymphopenia observed across all treatment arms is consistent with the underlying
dermatomyositis disease population and concomitant use of background immunosuppressive
therapy. Nevertheless, consistent with labeling for other JAK inhibitors, the proposed
brepocitinib USPI also includes monitoring of lymphocyte counts under Warnings and
Precautions.

7.6.1.8. Vital-Sign Analyses, Trial PVT-2201-301
There were no clinically meaningful findings in vital signs measurements, physical examination,
or EKG during the double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301.
During the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as presented in the 90 DSU, no clinically
significant abnormalities in vital signs were observed across any treatment sequence.
Additionally, most subjects (93.5%, n=173) experienced maximum post-baseline QTcF values
within the normal range (≤450 msec). No subjects exceeded the regulatory threshold of QTcF
>500 msec, and no subjects experienced a maximum post-baseline QTcF >480 msec.
In summary, data from trial PVT-2201-301 do not indicate a significant effect of brepocitinib
30 mg on cardiac repolarization or other vital sign parameters during long-term use.
7.6.1.9. Subgroup Analysis, Trial PVT-2201-301
Table 46 presents an overview of TEAEs stratified by demographic subgroups during the 52week double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301 (placebo N=79, brepocitinib 15 mg
N=81, brepocitinib 30 mg N=81). Overall, the subgroup analysis did not identify any
demographic subgroup in which the overall TEAE incidence was meaningfully different between
either brepocitinib arm or placebo. The high and generally comparable TEAE rates across all
subgroups are consistent with the overall safety findings from Trial PVT-2201-301, and no
subgroup-specific safety signals were identified. Interpretability of findings in smaller
subgroups, particularly those defined by race and sex, is limited by the small number of subjects
within each category.
The review team also analyzed SAEs and AESI by demographic subgroups during the doubleblind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301. The most notable finding was a higher rate of
SAEs in adults ≥65 years of age (placebo 15.4%, brepocitinib 15 mg 30.0%, and brepocitinib 30
mg 21.4%) which was driven largely by viral reactivation of herpes zoster and herpes simplex.
However, the risk of viral reactivation, particularly herpes zoster, is a well-characterized class
effect for JAK inhibitors and is already addressed with a Boxed Warning in the prescribing
information of brepocitinib.

7.6.1.10. Exposure-Adjusted Analyses, Trial PVT-2201-301,
Open-Label Extension Period, Data Cut March 1, 2025
(Not Including 90-DSU)
The OLE period of Trial PVT-2201-301 enrolled 121 subjects who had completed the 52-week
double-blind treatment period and elected to continue into the optional OLE, during which all
subjects received brepocitinib 30 mg once daily regardless of their prior blinded treatment
assignment, resulting in three treatment sequences: placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg (N=36),
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg (N=41), and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg (N=44). The OLE period
was also 52 weeks in duration, and safety data were analyzed using a data cut of March 1, 2025.
The population was predominantly female (approximately 78% across all sequences) and White
(approximately 74%), with a mean age of approximately 50.6 years, broadly consistent with the
demographics of the blinded treatment period. Mean treatment duration during the OLE period
was 26.2 weeks (SD 15.3), 26.9 weeks (SD 14.5), and 24.0 weeks (SD 15.9) for the placebo-to30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences, respectively,
with total exposure of 18.1, 21.2, and 20.3 patient-years across the three sequences.
Overall TEAE rates were higher in the brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg and brepocitinib 30 mg-to30 mg sequences (EAIRs of 348.4 and 329.7 per 100 patient-years, respectively) compared to the
placebo-to-30 mg sequence (180.1 per 100 patient-years), though this difference likely reflects
the longer cumulative brepocitinib exposure in the former two sequences (see Table 47). The
majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity, with severe TEAEs occurring at low and
comparable EAIRs of 5.8, 4.7, and 5.1 per 100 patient-years for the placebo-to-30 mg,
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences, respectively. SAE
rates were low and comparable across all three sequences (EAIRs of 11.7, 14.7, and 10.2 per 100
patient-years), and AEs leading to permanent treatment discontinuation were rare during the
OLE period, occurring in 0, 1 (EAIR 4.8), and 1 (EAIR 4.9) subjects in the placebo-to-30 mg,
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences, respectively. No
deaths were reported during the OLE period in this data cut (see discussion of deaths in 90 DSU,
in Section 7.6.1.2). Treatment interruptions due to AEs occurred at similar rates across
sequences, with EAIRs of 33.0, 39.8, and 26.4 per 100 patient-years in the placebo-to-30 mg,
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences, respectively.
SAEs were infrequent across all treatment sequences and occurred in 2, 3, and 2 subjects in the
placebo-to-30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences,
respectively (see Table 48). The most common SAE SOC was Infections and Infestations, with
no events occurring in the placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg arm, 2 (EAIR 9.7) in the brepocitinib
15 mg-to-30 mg arm, and 1 (EAIR 5.1) brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg arm. No SAEs were
reported in more than one subject within any individual preferred term across any treatment
sequence, reflecting the overall rarity of serious events during the OLE period.
The most frequently reported TEAEs during the OLE period were infections, consistent with the
blinded treatment period findings. Upper respiratory tract infection was among the most common
events across all sequences, with EAIRs of 18.1, 19.8, and 15.3 per 100 patient-years in the
placebo-to-30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences,
respectively (see Table 49). Herpes zoster was not reported in the placebo-to-30 mg sequence,
while two subjects were affected in each of the brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg (EAIR 9.6 per 100
136
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
patient-years) and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg (EAIR 10.1 per 100 patient-years) sequences,
consistent with the known class risk of herpes zoster reactivation with JAK inhibitors. Fatigue
was not reported in the placebo-to-30 mg or brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg sequences but occurred
in two subjects in the brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequence (EAIR 10.3 per 100 patient-years).
Acne was reported in one subject each in the placebo-to-30 mg and brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg
sequences and in two subjects in the brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequence, consistent with the
known class-related dermatologic effects of JAK inhibitors. Urinary tract infection was reported
across all three sequences at comparable rates, with EAIRs of 11.7, 9.9, and 15.5 per 100 patientyears, respectively. COVID-19 was reported in two subjects in the placebo-to-30 mg sequence
and one subject each in the brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg
sequences, reflecting ongoing background infection risk in this immunosuppressed population.
Conclusion
The OLE period safety data from Trial PVT-2201-301, reflecting up to 52 weeks of open-label
brepocitinib 30 mg treatment across all three prior treatment sequences, are broadly consistent
with the safety profile established during the 52-week blinded treatment period and do not
identify any new or unexpected safety signals with longer-term brepocitinib exposure. SAEs
were infrequent and comparable across treatment sequences, with infections remaining the most
common SAE SOC, consistent with the known class risk of JAK inhibitors. AEs leading to
permanent treatment discontinuation were rare, and no deaths were reported during this data cut
of the OLE period. The overall TEAE burden appeared numerically higher in the brepocitinib
15 mg-to-30 mg and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences compared to the placebo-to-30 mg
sequence, likely reflecting the longer cumulative brepocitinib exposure in these groups rather
than a true increase in risk with continued treatment. The occurrence of herpes zoster in both the
brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg and brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequences, and the single case of
peripheral artery thrombosis leading to treatment discontinuation, are consistent with the known
class-related risks of JAK inhibitors and are adequately addressed by the proposed labeling. In
summary, the OLE period safety data support the conclusion that brepocitinib 30 mg has an
acceptable and manageable safety profile with longer-term use in adults with dermatomyositis.

7.6.1.11. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Trial PVT-2201301
This section provides an overview of the following AESI based on class-related concerns:
•

Cardiovascular events

•

Thromboembolic events

•

Viral reactivation

•

New or recurrent diagnoses of malignancy

•

Opportunistic infections

•

Liver enzyme elevation AEs (see discussion of liver enzyme elevation in Section 7.6.1.6)

Overall, the review of AESI from Trial PVT-2201-301 identified no unexpected adverse events
and no evidence of worsening of previously identified known risks within the class of JAK
inhibitors. The current labeling is adequate to convey the risk of cardiovascular and
thromboembolic events, viral reactivation, new or recurrent diagnoses of malignancy,
opportunistic infections, and liver enzyme elevations, with the agreed-upon revisions to the
USPI.
Cardiovascular Events
Table 50 presents the assessment of cardiovascular AESI during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301.
In the brepocitinib arms, 1 cardiovascular AESI was reported: a cerebrovascular accident
occurring in 1 (1.2%) subject in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, which was assessed as moderate in
severity, serious, and led to treatment discontinuation. No cardiovascular AESIs were reported in
the brepocitinib 15 mg arm. In the placebo arm, 2 cardiovascular AESIs were reported: 1 (1.3%)
subject with cardiac failure congestive and 1 (1.3%) subject with myocardial injury, with the
cardiac failure congestive case assessed as life-threatening and severe and leading to treatment
discontinuation, and the myocardial injury case also assessed as investigator related. No deaths
due to cardiovascular events were reported in any arm during the double-blind treatment period.
140
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
The risk difference for cardiovascular AESIs was −2.5% (95% CI: −8.8, 2.1) for brepocitinib
15 mg versus placebo and −1.3% (95% CI: −7.7, 4.4) for brepocitinib 30 mg versus placebo.
During the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as presented in the 90 DSU, 5 cardiovascular
AESIs occurred in 2 subjects (1.1%) across the brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg and brepocitinib
30 mg-to-30 mg treatment sequences, with preferred terms of coronary artery disease (×2),
myocarditis, cardiogenic shock, and myocardial rupture. Four of the five events met adjudication
criteria for MACE, specifically cardiovascular death (cardiogenic shock and myocardial rupture),
myocardial infarction (myocarditis), and unstable angina (coronary artery disease [worsening]),
and no events met adjudication criteria for urgent heart failure, transient ischemic attack, or
stroke. Notably, both affected subjects had significant pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors
and comorbidities at baseline, including multiple cardiovascular risk factors and abnormal ECG
in the subject who experienced cardiogenic shock following CABG, and a history of mitral and
tricuspid regurgitation, mitral valve calcification, lung fibrosis, pulmonary hypertension, and
diabetes in the subject who experienced myocardial rupture following planned double valve
replacement surgery, and both fatal cardiovascular events were assessed as not related to
brepocitinib by both the Investigator and the Applicant. EAIRs for cardiovascular AESIs
remaining low and similar across treatment sequences: 0 per 100 patient-years (PYs) for the
placebo-to-30 mg sequence, 1.6 [95% CI: 0.23, 11.53] per 100 PYs for the brepocitinib 15 mgto-30 mg sequence, and 1.5 [95% CI: 0.21, 10.47] per 100 PYs for the brepocitinib 30 mg-to30 mg sequence.
In summary, cardiovascular AESIs were infrequent during the double-blind treatment period of
Trial PVT-2201-301, with a numerically lower total rate in both brepocitinib arms compared to
placebo. The single cardiovascular AESI in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, a cerebrovascular
accident, was positively adjudicated by the external Clinical Events Classification Committee
(CEC), while neither of the two cardiovascular AESIs in the placebo arm met the prespecified
adjudication criteria for cardiovascular events according to the CEC. In the OLE Period,
cardiovascular AESIs occurred in subjects with significant pre-existing cardiovascular risk
factors and comorbidities at baseline; the majority of events met adjudication criteria for MACE,
and all fatal cardiovascular events were assessed as not related to brepocitinib by both the
Investigator and the Applicant. EAIRs for cardiovascular AESIs remained low across all
treatment sequences, with wide and overlapping confidence intervals suggesting no meaningful
difference in cardiovascular risk between treatment groups. Taken together, the totality of
cardiovascular safety data from both the double-blind treatment period and the OLE Period of
Trial PVT-2201-301 does not suggest a brepocitinib-attributable cardiovascular risk signal, and
the current labeling is considered adequate to convey the cardiovascular risk associated with
brepocitinib treatment.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Opportunistic Infections
There were no events of opportunistic infections in the brepocitinib arms during the double-blind
treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301. Two subjects reported three events of opportunistic
infection in the open-label treatment period: one subject developed disseminated herpes zoster
followed by varicella zoster pneumonia and the other subject developed legionella pneumonia.
All three events met adjudication criteria for opportunistic infection by the CEC and resolved.
Additionally, there was case of bone tuberculosis during the open-label treatment period that did
not meet the prespecified criteria of an AESI but was adjudicated as an opportunistic infection by
the CEC. This AE occurred in a 44-year-old subject in the brepocitinib 15mg-to-30mg sequence
with a history of diabetes, corticosteroid and immunosuppressant use, and negative InterferonGamma Release Assay tuberculosis test at screening. The subject traveled to a tuberculosisendemic region during the study and developed bone tuberculosis. Brepocitinib was interrupted
and was not restarted at the time of the data cut for the 90 DSU.
Thromboembolic Events
Table 52 presents the assessment of thromboembolic AESI during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301, with relatedness determined by the investigator.
No thromboembolic AESIs were reported in either brepocitinib arm during the double-blind
treatment period. In the placebo arm, 1 (1.3%) subject experienced a thromboembolic AESI of
pulmonary embolism, which was assessed as moderate in severity, nonserious, and led to
treatment discontinuation.
During the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as reported in the 90 DSU, one thromboembolic
AESI of peripheral artery thrombosis was reported in a single subject (0.5%) in the brepocitinib
15 mg-to-30 mg treatment sequence; this event was mild in severity, met adjudication criteria for
a thromboembolic event, and occurred in a 41-year-old subject with pre-existing risk factors for
thrombosis, including hyperlipidemia and overweight BMI. The event resolved following
thrombectomy and treatment with anticoagulant therapy, and brepocitinib treatment was
discontinued.
In summary, thromboembolic AESIs were rare across both the double-blind treatment period and
the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301. No thromboembolic AESIs were observed in either
brepocitinib arm during the double-blind treatment period, with the only thromboembolic event
occurring in the placebo arm. In the OLE Period, a single thromboembolic AESI of peripheral
artery thrombosis was reported in one subject in the brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg sequence,
occurring in the context of pre-existing thromboembolic risk factors. Taken together, the totality
of thromboembolic safety data from Trial PVT-2201-301 supports the adequacy of the current
labeling to convey the thromboembolic risk associated with brepocitinib treatment, which
includes the Boxed Warning for thrombosis (i.e., pulmonary embolism and venous and arterial
thrombosis) that is applied across approved JAK inhibitors in rheumatologic indications.
