Viral Reactivation
Table 53 presents the assessment of viral reactivation AESI during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-30, with relatedness determined by the investigator.
Viral reactivation AESIs were reported in 4 (5.1%) placebo subjects, 2 (2.5%) brepocitinib
15 mg subjects, and 4 (4.9%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, with risk differences of −2.6% (95%
CI: −10.2, 4.2) and −0.1% (95% CI: −8.1, 7.7) for brepocitinib 15 mg and 30 mg versus placebo,
respectively. Herpes zoster was the predominant preferred term across all arms, reported in 3
(3.8%) placebo subjects, 1 (1.2%) brepocitinib 15 mg subject, and 3 (3.7%) brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects. Additional preferred terms included herpes simplex and oral herpes in the brepocitinib
15 mg arm, herpes zoster disseminated in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, and hepatitis B in the
placebo arm.
The majority of viral reactivation events were mild to moderate in severity across all arms. One
event in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm was assessed as severe and serious, representing the only
serious viral reactivation AESI across all treatment arms. No deaths due to viral reactivation
were reported in any arm during the double-blind treatment period, and no viral reactivation
events led to treatment discontinuation in any arm.
During the OLE Period of Trial PVT-2201-301, as reported in the 90-Day Safety Update, viral
reactivation was the most frequently reported AESI category, with 13 total viral reactivation
AESIs reported in 11 subjects (5.9%) across all treatment sequences, including 4 subjects (7.3%)
in the placebo-to-brepocitinib 30 mg sequence, 4 subjects (6.5%) in the brepocitinib 15 mg-to30 mg sequence, and 3 subjects (4.4%) in the brepocitinib 30 mg-to-30 mg sequence; the
majority of events were herpes zoster (10 of 13 events), with additional events of ophthalmic
herpes zoster, varicella, and one mild event of low-level hepatitis B (HBV) DNA detectability in
a subject with prior resolved HBV infection, which was considered consistent with occult HBV
infection rather than clinically meaningful HBV reactivation. The majority of viral reactivation
events were mild or moderate in severity, with two SAEs of herpes zoster both resolving without
sequelae, and the majority were assessed as related to brepocitinib by the Investigator. EAIRs for
viral reactivation AESIs were similar across treatment sequences (7.8, 6.6, and 4.5 per 100
patient-years for the placebo-to-30 mg, brepocitinib 15 mg-to-30 mg, and brepocitinib 30 mg-to30 mg sequences, respectively), with overlapping confidence intervals suggesting no meaningful
difference in viral reactivation risk between treatment groups.
In summary, viral reactivation AESIs were infrequent and occurred at comparable rates across all
treatment arms during the double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301, with no notable
differences identified between either brepocitinib arm and placebo, and no viral reactivation
events leading to treatment discontinuation or death during this period. In the OLE Period, viral
reactivation remained the most frequently reported AESI category, with herpes zoster being the
predominant event across both study periods, consistent with the known class effect of JAK
inhibitors and the immunosuppressed nature of the underlying dermatomyositis population; the
majority of events were mild or moderate in severity, resolved without sequelae, and EAIRs for
viral reactivation AESIs were low and similar across all treatment sequences, suggesting no
meaningful difference in viral reactivation risk between treatment groups. The current labeling is
considered adequate to convey the risk of viral reactivation associated with brepocitinib
treatment.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Safety Pool S1,
Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Pooled Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT2201-301 and Study B7931028
Table 54 presents an overview of TEAEs from the pooled Safety Pool S1 analysis, which
integrates data from the pivotal dermatomyositis trial (PVT-2201-301) and the phase 2 systemic
lupus erythematosus study (B7931028) across four treatment arms: placebo (N=179),
brepocitinib 15 mg (N=131), brepocitinib 30 mg (N=182), and brepocitinib 45 mg (N=99), with
a total brepocitinib arm of N=412. Treatment-emergent AEs are defined as any AE that begins or
worsens in severity on or after the first dose of study treatment up to 28 days after the last dose,
coded using Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) Version 27.0 over a 52week duration.
Any TEAE was reported in 84.9% of placebo subjects, 82.4% of brepocitinib 15 mg subjects,
88.5% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 83.8% of brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, with a total
brepocitinib incidence of 85.4%. By severity, severe and worse TEAEs were reported in
7.8%, 6.9%, 10.4%, and 10.1% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, brepocitinib 30 mg, and
brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, respectively. Moderate TEAEs were reported in 34.6%, 39.7%,
37.4%, and 33.3% of subjects across the same arms, and mild TEAEs in 42.5%, 35.9%,
40.7%, and 40.4%, respectively.
Any SAE was reported in 18 (10.1%) placebo subjects, 11 (8.4%) brepocitinib 15 mg
subjects, 21 (11.5%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 9 (9.1%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects,
with a total brepocitinib SAE incidence of 41 (10.0%). Two deaths were reported in the total
brepocitinib arm, 1 (0.5%) in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm and 1 (1.0%) in the brepocitinib
45 mg arm, compared to zero deaths in the placebo arm.
AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of treatment were reported in 21 (11.7%) placebo
subjects, 13 (9.9%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 17 (9.3%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and
19 (19.2%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, with a total brepocitinib discontinuation rate of 49
(11.9%). Note that the 45 mg dose is not being proposed for the population of
dermatomyositis in this application.
AEs leading to interruption of treatment were reported in 45 (25.1%) placebo subjects, 41
(31.3%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 49 (26.9%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 28 (28.3%)
brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, with a total brepocitinib interruption rate of 118 (28.6%). The
proportion of subjects for whom the dose was not changed was 71.5%, 75.6%, 78.0%, and
70.7% across placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, brepocitinib 30 mg, and brepocitinib 45 mg arms,
respectively.
In summary, the overall TEAE profile across Pool S1 was generally comparable between the
total brepocitinib arm and placebo, with similar rates of any TEAE, SAEs, and treatment
discontinuations at the doses relevant to this application (brepocitinib 15 mg and 30 mg). The
two deaths reported in the total brepocitinib arm, and the higher discontinuation rate observed
in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm, a dose not proposed for approval, are noted but do not alter the
150

overall safety assessment for the proposed 30-mg dose. These pooled findings are consistent
with and supportive of the safety profile observed in the pivotal Trial PVT-2201-301.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT2201-301 and Study B7931028
Table 55 presents individual subject-level data for all deaths that occurred during the 52-week
double-blind placebo-controlled treatment period across Pool S1. Two deaths were reported in
the total brepocitinib arm during this period, both occurring in Study B7931028 (SLE); no deaths
occurred in the placebo arm or in Trial PVT-2201-301 during the blinded treatment period.
5. Brepocitinib 30 mg (Study B7931028): A 56-year-old female subject randomized to
brepocitinib 30 mg received 197 days of treatment. The fatal event, coded as cardiac failure
acute with the verbatim term “acute cardiovascular failure,” occurred on Study Day 205,
approximately 8 days after the last dose of brepocitinib. The subject had a history of SLE,
vascular encephalopathy, and baseline ECG abnormalities. The event was assessed as not
related to brepocitinib by both the Investigator and the Applicant, and it met adjudication
criteria for cardiovascular death under the MACE definition. Notably, the subject was not
hospitalized at the time of the event.
6. Brepocitinib 45 mg (Study B7931028): A 28-year-old male subject randomized to
brepocitinib 45 mg received 258 days of treatment. The fatal event, coded as COVID-19 with
the verbatim term “COVID-19 severe,” occurred on Study Day 287, approximately 29 days
after the last dose of brepocitinib. The Investigator and the Applicant assessed this event as
not related to brepocitinib. This death occurred in the context of a serious COVID-19
infection and is consistent with the known infectious risks associated with
immunosuppressive therapies in the underlying SLE population.
Of note, the brepocitinib 45-mg dose is not included in this application.
In summary, the two deaths reported in Table 55 occurred in Study B7931028 (SLE) in subjects
receiving brepocitinib doses of 30 mg and 45 mg, respectively, and were assessed as not related
to brepocitinib by both the Investigator and the Applicant. No deaths occurred in the placebo arm
or in the pivotal dermatomyositis trial during the blinded treatment period, and the overall
mortality data from Pool S1 do not suggest a meaningful increased risk of fatal events
attributable to brepocitinib 30 mg.

7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled
Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and
Study B7931028
Table 56 presents SAEs by SOC and preferred term (PT) across Pool S1 during the 52-week
double-blind placebo-controlled treatment period.
Overall, SAEs were reported in 18 (10.1%) placebo subjects, 11 (8.4%) brepocitinib 15 mg
subjects, 21 (11.5%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 9 (9.1%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, for a
total brepocitinib incidence of 41 (10.0%).
The Infections and Infestations SOC had the greatest number of SAEs in the brepocitinib 30 mg
group and represented the most clinically notable finding in Table 56. SAEs in this SOC were
reported in 10 (5.5%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects compared to 3 (1.7%) placebo subjects, 3
(2.3%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 3 (3.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, for a total
brepocitinib incidence of 16 (3.9%). The most frequently reported preferred terms in the
brepocitinib 30 mg group were pneumonia (2 [1.1%]) and sepsis (2 [1.1%]), each occurring in
two subjects. Additional serious infection preferred terms reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg
group, each occurring in 1 (0.5%) subject, included varicella zoster pneumonia, respiratory tract
infection viral, pyelonephritis, pneumonia pseudomonal, pneumonia parainfluenzae viral,
peritonitis, osteomyelitis, oral infection, klebsiella urinary tract infection, influenza, herpes
zoster disseminated, COVID-19, appendicitis, arthritis bacterial, and tooth infection. In the
placebo arm, serious infections included periorbital cellulitis and upper respiratory tract infection
(one subject each). In the brepocitinib 15 mg arm, serious infections included pneumonia (two
subjects) and urinary tract infection (one subject). In the brepocitinib 45 mg arm, serious
infections included bacteraemia and COVID-19 pneumonia (one subject each).
Across the Musculoskeletal and Connective Tissue Disorders, Nervous System Disorders,
Gastrointestinal Disorders, Immune System Disorders, Neoplasms Benign Malignant and
Unspecified, Cardiac Disorders, Blood and Lymphatic System Disorders, Metabolism and
Nutrition Disorders, General Disorders and Administration Site Conditions, Hepatobiliary
Disorders, Injury Poisoning and Procedural Complications, Renal and Urinary Disorders,
Reproductive System and Breast Disorders, Respiratory Thoracic and Mediastinal Disorders,
Endocrine Disorders, and Skin and Subcutaneous Tissue Disorders SOCs, SAEs occurred at low
and variable frequencies across treatment arms and did not demonstrate a notable or consistent
dose-dependent increase with brepocitinib treatment.
In summary, the most clinically significant finding from Table 56 is the numerically higher
incidence of serious infections in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (5.5%) compared to placebo
(1.7%), driven predominantly by a diverse range of bacterial and viral infections, with
pneumonia and sepsis being the most frequently reported. The ISS confirms that the exposureadjusted incidence rate for serious infections was 5.7 per 100 patient-years for brepocitinib
30 mg compared to 1.7 per 100 patient-years for placebo, with the weighted risk difference for
serious infections in Pool S1 being 4.4% (95% CI: −3.3%, 12.1%), a finding is consistent with
the known class effect of JAK inhibitors on infection risk. SAEs in all other SOCs were
generally infrequent and comparable between treatment arms, with no other SOC demonstrating
a clinically meaningful or consistent dose-dependent increase in serious events with brepocitinib.

7.6.2.4. Adverse Events and OND Custom Medical Queries
Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled
Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and
Study B7931028
Table 57 presents AEs leading to permanent treatment discontinuation by SOC and PT across
Pool S1.
Overall, AEs leading to treatment discontinuation occurred in 21 (11.7%) placebo subjects, 13
(9.9%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 17 (9.3%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 19 (19.2%)
brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, with a total brepocitinib incidence of 49 (11.9%).
The most frequently represented SOCs contributing to discontinuations across all arms were
Infections and Infestations, Gastrointestinal Disorders, Investigations, Musculoskeletal and
Connective Tissue Disorders, and Nervous System Disorders. Within Infections and Infestations,
discontinuations occurred in 3 (1.7%) placebo subjects, 3 (2.3%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 2
(1.1%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 5 (5.1%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, with COVID-19
being the most frequently reported preferred term across arms. Within Gastrointestinal
Disorders, discontinuations were more frequent in the brepocitinib arms, 3 (2.3%) for
brepocitinib 15 mg and 2 (1.1%) for brepocitinib 30 mg, compared to 0 in the placebo arm,
driven by abdominal pain, nausea, diarrhoea, and gastrointestinal pain. Within Investigations,
discontinuations occurred across all arms, with hepatic enzyme increased and weight decreased
in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm and blood creatinine increased in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm.
Within Musculoskeletal and Connective Tissue Disorders, systemic lupus erythematosus was the
predominant preferred term, occurring in 1 (0.6%) placebo subject, 1 (0.8%) brepocitinib 15 mg
subject, 2 (1.1%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 3 (3.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects. Within
Nervous System Disorders, cerebrovascular accident led to discontinuation in 1 (0.5%)
brepocitinib 30 mg subject, while thalamic stroke led to discontinuation in 1 (0.8%) brepocitinib
15 mg subject. Additional SOCs contributing to discontinuations included General Disorders and
Administration Site Conditions, Immune System Disorders, Neoplasms, Pregnancy-related
conditions, Respiratory Disorders, Blood and Lymphatic System Disorders, Cardiac Disorders,
Psychiatric Disorders, Renal and Urinary Disorders, Hepatobiliary Disorders, Skin and
Subcutaneous Tissue Disorders, and Vascular Disorders, each contributing small numbers of
events across arms.
Overall, there was a numerically lower discontinuation rate in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm
compared to placebo but a higher discontinuation rate observed in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm,
driven in part by infections and musculoskeletal events.

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled
Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and
Study B7931028
Table 58 presents TEAEs occurring at a frequency of at least 3.5% in any treatment arm during
the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Pool S1.
The overall incidence of any TEAE was comparable across arms, ranging from 82.4% to 88.5%.
The most clinically significant finding was headache, which occurred in a higher proportion of
brepocitinib 30 mg subjects (15.9%) compared to placebo (3.9%), brepocitinib 15 mg (6.1%),
and brepocitinib 45 mg (9.1%), representing a dose-dependent increase at the 30-mg dose level.
Fatigue similarly showed an increase in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (8.8%) relative to placebo
(1.1%) and both other brepocitinib dose groups (3.1% and 2.0% for 15 mg and 45 mg,
respectively). Nausea was most prominent in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm (11.5%) compared to
brepocitinib 30 mg (6.6%) and placebo (4.5%), consistent with findings from the pivotal trial
analysis.
Acne showed a dose-dependent increase across all brepocitinib arms, 3.1%, 3.3%, and 4.0% for
brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg, respectively, compared to 0% in the placebo arm,
consistent with a likely class-related dermatologic effect of JAK inhibition. Similarly, oral
candidiasis demonstrated a dose-dependent pattern, occurring in 0% of brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects but 2.3% of brepocitinib 15 mg subjects and 6.1% of brepocitinib 45 mg subjects,
compared to 0.6% with placebo. Pyrexia was lower in both brepocitinib arms (3.8% for 30 mg
and 0.8% for 15 mg) compared to placebo (4.5%), and lymphopenia was also lower across all
brepocitinib arms (1.5% to 2.2%) relative to placebo (3.9%), likely reflecting the high
background rate of these findings in the underlying autoimmune disease population.
Several commonly reported preferred terms were broadly comparable across treatment arms and
did not suggest a meaningful brepocitinib-related signal. These included upper respiratory tract
infection, urinary tract infection, COVID-19, nasopharyngitis, diarrhoea, herpes zoster,
arthralgia, back pain, anaemia, dizziness, vomiting, and blood creatine phosphokinase increased.
Systemic lupus erythematosus was reported across arms at low frequencies, reflecting the SLE
population included in Pool S1 from Study B7931028.
The review team also analyzed adverse events categorized by Organ System and OND Custom
Medical Query (Narrow). The most frequently affected organ system, Infections and infestations,
showed overall balanced rates between brepocitinib and placebo for viral infections (23.2% to
26.7% versus 25.1%) and nasopharyngitis (20.2% to 22.5% versus 23.5%). However, fungal
infections were higher across all brepocitinib groups (3.3% to 10.1%) compared to placebo
(1.1%), supporting the observed preferred term-level increases in oral candidiasis. The grouped
Organ System analysis corroborates the finding of higher rates of headaches in the brepocitinib
30 mg arm versus placebo (18.1% versus 6.7%), fatigue in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm versus
placebo (9.9% versus 1.7%), and nausea in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm (11.5% versus 4.5%).
There were no significant imbalances across other organ systems, such as cardiovascular,
respiratory, and musculoskeletal disorders.
In summary, the most clinically notable findings from Table 58 are the higher frequencies of
headache and fatigue with brepocitinib 30 mg, nausea with brepocitinib 15 mg, and dose161
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
dependent increases in acne and oral candidiasis across brepocitinib arms, all of which are
consistent with the known pharmacological class effects of JAK inhibitors. The majority of other
commonly reported preferred terms were balanced across treatment arms and do not suggest
additional safety signals beyond those already identified in the pivotal trial and broader
integrated analyses.

7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Safety Pool
S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028
Table 59 presents the proportions of subjects with general chemistry analyte values exceeding
pre-specified threshold levels during the 52-week double-blind treatment period of Pool S1.
Threshold Levels 1, 2, and 3 are defined per the Standard Safety Tables and Figures Integrated
Guide (FDA 2025).
Low bicarbonate at Level 1 (<20 mEq/L) was reported in 38.2% of placebo subjects, 25.2% of
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 37.9% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 70.7% of brepocitinib
45 mg subjects. At Level 2 (<18 mEq/L), rates were 12.4% for placebo, 9.9% for brepocitinib
15 mg, 16.5% for brepocitinib 30 mg, and 35.4% for brepocitinib 45 mg. At Level 3 (<15
mEq/L), rates were 1.7% for placebo, 0% for brepocitinib 15 mg, 0.5% for brepocitinib 30 mg,
and 3.0% for brepocitinib 45 mg. The most notable low bicarbonate finding is in the brepocitinib
45 mg arm, which was studied only in SLE subjects in Study B7931028. The review team also
analyzed mean baseline bicarbonate levels in Pool S1 and found that the brepocitinib 45 mg
group had a lower mean baseline compared to the other arms: brepocitinib 45 mg (22.7 mEq/L),
placebo (24.5 mEq/L), brepocitinib 15 mg (24.9 mEq/L), and brepocitinib 30 mg (24.4 mEq/L).
At Week 52, the mean change from baseline was −0.4 mEq/L (95% CI: −0.9, 0.0) for placebo,
−0.4 mEq/L (95% CI: −0.9, 0.0) for brepocitinib 15 mg, −0.4 mEq/L (95% CI: −0.8, −0.0) for
brepocitinib 30 mg, and −0.7 mEq/L (95% CI: −1.2, −0.2) for brepocitinib 45 mg, with a total
brepocitinib mean change of −0.5 mEq/L (95% CI: −0.7, −0.2). The magnitude of change was
small and broadly comparable across all arms, with no clinically meaningful differences between
any brepocitinib dose and placebo at Week 52. The observed bicarbonate decreases at the labeled
30-mg dose are modest and did not result in clinically significant metabolic acidosis in any
subject during the study. The findings do not appear to represent a safety issue at the proposed
labeled dose of 30 mg based on the available data.
Low glucose was also a notable finding. At Level 1 (<70 mg/dL), low glucose was reported in
27.3% of brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, 17.0% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, 24.4% of
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 15.2% of placebo subjects. At Level 2 (<54 mg/dL), rates were
2.0%, 2.7%, 4.6%, and 0.6% for brepocitinib 45 mg, 30 mg, 15 mg, and placebo, respectively,
with the brepocitinib 15 mg arm showing the highest frequency at this threshold. See
Section 7.6.2.7 for further discussion on the hypoglycemia events in the clinical development
program.
CPK elevations were common across all arms, consistent with the underlying dermatomyositis
disease population. At Level 1 (>3× ULN), CPK elevations were reported in 14.1% of
brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, 19.2% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, 18.3% of brepocitinib 15 mg
subjects, and 15.2% of placebo subjects. At Level 3 (>10× ULN), rates were 3.0%, 4.4%, 3.8%,
and 6.2% for brepocitinib 45 mg, 30 mg, 15 mg, and placebo, respectively, with the placebo arm
showing the highest rate at this threshold, further reflecting the association of CK elevation with
active dermatomyositis disease rather than drug effect.
Creatinine elevations showed a dose-dependent increase with brepocitinib, consistent with the
proposed pharmacological effect of brepocitinib on organic cation transporters. At Level 1
(≥1.5× baseline), creatinine elevations were reported in 22.2% of brepocitinib 45 mg subjects,
26.9% of brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, 18.3% of brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 3.4% of
165
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
placebo subjects. At Level 2 (≥2× baseline), rates were 2.0%, 4.9%, 0%, and 0.6% for
brepocitinib 45 mg, 30 mg, 15 mg, and placebo, respectively. Correspondingly, eGFR decreases
at Level 1 (≥25% decrease) were reported in 58.6% of brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, 50.0% of
brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, 35.9% of brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, and 9.0% of placebo
subjects. As proposed in the ISS, this creatinine and eGFR changes are likely based on
brepocitinib’s inhibition of organic cation transporters and reflect reduced tubular secretion of
creatinine rather than true glomerular filtration impairment, a conclusion supported by the
absence of corresponding decreases in cystatin C-based eGFR. Refer also to Kidney Function,
above, and the Clinical Pharmacology discussion of the effect of brepocitinib on creatinine in
Section 8.2.
Sodium, potassium, chloride, calcium, albumin, total protein, blood urea nitrogen, and liver
biochemistry analytes (i.e., ALT, AST, ALP, and total bilirubin) were generally comparable
across all treatment arms at all threshold levels, with no clinically meaningful or dose-dependent
differences identified. No potential Hy’s Law cases were identified in any treatment arm,
providing reassurance regarding the hepatic safety profile of brepocitinib across the Pool S1
population (refer to Section 7.6.2.8 for further discussion of the Assessment of DILI in Pool S1).

Glucose values for hyperglycemia do not follow a nested format like the other labs. Level 1 corresponds to the diagnosis of
prediabetes and is not inclusive of Level 2 and 3. Level 2 corresponds to the diagnosis of diabetes. Level 3 represents significant
hyperglycemia that may indicate need for insulin or increased risk for diabetic ketoacidosis or other complications
Abbreviations: CPK, creatine phosphokinase; ISS, integrated summary of safety; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number
of subjects meeting the specified laboratory criteria; NA, not applicable; Nw, total number of subjects with data available for the
laboratory test of interest; ULN, upper limit of normal

Kidney Function
Table 60 reveals a dose-dependent effect of brepocitinib on serum creatinine and eGFR
parameters across the 52-week double-blind treatment period. Creatinine elevations at Level 1
(≥1.5× baseline) were higher in all brepocitinib arms compared to placebo, occurring in 18.3%,
26.9%, and 22.2% of subjects in the brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg arms, respectively,
versus only 3.4% in the placebo arm. At Level 2 (≥2× baseline), elevations were most
pronounced in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (4.9%) compared to 0.6% in placebo, 0% in the 15 mg
arm, and 2.0% in the 45 mg arm, with no subjects in any arm meeting the Level 3 threshold (≥3×
baseline).
eGFR decreases at Level 1 (≥25% decrease) demonstrated a similar dose-dependent pattern,
occurring in 35.9%, 50.0%, and 58.6% of subjects in the brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg
arms, respectively, compared to only 9.0% in the placebo arm. At Level 2 (≥50% decrease), rates
remained low across all arms (ranging from 0% to 3.8%), and only one placebo subject met the
Level 3 threshold (≥75% decrease). These creatinine and eGFR changes are most likely
attributed to brepocitinib’s inhibition of organic cation transporters, which reduces tubular
secretion of creatinine rather than reflecting true glomerular filtration impairment, given the
absence of corresponding reductions in cystatin C-based eGFR. Refer to further Clinical
Pharmacology discussion of the effect of brepocitinib on creatinine in Section 8.2.
The United States Prescribing Information (USPI) states that brepocitinib is not recommended in
patients with severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²), which is reasonable as the
pharmacokinetic and safety profile of brepocitinib in this population has not been adequately
characterized.

Lipids
Table 61 presents lipid analyte values exceeding pre-specified threshold levels across the four
treatment arms in Pool S1. Total cholesterol elevations were common across all arms, with Level
1 elevations (>200 mg/dL) occurring in 55.9%, 66.2%, 63.2%, and 65.7% of placebo,
brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg subjects, respectively, and numerically higher rates in all
brepocitinib arms compared to placebo at Levels 2 and 3. The overall prevalence across all arms
likely reflects the underlying disease population and background immunosuppressive therapies.
Among female subjects, there were numerically lower rates of low HDL cholesterol at Level 2
(<40 mg/dL) in all brepocitinib arms compared to placebo. LDL elevations were similarly
prevalent across all arms, with Level 1 elevations (>130 mg/dL) occurring in 48.0%, 52.3%,
58.2%, and 46.5% across the four arms (placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg,
respectively), with no consistent dose-dependent pattern identified. Triglyceride elevations were
highly prevalent across all arms at Level 1 (>150 mg/dL), occurring in 54.2%, 54.6%, 56.0%,
and 33.3% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg subjects, respectively, with rates
numerically lower in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm compared to the other arms. Overall, the lipid
findings from Pool S1 did not identify a meaningful adverse lipid effect associated with
brepocitinib treatment, and the high prevalence of lipid abnormalities across all arms is
consistent with the underlying autoimmune disease population and concomitant use of
background corticosteroids and immunosuppressive therapies. Nevertheless, consistent with
labeling for other JAK inhibitors, the proposed brepocitinib USPI also includes monitoring of
lipids under Warnings and Precautions.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Hematology
Table 62 presents hematology analyte values exceeding pre-specified threshold levels across the
four treatment arms in Pool S1. Hemoglobin decreases were notably more frequent in the
brepocitinib arms compared to placebo, with Level 2 decreases (>1.5 g/dL decrease from
baseline) occurring in 23.0%, 20.6%, 34.1%, and 35.4% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg,
and 45 mg subjects, respectively, and Level 3 decreases (>2 g/dL decrease from baseline)
occurring in 11.2%, 10.7%, 20.9%, and 19.2% across the same arms, suggesting a dosedependent effect on hemoglobin at the higher brepocitinib doses. Lymphopenia was highly
prevalent across all arms, consistent with the underlying autoimmune disease population and
background immunosuppressive therapies, with Level 1 low lymphocyte counts (<1 × 10³
cells/uL) occurring in 58.4%, 55.7%, 57.7%, and 63.6% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg,
and 45 mg subjects, respectively, with no meaningful dose-dependent differences identified
across threshold levels. Low neutrophil counts at Level 1 (<2 × 10³ cells/uL) were numerically
higher in the brepocitinib arms, occurring in 26.4%, 26.0%, 36.8%, and 48.5% across the four
arms, with a dose-dependent pattern most pronounced in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm, though
Grade 3 neutropenia (Level 2, <1 × 10³ cells/uL) remained uncommon across all arms at 2.8%,
3.1%, 6.6%, and 6.1%, respectively. White blood cell counts, platelet counts, and eosinophil
counts were generally comparable across all treatment arms at all threshold levels, with no
clinically meaningful or dose-dependent differences identified.
The most clinically notable hematology findings in Pool S1 were the dose-dependent increases in
hemoglobin decreases and low neutrophil counts in the higher brepocitinib dose arms. There was
a high prevalence of lymphopenia across all arms which may be attributable to the underlying
disease population and concomitant immunosuppressive therapy. Overall, these findings are in
line with the known hematologic effects of JAK inhibitors; accordingly, the proposed
brepocitinib USPI includes recommendations under Warnings and Precautions to monitor
hemoglobin, lymphocyte, and neutrophil counts due to the potential for clinically meaningful
changes.

7.6.2.7. Assessment of Hypoglycemia, Pooled Analyses, Safety
Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028
In March 2026, FDA issued a class-wide labeling mandate requiring drug manufactures to update labels
for all oral JAK inhibitor medications used for autoimmune diseases to include safety information about
hypoglycemia risk in diabetic patients.
In light of this, the brepocitinib label was adjusted to incorporate relevant language from the Safety
Labeling Change (refer to Labeling in Section 16).
The review team described laboratory observations of hypoglycemia in Sections 7.6.1.6 and 7.6.2.6 and
this section will summarize the Applicant interactions that were needed to collect further data to
describe the potential hypoglycemia signal within the brepocitinib clinical development program.
The Division issued three substantive rounds of IRs pertaining to hypoglycemia:
1. On May 27, 2026 an IR was communicated for baseline data collection and standardization of the
data to a MedDRA-coded summary of hypoglycemia and blood glucose decrease TEAEs across the
brepocitinib clinical development program. In response, the Applicant reported only one
hypoglycemia TEAEs.
2. Given the discrepancy between the Applicant’s provided data on hypoglycemia and the Division’s
findings of higher rates of hypoglycemia (see Sections 7.6.1.6 and 7.6.2.6), a second IR was
communicated on June 17, 2026. The Division inquired as to why were lab-based hypoglycemia
events not flagged as TEAEs, and requested the Applicant to describe the process by which
laboratory abnormalities were evaluated for TEAE reporting and provide complete subject-level
listings of all participants meeting hypoglycemia thresholds (Level 1, 2, 3). The Applicant explained
that laboratory values were reviewed by site investigators in a clinical context. Most were not
deemed clinically significant due to: isolated/sporadic/self-resolving nature; laboratory normal range
(60 mg/dL); absence of diabetes or antidiabetic medications; lack of hypoglycemia symptoms;
concomitant medications affecting glucose; fasting status; and pre-analytical artifacts. The Applicant
provided data and subject-level listings; however, there were still discrepancies between the
provided data and the data generated by the review team.
3. In the third IR communicated on July 13, 2026, the Division requested the Applicant recalculate
hypoglycemia using American Diabetes Association (ADA) 2024 definitions (Level 1: <70 and ≥54
mg/dL; Level 2: <54 mg/dL; Level 3: severe event with altered mental/physical function); provide
SAS programs for reproducibility; submit case narratives for all Level 3 events; fill standardized
summary tables by level, treatment, and diabetes status for S1, S2, S3. These data more accurately
reflected the review teams data regarding hypoglycemia and will be described below.
Key Findings From the ADA-Standardized Analysis
Table 43, Table 44, and Table 45 describe the exposure adjusted incidence rates (EAIRs) for Levels 1, 2,
and 3 hypoglycemia for Pools S1, S2, and S3. EAIRs for Level 1 hypoglycemia (<70 and ≥54 mg/dL)
were higher in brepocitinib treated subjects compared to placebo across all three safety pools. EAIRs for
Level 2 hypoglycemia (<54 mg/dL) were also higher in brepocitinib-treated subjects compared to
placebo across the safety pools. Finally, using the ADA-definition of Level 3 hypoglycemia, there were
no cases of Level 3 hypoglycemia across the clinical development program.

Review of the data demonstrates that Level 1 events were more common but were largely asymptomatic
and mild. The diabetic subject sample size is small; however, the data suggests that diabetic subjects
may have a higher incidence of Level 2 hypoglycemia.
The SLC language has been incorporated into the brepocitinib USPI with minor modifications to ensure
accuracy with the available data (see Section 16).

7.6.2.8. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled
Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and
Study B7931028
Liver Biochemistry
Table 66 presents liver biochemistry analyte values exceeding pre-specified threshold levels
across the four treatment arms in Pool S1. As discussed for Study PVT-2201-301 in
Section 7.6.1.6, above, an important interpretive consideration for all liver biochemistry findings
in Pool S1 is the underlying disease population. Both DM and SLE are associated with ongoing
muscle and systemic inflammation that can result in shedding of ALT and AST from skeletal
muscle, independent of hepatic injury. As discussed further below, this creates a challenge for
the detection of drug-induced liver injury in this population, particularly in the dermatomyositis
subgroup.
Liver Biochemistry Analytes
Alkaline phosphatase (ALP) elevations were infrequent across all arms, with Level 1 elevations
(>1.5× ULN) occurring in 2.8%, 1.5%, 3.3%, and 2.0% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg,
and 45 mg subjects, respectively. ALT elevations at Level 1 (>3× ULN) were reported in 4.5%,
4.6%, 7.1%, and 7.1% across the same arms, with Level 3 elevations (>10× ULN) occurring only
in one subject (0.8%) in the brepocitinib 15 mg arm and one subject (0.5%) in the brepocitinib
30 mg arm. AST elevations at Level 1 were reported in 1.7%, 4.6%, 2.7%, and 5.1% across
arms, with Level 3 elevations occurring only in one subject (0.5%) in the brepocitinib 30 mg

178
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
arm. Total bilirubin elevations were low across all arms, with no subjects in any arm meeting the
Level 3 threshold (>3× ULN) in the brepocitinib arms.

Liver Injury Screening Analyses
Table 67, and Figure 19 present the results of a hepatocellular DILI screening analysis for Pool
S1. No potential Hy’s Law cases were identified in any treatment arm, with 0% of subjects in the
placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg arms meeting the combined criterion of
postbaseline total bilirubin ≥2× ULN within 30 days of a postbaseline ALT or AST ≥3× ULN.
Cholestasis cases (left upper quadrant, defined as bilirubin ≥2× ULN and ALP <2× ULN) were
reported in two subjects each in the placebo (1.1%) and brepocitinib 15 mg (1.5%) and 30 mg
(1.1%) arms, with none in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm. Temple’s corollary cases, defined as
transaminase elevations ≥3× ULN without concurrent bilirubin elevation, were reported in 4.5%,
4.6%, 8.8%, and 9.1% of placebo, brepocitinib 15 mg, 30 mg, and 45 mg subjects, respectively,
with the numerically higher rate in the brepocitinib 30 mg and 45 mg arms reflecting
transaminase elevations in the absence of bilirubin elevation. The total proportion of subjects
meeting any quadrant of interest criterion was 5.6%, 6.1%, 9.9%, and 9.1% across the four arms.

DHN DILI Team Consultation
Given the presence of subjects in the Temple’s Corollary and Cholestasis Quadrants on the
eDISH plot for Pool S1, the Division consulted the CDER DHN DILI Team to review the
hepatic safety data from Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028 (SLE). The DILI Team
confirmed that no cases met Hy’s Law criteria in either study. Of the subjects with
aminotransferase (AT) elevations >5× ULN across both studies, the DILI Team assessed eight
cases for likelihood of attribution to brepocitinib using U.S. DILIN causality scoring. Four cases
were considered unlikely DILI, with myopathy favored as the more likely explanation for AT
elevations based on concurrent CK elevations. One case was considered indeterminate due to
insufficient data. Of the remaining three cases, two were assessed as probable DILI due to
brepocitinib: one from Trial PVT-2201-301 (brepocitinib 15 mg arm) and one from Study
B7931028 (brepocitinib 45 mg arm) and one was assessed as possible DILI. All three cases were
asymptomatic, non-hyperbilirubinemic, and resolved completely; the two probable cases
resolved following brepocitinib discontinuation, and the possible case recovered without dose
interruption, suggesting adaptation if DILI did occur.
The DHN DILI Team’s review of Pool S1 extended the case-level analysis beyond Trial PVT2201-301 to include Study B7931028 (SLE). As described in Section 7.6.1.6, the DILI Team
assessed eight cases across both studies using DILIN causality scoring, identifying two cases of
probable DILI (one from each study) and one possible DILI case, all of which were
asymptomatic, non-hyperbilirubinemic, and resolved completely. The Pool S1 eDISH data are
183
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
consistent with the dose-response signal and monitoring challenges described in the
Section 7.6.1.7, with the numerically higher proportion of Temple’s corollary cases in the
brepocitinib 30 mg and 45 mg arms compared to placebo across Pool S1 further supporting the
DILI Team’s conclusion that a hepatotoxicity signal exists at the labeled dose. The DILI Team’s
recommendations regarding GGT monitoring, baseline liver blood test requirements, enhanced
early monitoring frequency apply to the full Pool S1 dataset and are discussed in detail in
Section 7.6.1.7 as well as in Section 16.

findings from Pool S1 do not suggest a clinically meaningful or consistent brepocitinib-related
effect on systolic blood pressure, and the distribution of values across categories was generally
comparable between the brepocitinib arms and placebo.

7.6.2.10. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Pooled
Analyses, Safety Pool S1, Trial PVT-2201-301 and
Study B7931028
This section provides an overview of the following AESI based on class-related concerns:
•

Cardiovascular events

•

Thromboembolic events

•

Viral reactivation

•

New or recurrent diagnoses of malignancy

•

Serious infections

•

MACE

Liver enzyme AESI are discussed separately, in Section 7.6.2.7.
Across all AESI categories assessed in Pool S1, the most clinically significant finding was the
higher incidence of serious infections in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (5.5%) compared to placebo
(1.7%), which represents the primary safety signal identified in the integrated safety analysis and
is consistent with the known class effect of JAK inhibitors on infection risk. Viral reactivation
events, predominantly herpes zoster, occurred at comparable rates between the brepocitinib
30 mg and placebo arms, with a possible dose-dependent trend at the 45-mg dose. MACE AESIs
were rare and occurred only in the brepocitinib 15 mg and 30 mg arms, with no events in the
placebo arm; the small number of events and the presence of pre-existing cardiovascular risk
factors in affected subjects limit the ability to attribute these events specifically to brepocitinib.
Thromboembolic AESIs occurred at a numerically higher rate in the placebo arm than in the total
brepocitinib arm, and no thromboembolic events were reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg or
45 mg arms. Malignancy AESIs were rare and comparable between the total brepocitinib arm
and placebo, with no malignancy events in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm during the 52-week
double-blind treatment period. Cardiovascular AESIs were limited to a single fatal event of
cardiac failure acute in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, with no events in the placebo arm. Taken
185
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
together, the AESI data from Pool S1 support the conclusion that the overall benefit-risk profile
of brepocitinib 30 mg is favorable for the treatment of adults with dermatomyositis, with
identified risks that are manageable through appropriate labeling, patient selection, monitoring,
and pharmacovigilance, consistent with the known safety profile of the JAK inhibitor class.
Cardiovascular Events
This section reports on cardiovascular and MACE AESI during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Pool S1 (placebo N=179, brepocitinib 15 mg N=131, brepocitinib 30 mg
N=182, brepocitinib 45 mg N=99, total brepocitinib N=412). Cardiovascular events encompass
cardiac-related AESIs, while MACE represents the regulatory-relevant subset defined as
cardiovascular death, non-fatal myocardial infarction, and non-fatal stroke.
In the brepocitinib arms, 1 cardiovascular AESI was reported: a cardiac failure acute
occurring in 1 (0.5%) subject in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, which was fatal in severity,
serious, and led to treatment discontinuation. No cardiovascular AESIs were repo rted in the
brepocitinib 15 mg or 45 mg arms, and no cardiovascular AESIs were reported in the placebo
arm. The single cardiovascular AESI in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm resulted in death,
representing the only fatal cardiovascular event across all treatment arms in Pool S1. The
event was not assessed as related to study drug by the investigator and occurred in the context
of Study B7931028 (SLE) in a subject with pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors.
MACE AESIs were reported in 0 placebo subjects, 1 (0.8%) brepocitinib 15 mg subject, and 2
(1.1%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, for a total brepocitinib incidence of 3 (0.7%). No MACE
AESIs were reported in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm or the placebo arm. In the brepocitinib
15 mg arm, the single MACE event was a thalamic stroke, assessed as severe in severity,
serious, and leading to treatment discontinuation; the event was not assessed as related to
study drug by the investigator. In the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, MACE events included a
cerebrovascular accident in 1 (0.5%) subject, assessed as moderate in severity, serious,
leading to treatment discontinuation, and assessed as related to study drug by the investigator,
and the cardiac failure acute described above, which was fatal. One death due to MACE was
reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, with no deaths in the placebo arm.
These findings are consistent with the known class risk of cardiovascular events and MACE
with JAK inhibitors and support the inclusion of a Boxed Warning for MACE and increased
risk of mortality in the brepocitinib label, consistent with the class-wide labeling for approved
JAK inhibitors. The current labeling is considered adequate to convey the cardiovascular and
MACE risk associated with brepocitinib treatment, and ongoing pharmacovigilance is
warranted to further characterize the cardiovascular safety profile of brepocitinib in the
postmarket setting.
Malignancy Non-Melanoma Skin Cancer
One non-melanoma skin cancer (NMSC) AESI was reported in the placebo arm: 1 (0.6%)
subject with squamous cell carcinoma, which was assessed as moderate in severity, non-serious,
and did not lead to treatment discontinuation. No NMSC malignancy AESIs were reported in any
brepocitinib treatment arm during the 52-week double-blind treatment period.
In summary, NMSC malignancy AESIs were rare in Pool S1, with a single event occurring in the
placebo arm and no events in any brepocitinib arm. However, given the known class risk of
186
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
NMSC with JAK inhibitors and the limited size of the safety database, the current labeling is
considered adequate to convey the malignancy risk associated with brepocitinib treatment, and
ongoing standard pharmacovigilance is warranted.
Malignancy Excluding Non-Melanoma Skin Cancer
Table 70 presents the assessment of malignancy AESIs excluding NMSC during the 52-week
double-blind treatment period of Pool S1.
Malignancy AESIs excluding NMSC were reported in 1 (0.6%) placebo subject, 1 (0.8%)
brepocitinib 15 mg subject, 0 brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 1 (1.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg
subject, for a total brepocitinib incidence of 2 (0.5%). In the placebo arm, the event was
nasopharyngeal cancer metastatic, which was assessed as moderate in severity and serious, and
led to treatment discontinuation. In the brepocitinib 15 mg arm, the event was invasive breast
carcinoma, which was assessed as moderate in severity and serious, and led to treatment
discontinuation. In the brepocitinib 45 mg arm, the event was cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, which
was assessed as severe in severity and serious, and led to treatment discontinuation. No deaths
due to malignancy excluding NMSC were reported in any arm. None of the malignancy events
were assessed as related to study drug by the investigator.
In summary, malignancy AESIs excluding NMSC were rare across all treatment arms in Pool S1,
with comparable rates between the total brepocitinib arm (0.5%) and the placebo arm (0.6%). No
malignancy events were reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period. The small number of events and the comparable rates between brepocitinib and
placebo do not suggest a brepocitinib-attributable malignancy signal in Pool S1. However, given
the known class risk of malignancy with JAK inhibitors, the limited size of the safety database,
and the occurrence of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm, the current
labeling is considered adequate to convey the malignancy risk associated with brepocitinib
treatment, and ongoing pharmacovigilance is warranted.

Thromboembolic Events
Table 71 presents the assessment of thromboembolic AESIs during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Pool S1.
Thromboembolic AESIs were reported in 2 (1.1%) placebo subjects and 1 (0.8%) brepocitinib
15 mg subject, with no events in the brepocitinib 30 mg or 45 mg arms, for a total brepocitinib
incidence of 1 (0.2%). In the placebo arm, events included pulmonary embolism in 1 (0.6%)
subject and deep vein thrombosis in 1 (0.6%) subject, both assessed as moderate in severity and
leading to treatment discontinuation; one event was assessed as related to study drug by the
investigator. In the brepocitinib 15 mg arm, the single event was a thalamic stroke, assessed as
severe in severity, serious, and leading to treatment discontinuation; the event was not assessed
as related to study drug by the investigator. No deaths due to thromboembolic events were
reported in any arm.
In summary, thromboembolic AESIs were rare in Pool S1 and occurred at a numerically higher
rate in the placebo arm (1.1%) compared to the total brepocitinib arm (0.2%). No
thromboembolic events were reported in the brepocitinib 30 mg or 45 mg arms during the 52week double-blind treatment period. These findings are consistent with the findings from the
pivotal Trial PVT-2201-301. The current labeling is considered adequate to convey the
thromboembolic risk associated with brepocitinib treatment consistent with the known JAK
inhibitor class profile, including the Boxed Warning for thrombosis.

Viral Reactivation
Table 72 presents the assessment of viral reactivation AESIs during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Pool S1.
Viral reactivation AESIs were reported in 5 (2.8%) placebo subjects, 2 (1.5%) brepocitinib
15 mg subjects, 6 (3.3%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 5 (5.1%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects,
for a total brepocitinib incidence of 13 (3.2%). Herpes zoster was the predominant preferred term
across all arms, reported in 5 (2.8%) placebo subjects, 2 (1.5%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 5
(2.7%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 4 (4.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects. Additional
preferred terms in the brepocitinib arms included varicella zoster pneumonia and herpes zoster
disseminated in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (one subject each, 0.5%), and ophthalmic herpes
zoster in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm (one subject, 1.0%). The majority of viral reactivation
events were mild to moderate in severity across all arms. One event in the brepocitinib 30 mg
arm was assessed as severe and serious, representing the only serious viral reactivation AESI
across all brepocitinib arms. No deaths due to viral reactivation were reported in any arm, and no
viral reactivation events led to treatment discontinuation in any arm during the double-blind
periods. Investigator-assessed relatedness was reported in 4 (2.2%) placebo subjects, 2 (1.5%)
brepocitinib 15 mg subjects, 6 (3.3%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 4 (4.0%) brepocitinib
45 mg subjects.
In summary, viral reactivation AESIs, which were predominantly herpes zoster, were reported at
comparable rates across the placebo and brepocitinib 30 mg arms (2.8% versus 3.3%), with a
numerically higher rate in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm (5.1%), suggesting a possible dosedependent trend. Most events were mild to moderate in severity. The occurrence of varicella
zoster pneumonia and herpes zoster disseminated in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm highlights the
potential for more serious viral reactivation events, consistent with the known class effect of
JAK inhibitors on herpes zoster risk. The current labeling is considered adequate to convey the
viral reactivation risk associated with brepocitinib treatment.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Serious Infection
Table 73 presents the assessment of serious infection AESIs during the 52-week double-blind
treatment period of Pool S1.
Serious infection AESIs were reported in 3 (1.7%) placebo subjects, 3 (2.3%) brepocitinib 15 mg
subjects, 10 (5.5%) brepocitinib 30 mg subjects, and 3 (3.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, for a
total brepocitinib incidence of 16 (3.9%). The brepocitinib 30 mg arm had the highest incidence
of serious infections across all treatment arms, with a diverse range of preferred terms including
sepsis (two subjects, 1.1%), pneumonia (two subjects, 1.1%), varicella zoster pneumonia, tooth
infection, respiratory tract infection viral, pyelonephritis, pneumonia pseudomonal, pneumonia
parainfluenzae viral, peritonitis, osteomyelitis, oral infection, Klebsiella urinary tract infection,
influenza, herpes zoster disseminated, COVID-19, arthritis bacterial, and appendicitis (each in
one subject, 0.5%). One death due to serious infection was reported in the brepocitinib 45 mg
arm (COVID-19, 1.0%), also see Section 7.6.2.2, above, with no deaths in the placebo or
brepocitinib 30 mg arms. The majority of serious infections in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm were
severe in maximum severity (seven subjects, 3.8%), with three subjects (1.6%) experiencing
moderate events. Investigator-assessed relatedness was reported in 6 (3.3%) brepocitinib 30 mg
subjects compared to 1 (0.6%) placebo subject. Treatment discontinuation due to serious
infection occurred in 1 (0.5%) brepocitinib 30 mg subject, 2 (1.5%) brepocitinib 15 mg subjects,
3 (3.0%) brepocitinib 45 mg subjects, and 1 (0.6%) placebo subject.
In summary, serious infection AESIs were the most clinically significant AESI category in Pool
S1, with a higher incidence in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm (5.5%) compared to the placebo arm
(1.7%), representing the most prominent safety signal identified in the integrated safety analysis.
The diversity of serious infection preferred terms in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm, spanning
bacterial, viral, and fungal pathogens across multiple organ systems, is consistent with the broad
immunosuppressive effects of JAK inhibition and the known class risk of serious infections with
JAK inhibitors. The single death due to serious infection occurred in the brepocitinib 45 mg arm
and was attributed to COVID-19. These findings support the inclusion of a Boxed Warning for
serious infections in the brepocitinib label, consistent with the class-wide labeling for approved
JAK inhibitors, and underscore the importance of appropriate patient screening, monitoring, and
management of infections during brepocitinib treatment.

LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

7.6.3. Safety Results, Pool S2 and Pool S3
The exposure-adjusted incidence rate (EAIR) analyses for Safety Pools S2 and S3 provide
supportive characterization of the brepocitinib safety profile across a broader range of
indications, dose levels, and treatment durations than the pivotal Pool S1 analysis. Pool S2
includes 7 completed randomized studies with at least 8 weeks of double-blind, placebocontrolled exposure across doses ranging from 10 mg to 60 mg, while Pool S3 encompasses all
11 completed phase 2 and phase 3 clinical trials with oral brepocitinib, including non-placebocontrolled periods and open-label extension data, providing the greatest breadth of exposure
across the clinical development program. EAIRs are expressed as events per 100 patient-years
with 95% confidence intervals.
Overall TEAE Incidence
Across both Pool S2 and Pool S3, the overall EAIR for any TEAE was broadly comparable
between brepocitinib-treated subjects and placebo. In Pool S3, the EAIR for any TEAE was
257.1 per 100 patient-years (95% CI: 234.02, 282.41) for placebo, 268.7 (95% CI: 252.41,
285.94) for brepocitinib ≥15 mg, 272.6 (95% CI: 255.09, 291.33) for brepocitinib ≥30 mg, and
288.9 (95% CI: 263.99, 316.25) for brepocitinib ≥45 mg, with a total brepocitinib EAIR of 268.0
(95% CI: 251.87, 285.20). The on-study analysis in Pool S3 yielded similar results, with EAIRs
of 255.5 (95% CI: 232.58, 280.73) for placebo and 269.6 (95% CI: 252.30, 288.17) for
brepocitinib ≥30 mg. These findings are consistent with the Pool S2 EAIR data, which similarly
showed comparable overall TEAE rates between brepocitinib and placebo.
Severity of TEAEs
Across both pools, the distribution of TEAEs by severity was broadly similar between
brepocitinib and placebo, with mild and moderate events predominating in all treatment groups.
In Pool S3, EAIRs for mild TEAEs were comparable between placebo (63.1 per 100 patientyears; 95% CI: 55.12, 72.29) and brepocitinib ≥30 mg (66.7; 95% CI: 60.56, 73.48), with a
numerically higher rate in the brepocitinib ≥45 mg group (73.4; 95% CI: 64.47, 83.53). EAIRs
for moderate TEAEs were similarly comparable across groups, ranging from 54.0 for placebo to
59.7 for brepocitinib ≥45 mg. EAIRs for severe TEAEs were numerically higher in all
brepocitinib dose groups (ranging from 9.7 to 10.0 per 100 patient-years for brepocitinib ≥15 mg
and ≥30 mg, respectively) compared to placebo (7.8 per 100 patient-years); however, overall
these EAIRS are generally similar. The on-study analysis in Pool S3 yielded consistent results.
These severity findings are broadly consistent with the Pool S2 data and do not suggest a
clinically meaningful increase in severe TEAE rates with brepocitinib at the proposed labeled
dose of 30 mg.
Drug-Related TEAEs
EAIRs for drug-related TEAEs were numerically higher in brepocitinib-treated subjects
compared to placebo across both pools. In Pool S3, the EAIR for any drug-related TEAE was
50.1 per 100 patient-years (95% CI: 43.19, 58.04) for placebo compared to 58.0 (95% CI: 52.43,
64.24) for brepocitinib ≥30 mg and 63.0 (95% CI: 54.92, 72.22) for brepocitinib ≥45 mg, with a
total brepocitinib EAIR of 56.4 (95% CI: 51.25, 61.98). The on-study analysis yielded similar
results. The numerically higher drug-related TEAE rate with brepocitinib compared to placebo is
199
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
consistent across both Pool S2 and Pool S3 and is consistent with the known pharmacological
effects of JAK inhibition, including the common adverse reactions of headache, fatigue, nausea,
and acne identified in the pivotal trial. The confidence intervals for drug-related TEAEs show
less overlap between brepocitinib and placebo than for overall TEAEs, suggesting a more
consistent signal for drug-related events, though the absolute magnitude of the difference is
modest.
Serious TEAEs
EAIRs for serious TEAEs were numerically similar between brepocitinib and placebo across
both pools, with overlapping confidence intervals in all analyses. In Pool S3, the EAIR for any
serious TEAE was 8.7 per 100 patient-years (95% CI: 6.36, 11.92) for placebo compared to 10.1
(95% CI: 8.15, 12.47) for brepocitinib ≥30 mg and 9.7 (95% CI: 7.17, 13.13) for brepocitinib
≥45 mg, with a total brepocitinib EAIR of 9.9 (95% CI: 8.17, 12.11). EAIRs for drug-related
serious TEAEs were also numerically higher in brepocitinib-treated subjects (2.6 to 2.7 per 100
patient-years for brepocitinib ≥15 mg and ≥30 mg) compared to placebo (1.7 per 100 patientyears), though confidence intervals overlap. These findings are consistent with the Pool S2 data
and with the serious TEAE findings from Pool S1, where serious infections in the brepocitinib
30 mg arm were the primary driver of the numerically higher serious TEAE rate compared to
placebo.
Fatal Events
EAIRs for fatal events were low across all treatment groups in both pools. In Pool S3, no fatal
events were reported in the placebo arm, while the EAIR for fatal events was 0.3 per 100 patientyears (95% CI: 0.10, 0.91) for brepocitinib ≥15 mg, 0.3 (95% CI: 0.11, 1.07) for brepocitinib
≥30 mg, and 0.5 (95% CI: 0.11, 1.81) for brepocitinib ≥45 mg. The on-study analysis yielded
similar results, with fatal event EAIRs of 0.2 per 100 patient-years for brepocitinib ≥15 mg and
≥30 mg. The three deaths reported in the brepocitinib arms across the pooled phase 2/3 studies
occurring in Study B7931028 (SLE) and Study B7931004 (plaque psoriasis), were all assessed as
not related to brepocitinib by the Investigator and Applicant Overall, there does not appear to be
an excess of fatal events attributable to brepocitinib in the clinical development program.
Treatment Discontinuation
EAIRs for events leading to study drug discontinuation were comparable between brepocitinib
and placebo in Pool S3, with rates of 13.2 per 100 patient-years for placebo, 14.1 for brepocitinib
≥30 mg, and 18.0 for brepocitinib ≥45 mg, with overlapping confidence intervals for the ≥30 mg
group. The numerically higher discontinuation rate in the brepocitinib ≥45 mg group is
consistent with the higher overall TEAE burden at this dose, which is not the proposed labeled
dose for dermatomyositis. EAIRs for events leading to study discontinuation were numerically
lower in brepocitinib-treated subjects (4.1 to 4.5 per 100 patient-years) compared to placebo (5.5
per 100 patient-years) in Pool S3, consistent with the lower study discontinuation rates observed
in the brepocitinib arms of the pivotal trial. EAIRs for events leading to temporary drug
discontinuation were numerically higher in brepocitinib-treated subjects compared to placebo
across both pools, though confidence intervals overlap substantially.

200
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Adverse Events of Special Interest
The AESI EAIR data from Pool S2 provide supportive characterization of the brepocitinib safety
profile across the broader clinical development program. The most clinically significant finding
was a meaningfully higher EAIR for serious infections in the brepocitinib 30 mg group (6.4 per
100 patient-years; 95% CI: 3.71, 11.00) compared to placebo (1.3; 95% CI: 0.41, 3.97), with
limited confidence interval overlap, consistent with the serious infection signal identified in Pool
S1 and the pivotal trial. Liver enzyme elevation EAIRs were also numerically higher with
brepocitinib ≥30 mg (3.6; 95% CI: 2.11, 6.26) compared to placebo (1.3; 95% CI: 0.41, 3.97),
consistent with the hepatotoxicity signal identified by the DHN DILI Team. All remaining
AESIs, including malignancy (excluding NMSC and NMSC only), cardiovascular events,
MACE, thromboembolic events, viral reactivation (herpes zoster and non-herpes zoster), CK
elevation, and opportunistic infections, were rare across all treatment groups with wide and
overlapping confidence intervals, and rates were generally comparable between brepocitinib and
placebo, consistent with the known class effects of JAK inhibitors. Notably, AESI-specific EAIR
data are not available for Pool S3, as the Applicant confirmed in their June 17, 2026, IR response
that adverse events of special interest were not reviewed against prespecified case definitions in
Pool S3 given its broader scope encompassing non-placebo-controlled and open-label periods
across multiple indications, precluding meaningful AESI attribution in that pool. Taken together,
the Pool S2 AESI EAIR data are consistent with and supportive of the AESI findings from Pool
S1 and do not identify any new or unexpected safety signals that would alter the overall benefitrisk assessment for brepocitinib 30 mg in the treatment of adults with dermatomyositis.
Overall Conclusion
The EAIR analyses from Safety Pools S2 and S3 are broadly supportive of the safety profile
established in the primary Pool S1 analysis and the pivotal Trial PVT-2201-301. Across both
supportive pools, overall TEAE rates were comparable between brepocitinib and placebo with
overlapping confidence intervals, while drug-related TEAE rates were numerically higher with
brepocitinib, consistent with the known pharmacological effects of JAK inhibition. Serious
TEAE rates were numerically similar between brepocitinib and placebo with overlapping
confidence intervals, and fatal event rates were low across all treatment groups. No new or
unexpected safety signals were identified in the Pool S2 or Pool S3 EAIR analyses that would
alter the overall benefit-risk assessment for brepocitinib 30 mg in the treatment of adults with
dermatomyositis. These findings support the conclusion that the safety profile of brepocitinib
30 mg is manageable through appropriate labeling, patient selection, and monitoring.
