7.6.4. Safety and Efficacy Results, Study PVT-2201202
As described in Section 3.2, data from Study PVT-2201-202 provide limited supportive evidence
for the safety and efficacy of brepocitinib in adults with dermatomyositis.
Study PVT-2201-202 was a prospective, 12-week, open-label, single-arm phase 2a pilot study
conducted at a single center in the United States (Mayo Clinic) under IND 158707. The study
enrolled adults with skin-predominant dermatomyositis, defined as active cutaneous disease with
minimal or no muscle involvement, who had refractory skin disease despite prior or ongoing
treatment with corticosteroids and/or immunomodulators. All 5 enrolled subjects received
brepocitinib 30 mg orally once daily for 12 weeks, followed by a 4-week off-drug follow-up
period. The study was initiated on August 28, 2024, and completed on February 28, 2025.
All 5 subjects were White, not Hispanic or Latino, and predominantly female (80%). The mean
age was 53.0 years (SD 9.7). All subjects had severe skin disease at baseline, with a mean
CDASI-A score of 40.2 (SD 6.06), and all had normal muscle function at baseline (mean MMT8 score of 150.0). Three subjects (60%) were receiving concomitant corticosteroids and
immunosuppressants at baseline, and 2 (40%) were receiving no background dermatomyositis
therapy. All subjects completed the 12-week treatment period and the off-drug follow-up visit,
with no discontinuation.
Primary endpoint: all 5 subjects experienced clinically meaningful improvements in CDASI-A
score (defined as a minimum 4-point reduction) as early as Week 4, with continued improvement
through Week 12. The mean change from baseline at Week 4 was −20.8 points (51.8%
reduction), and at Week 12 was −30.6 points (77.0% reduction). By Week 12, 80% of subjects
had improved to mild disease category (CDASI-A ≤14) and 20% to moderate disease, compared
to 100% in the severe category at baseline. Notably, all subjects experienced a rebound in skin
disease activity following discontinuation of brepocitinib, with the mean CDASI-A score
increasing from 9.6 at Week 12 to 24.0 at the Week 16 off-drug follow-up visit, reverting to
moderate or severe disease categories.
Secondary endpoints: improvements were observed across all secondary efficacy measures as
early as Week 4, with continued improvement through Week 12. Key findings included a mean
58.3% reduction in CDA-IGA score by Week 12, with all subjects achieving a clinically
meaningful ≥2-point reduction; a mean 86.1% reduction in PhGA-VAS; a mean 73.0% reduction
in PtGA-VAS; a mean 72.0% reduction in Skindex-16 score, reflecting meaningful improvement
in patient-reported quality of life; and a mean DMOMS score of 66.0 at Week 12, reflecting
overall improvement across composite disease measures. CDASI-D scores were largely stable,
with a mean change of only 0.6 points from baseline, indicating that brepocitinib helped stabilize
disease damage. MMT-8 scores remained unchanged throughout the study, as expected in this
skin-predominant population. Photographic documentation confirmed visual improvements in
skin lesions across subjects.
Brepocitinib 30 mg was generally safe and well-tolerated in this population. A total of 11 TEAEs
were reported across all 5 (100%) subjects, of which 8 were mild and 3 were moderate in
severity. No severe AEs, SAEs, deaths, AESIs, or AEs leading to treatment discontinuation were
reported. Four AEs in 2 (40%) subjects were assessed as treatment-related by the Investigator, all
212
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
of which were mild. The most commonly reported preferred terms were acne (2 [40%] subjects)
and headache (2 [40%] subjects), consistent with the known class effects of JAK inhibitors.
Other reported AEs included dyspepsia, gastritis, oral paresthesia, herpes simplex, postprocedural inflammation, and sinus congestion, each occurring in 1 subject. No clinically
significant abnormalities were identified in laboratory parameters, vital signs, physical findings,
or ECGs. No subject experienced a Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events Grade 3
or higher laboratory abnormality, no ALT or AST elevations ≥3× ULN were observed, and all
post-baseline QTcF values remained ≤450 msec with maximum increases from baseline below
30 msec.
Study PVT-2201-202 noted improvements across all cutaneous endpoints for adults with skinpredominant DM and demonstrated an acceptable safety and tolerability profile. Though the
small sample size (N=5) and open-label, single-arm design limit the generalizability of these
findings, the results support a favorable benefit-risk profile of brepocitinib for the treatment of
adults with dermatomyositis.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Cardiovascular Events
Issue
Cardiovascular events occurred in subjects treated with brepocitinib across clinical studies.
Background
Brepocitinib is a JAK inhibitor, and FDA-approved JAK inhibitors for rheumatologic indications
carry Boxed Warnings for increased rates of all-cause mortality, including sudden cardiovascular
death, and higher rates of MACE compared to TNF inhibitors in RA patients.
Assessment
In the pooled S1 analysis, MACE (including thalamic stroke, cerebrovascular accident, and acute
cardiac failure) occurred in three subjects (0.7%) in the total brepocitinib group versus 0 subjects
in the placebo group, with one event resulting in death in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm.
In Trial PVT-2201-301, 1 cardiovascular AESI (cerebrovascular accident) was reported in the
brepocitinib 30 mg arm, which led to treatment discontinuation. During the OLE period, as
reported in the 90 DSU (data cut December 1, 2025), five cardiovascular AESIs occurred in 2
subjects (2 cases of coronary artery disease, and one case each of myocarditis, cardiogenic
shock, and myocardial rupture), four of which met CEC adjudication criteria for MACE; both
affected subjects had significant pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors and comorbidities, and
all fatal cardiovascular events were assessed as not related to brepocitinib by both the
Investigator and the Applicant.
In Trial PVT-2201-301, cardiovascular events were relatively low in frequency and the overall
AESI analyses did not reveal concerning findings beyond what is anticipated for the JAK
inhibitor class.
213
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Conclusion
Current labeling, consistent with the JAK inhibitor class Boxed Warning for MACE, is adequate
to convey this risk; no additional labeling action beyond class-level warnings is recommended at
this time.

7.7.2. Thromboembolic Events
Issue
Thromboembolic events occurred in subjects treated with brepocitinib across clinical studies.
Background
FDA-approved JAK inhibitors carry a Boxed Warning for thrombosis, including increased
incidence of pulmonary embolism, venous, and arterial thrombosis compared to TNF inhibitors
in RA patients, and this class-level risk is expected to apply to brepocitinib.
Assessment
In the pooled S1 analysis, thromboembolic events occurred in one subject (0.2%) in the total
brepocitinib group compared to two subjects (1.1%) in the placebo group, with the single
brepocitinib event being a thalamic stroke in the 15 mg arm; no thromboembolic events were
observed in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm. In Trial PVT-2201-301, one case of pulmonary
embolism occurred in the placebo arm, and no thromboembolic events were observed in either
brepocitinib arm.
Conclusion
The current Boxed Warning for thrombosis, consistent with the JAK inhibitor class, is adequate
to convey this risk; the observed event rates do not suggest a signal beyond the established class
effect.

7.7.3. Viral Reactivation
Issue
Viral reactivation occurred in subjects treated with brepocitinib across clinical studies.
Background
JAK inhibitor-mediated immunosuppression is associated with reactivation of latent viral
infections, particularly herpes zoster, and this risk is reflected in the Warnings and Precautions of
approved JAK inhibitors.
Assessment
In the pooled S1 analysis, viral reactivation events occurred in 13 subjects (3.2%) in the total
brepocitinib group compared to 5 subjects (2.8%) in the placebo group, with herpes zoster being
the predominant event and one case of serious disseminated herpes zoster and one case of
varicella zoster pneumonia occurring in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm. In Trial PVT-2201-301,
214
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
viral reactivation rates were similar between brepocitinib and placebo arms, with no deaths
attributable to viral reactivation.
Conclusion
Current labeling warnings for serious infections and viral reactivation are adequate to convey
this risk, and standard prophylaxis and monitoring recommendations consistent with the JAK
inhibitor class are included in the label, including recommendations for prophylactic varicella
zoster and herpes zoster vaccinations, according to current immunization guidelines.

7.7.4. Malignancy
Issue
New or recurrent diagnoses of malignancy events occurred rarely in subjects treated with
brepocitinib across clinical studies.
Background
FDA-approved JAK inhibitors carry a Boxed Warning for malignancy, including higher rates of
lymphoma and lung cancer compared to TNF inhibitors in RA patients, and this class-level
concern is applicable to brepocitinib given its shared mechanism.
Assessment
In the pooled S1 analysis, new or recurrent malignancies (excluding non-melanoma skin cancer)
occurred in two subjects (0.5%) in the total brepocitinib group compared to one subject (0.6%) in
the placebo group, with no malignancies observed in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm during the
double-blind period. In Trial PVT-2201-301, no malignancy events were observed in either
brepocitinib arm, with malignancy events occurring only in the placebo arm.
Conclusion
The current Boxed Warning for malignancy, consistent with the JAK inhibitor class, is adequate
to convey this risk; the observed event rates do not suggest a signal beyond the established class
effect.

7.7.5. Opportunistic Infections
Issue
Opportunistic infections events occurred in subjects treated with brepocitinib across clinical
studies.
Background
JAK inhibitor-mediated immunosuppression increases the risk of serious bacterial, fungal, and
opportunistic infections, including tuberculosis, and this risk is reflected in the Boxed Warning
of approved JAK inhibitors.

215
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Assessment
In the pooled S1 analysis, serious infections occurred in 16 subjects (3.9%) in the total
brepocitinib group compared to three subjects (1.7%) in the placebo group, with the brepocitinib
30 mg arm showing the highest rate (5.5%), including cases of sepsis, pneumonia, and
disseminated herpes zoster. In Trial PVT-2201-301, the majority of SAEs in the brepocitinib
30 mg arm (8 of 13 events) were under the Infections and Infestations SOC, underscoring the
clinical significance of this safety signal.
Conclusion
Current labeling, including the Boxed Warning for serious infections and the Warning and
Precaution for serious infections, is adequate to convey this risk; standard pre-treatment
screening for tuberculosis and other latent infections, as well as monitoring during treatment, are
recommended in the label.

7.7.6. Liver Enzyme Elevation
Issue
Liver enzyme elevation AESI (defined as increased ALT or AST ≥3× ULN [if baseline ≤ ULN],
or ≥3× the baseline value [if baseline is > ULN]) events occurred in subjects treated with
brepocitinib across clinical studies. The Division consulted the CDER DHN DILI Team to
evaluate the hepatic safety data from Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028 (SLE) given the
presence of subjects in the Temple’s Corollary and Cholestasis Quadrants on the eDISH plots for
both studies.
Background
Hepatotoxicity, including elevated liver enzymes, is a recognized risk associated with JAK
inhibitors and is included as a labeled Warning and Precaution in approved JAK inhibitor
products. The dermatomyositis population presents a unique challenge for the detection of druginduced liver injury, as chronic and recurrent myopathy results in ongoing shedding of ALT and
AST from skeletal muscle, which does not shed GGT, potentially obscuring early recognition of
hepatocellular injury. This interpretive challenge is particularly relevant for brepocitinib given
the high background rate of muscle-derived aminotransferase elevations in the target population
and the need to distinguish drug-induced hepatotoxicity from disease-related enzyme elevations.
Assessment
In Pool S1, liver enzyme elevation AESIs occurred in 12 subjects (2.9%) in the total brepocitinib
group compared to two subjects (1.1%) in the placebo group, with no Hy’s Law cases or
clinically apparent liver injury identified. The DHN DILI Team identified two cases of probable
DILI and one possible DILI case across Trial PVT-2201-301 and Study B7931028; all were
asymptomatic, non-hyperbilirubinemic, and resolved completely. The DILI Team noted a
numerically higher proportion of Temple’s corollary cases in the brepocitinib 30 mg arm
compared to placebo, suggesting a possible dose-response relationship at the labeled dose, and
observed that AT elevation rates in the brepocitinib trials appear higher than those reported for
other JAK inhibitors used off-label for dermatomyositis. Based on these findings, the DILI Team
216
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
recommended baseline liver blood testing prior to treatment initiation and the addition of GGT to
the recommended monitoring panel to help clinicians distinguish hepatic from muscle-derived
aminotransferase elevations in this population. The DILI Team did not recommend a
postmarketing requirement (PMR) or postmarketing commitment, and noted that standard
pharmacovigilance is considered sufficient. A discrepancy between the AT >3× ULN rates in the
clinical data summary outputs and those in the proposed label was identified and the Applicant
was asked to reconcile these figures (see Section 16).
Conclusion
The identified hepatic safety signal is manageable through enhanced labeling, including
requirements for baseline liver blood testing and the addition of GGT to the recommended
monitoring panel to facilitate the distinction of hepatic from muscular aminotransferase
elevations in this population. No PMR or postmarketing commitment is recommended for this
safety finding.

7.7.7. Hypoglycemia
Issue
Hypoglycemia events occurred in subjects treated with brepocitinib across clinical studies.
Background
Hypoglycemia is a newly identified class risk associated with JAK inhibitors and is included as a
labeled Warning and Precaution in approved JAK inhibitor products.
Assessment
To resolve discrepancies between the Division’s findings of higher hypoglycemia rates and the
Applicant's initial reporting of only one hypoglycemia TEAE, the Division issued three IRs
between May and July 2026. The Applicant initially explained that site investigators dismissed
most laboratory abnormalities as clinically insignificant due to factors such as fasting status or
lack of symptoms. Ultimately, the Division required the Applicant to recalculate and standardize
hypoglycemia events using American Diabetes Association (ADA) 2024 definitions. The
resulting ADA-standardized analysis revealed that exposure-adjusted incidence rates for Level 1
(<70 and ≥54 mg/dL) and Level 2 (<54 mg/dL) hypoglycemia were higher in brepocitinibtreated subjects compared to placebo across all safety pools. While Level 1 events were common
but largely asymptomatic, the data suggest diabetic subjects may have a higher incidence of
Level 2 hypoglycemia; however, the sample size was small. No Level 3 (severe) events occurred
during the clinical development program.
Conclusion
Based on the ADA-standardized analyses, the review team confirmed a higher incidence of
Level 1 and Level 2 hypoglycemia in brepocitinib-treated patients. To address this safety signal
and align with the March 2026 FDA class-wide mandate for oral JAK inhibitors, the required
Safety Labeling Change (SLC) language was added to the brepocitinib USPI, tailored slightly to
reflect the available clinical data.
217
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

7.7.8. Kidney Function
Issue
Dose-dependent elevations in serum creatinine and corresponding decreases in creatinine-based
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) were observed in subjects treated with brepocitinib.
Background
Brepocitinib is an inhibitor of renal transporters (OCT2 and MATE1/2K) responsible for the
tubular secretion of creatinine. Additionally, in patients with dermatomyositis, baseline
creatinine levels may be artificially low due to muscle atrophy, leading to an overestimation of
baseline kidney function. Because of these factors, transporter inhibition by brepocitinib can
cause artificial decreases in creatinine-based eGFR that do not reflect true glomerular filtration
impairment. Cystatin C, which is freely filtered and not subject to tubular secretion, serves as a
more reliable biomarker for true GFR in this setting.
Assessment
Across clinical trials, including Trial PVT-2201-301, brepocitinib demonstrated a dosedependent increase in serum creatinine and a decrease in creatinine-based eGFR.
In the double-blind treatment period of Trial PVT-2201-301, Level 1 creatinine elevations
(≥1.5× baseline) occurred in up to 37.0% of subjects in the 30 mg arm compared to 2.6% to 3.4%
in placebo arms. Similarly, Level 1 eGFR decreases (≥25% decrease) occurred in up to 61.7% of
subjects in the 30 mg arm compared to 9.0% to 10.3% in placebo. Data from Pool S1 also
demonstrated a consistent dose-dependent pattern, with Level 1 eGFR decreases occurring in up
to 58.6% of subjects in the 45 mg arm compared to 9.0% in the placebo arm. However, the
observed changes in Trial PVT-2201-301 and Pool S1 were not accompanied by clinically
meaningful or sustained declines in cystatin C-based eGFR.
Open-label extension data confirmed that concurrent clinically meaningful reductions (>50%) in
both creatinine- and cystatin C-based eGFR were rare.
Additionally, in Trial PVT-2201-301, eGFR following treatment discontinuation for any reason
demonstrated an overall trend where eGFR improved or remained stable in brepocitinib-treated
participants.
Conclusion
The observed decreases in creatinine-based eGFR are considered an artificial effect driven by
brepocitinib’s inhibition of renal creatinine transporters rather than genuine nephrotoxicity, a
conclusion supported by the lack of decline in cystatin C-based eGFR and eGFR improvement
following treatment discontinuation.
Additionally, the proposed USPI statement that brepocitinib is not recommended in patients with
severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²) is reasonable, as the pharmacokinetic and
safety profile in this population has not been adequately characterized.

218
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Based on population pharmacokinetics (PK) analysis, brepocitinib AUCss in patients with DM
was estimated to be 38% higher relative to the non-DM population with minimal change in
maximum plasma concentration (Cmax,ss). No dosage adjustment is recommended based on body
weight (39 to 204 kg), sex, age (18 to 77 years), and race (White, Black, Asian, Native
American, Pacific Islander, Other Race).
Renal Impairment
No dosage adjustments are recommended for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment.
Brepocitinib is not recommended for patients with severe renal impairment.
A dedicated renal impairment study, B7931048, was conducted to evaluate the pharmacokinetics
of brepocitinib following a single oral administration of 30 mg brepocitinib in subjects with
mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment, relative to subjects with normal renal function.
eGFR was estimated based on Modification of Diet in Renal Disease formula and then converted
to absolute eGFR (mL/min). by adjusting subject’s body surface area (calculated as body surface
area = (Weight0.425 × Height0.725) × 0.007184) for eligibility assessment.
In subjects with mild renal impairment (eGFR: 60 to 89 mL/min), brepocitinib Cmax and AUC
decreased by 5% and 29%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC increased by 33% and 45%,
respectively, compared to subjects with normal renal function (eGFR: ≥90 mL/min). In subjects
with moderate renal impairment (eGFR: 30 to 59 mL/min), brepocitinib Cmax and AUC increased
by 24% and 48%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC increased by 22% and 129%, respectively,
compared to subjects with normal renal function. In subjects with severe renal impairment
(eGFR: 15 to 29 mL/min), brepocitinib Cmax and AUC increased by 10% and 12%, respectively,
M1 Cmax and AUC increased by 78% and 346%, respectively, compared to subjects with normal
renal function.
The safety assessment in patients with moderate renal impairment is supported by nonclinical
toxicology studies, concentration-QT relationship findings, and clinical safety data at
supratherapeutic doses:
•

In animal toxicology studies, the rat no observed adverse effect level is associated with an
M1 AUC (27200 ng·h/mL) that is 7-fold higher than the M1 AUC (3984 ng·h/mL) observed
in subjects with moderate renal impairment.

•

The concentration QT relationship predicted that changes in QTcF were 7.32 msec (90% CI:
6.18, 8.83) and 8.40 msec (90% CI: 7.28, 9.84), respectively also assuming an increase in
brepocitinib Cmax of 1.5- and 2.0-fold (297 and 396 ng/mL, respectively). Since brepocitinib
and metabolite M1 concentrations are correlated (based on PK profiles with similar Tmax) and
M1 is less potent in inhibiting hERG currents (IC50: 13000 ng/mL for brepocitinib, 20300
ng/mL for M1), no additional QTc prolongation is expected beyond that predicted by the
brepocitinib concentration-QT model.
219

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
•

In clinical safety assessment, no QT prolongation related safety findings at 45mg and 60 mg,
e.g. Torsades, sudden cardiac death, elevated MACE events, were observed (see Section 5
for detailed safety assessment).

Therefore, no dosage adjustments are recommended for patients with mild or moderate renal
impairment. Brepocitinib is not recommended for patients with severe renal impairment.
Hepatic Impairment
No dosage adjustments are recommended for patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment
(Child-Pugh A or B). Brepocitinib has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic
impairment (Child-Pugh C).
A dedicated hepatic impairment study, PVT-2201-101, was conducted to evaluate the
pharmacokinetics of brepocitinib following a single oral administration of 30 mg brepocitinib in
subjects with moderate hepatic impairment relative to subjects with normal hepatic function.
Compared to subjects with normal hepatic function, brepocitinib Cmax and AUC decreased by
17% and increased by 19%, respectively, M1 Cmax and AUC decreased by 19% and increased by
19%, respectively, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child Pugh 7 to 9). The
observed changes in exposure are not considered clinically relevant. Therefore, no dosage
adjustments are recommended for patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Brepocitinib may be administered without regard to food. Smoking may reduce the effectiveness
of brepocitinib in patients with dermatomyositis. No dosage adjustments are recommended when
brepocitinib is coadministered with cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 inhibitors and P-glycoprotein
(P-gp) inhibitors. However, co-administration with medications known as a substrate of P-gp,
breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), MATE1, MATE2K, organic cation transporter (OCT)1,
or OCT2 for which a small change in substrate plasma concentration may lead to serious
toxicities should be closely monitored.
Food Effect
The effect of food on the pharmacokinetics of brepocitinib was assessed in Study B7931001.
Following a single oral dose of 100-mg tablets, brepocitinib Cmax and AUC decreased by 36% and
18%, respectively, under fed (high-fat and high-calorie) condition compared to fasted condition.
The median time to maximum concentration of brepocitinib was delayed from 0.5 hours (range
0.5 to 2 hours) under fasted condition to 4 hours (range 1.0 to 4.0 hours) under fed condition.
Food was not found to have a clinically relevant impact on the pharmacokinetics of brepocitinib.
Therefore, brepocitinib may be administered with or without food.
Smoking
Smoking causes induction of CYP1A1 and CYP1A2, which are the major metabolism enzymes
of brepocitinib. Following 30 mg brepocitinib once daily administration in patients with
dermatomyositis, brepocitinib AUC and Cmax at steady state was estimated to be 45% and 33%
lower, respectively, in current smokers compared to non-current smokers. Therefore, the
effectiveness of brepocitinib may be reduced in smokers.
220
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
Effect of Other Drugs on Brepocitinib
Brepocitinib is mainly metabolized by CYP1A1 and CYP1A2 with minor contribution from
CYP3A4. Brepocitinib is also a substrate of the transporter P-gp.
The Applicant conducted one clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) study (B7931033) to explore
the impact of strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and P-gp inhibitor (itraconazole) on brepocitinib PK.
When co-administered with the strong CYP3A4 and P-gp inhibitor, itraconazole, brepocitinib
Cmax decreased by 2% and AUC increased by 26%, M1 Cmax and AUC decreased by 30% and
11%, respectively, relative to that after administration of brepocitinib alone. The exposure
change of brepocitinib and M1 is not considered clinically relevant. Therefore, no dosage
adjustments are recommended when brepocitinib is co-administered with CYP3A4 inhibitors and
P-gp inhibitors.
Effect of Brepocitinib on Other Drugs
Based on in vitro studies, brepocitinib inhibits transporters of P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2K,
OCT1, OCT2, and M1 inhibits MATE2K. However, no clinical DDI studies were conducted to
explore the impact of brepocitinib on substrates of these transporters. In the pivotal efficacy
study (Study PVT-2201-301), narrow therapeutic index P-gp/BCRP and OCT1/OCT2 substrates,
including colchicine, digoxin, fentanyl, quinidine, dofetilide, procainamide, were prohibited at
least 2 weeks prior to randomization and during the study. Therefore, co-administration with
medications known as a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2K, OCT1, OCT2
transporters for which a small change in substrate plasma concentration may lead to serious
toxicities should be closely monitored.
Notably, brepocitinib is an OCT2 inhibitor and MATE1/2K inhibitor, which are the basolateral
transporter and apical transporters of creatinine in the renal proximal tubules. A post-treatment
elevation in creatinine and decrease in creatinine based eGFR were observed in the pivotal
clinical Trial PVT-2201-301. Oppositely, serum cystatin C based eGFR increased after
brepocitinib treatment in patients with DM.
In dermatomyositis, progressive inflammation can lead to severe muscle cell destruction, tissue
necrosis, and eventual loss of muscle bulk (atrophy). Because creatinine is a byproduct of muscle
metabolism, this significant reduction in muscle mass directly causes a drop in baseline
creatinine levels and an overestimation of kidney function based on Modification of Diet in
Renal Disease formula (creatinine based eGFR). Therefore, the baseline creatinine based eGFR
is significantly higher than the baseline Cystatin C based eGFR.
After treatment with brepocitinib, a dose dependent increase in serum creatinine was observed in
Study PVT-2201-301, which may be contributed by decrease creatinine excretion via kidney
transporter inhibition. Therefore, the renal toxicity, as measured by decreased creatinine based
eGFR, is considered artificial. Cystatin C based eGFR, which showed an opposite trend in renal
function (increase in GFR estimates), reassured the lack of renal toxicity in DM subjects treated
with brepocitinib. See Section 7 for more details.

221
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Brepocitinib was granted FDA Orphan Drug Designation (ODD) for the treatment of
dermatomyositis in May 2025. An initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) was submitted by the
Applicant in March 2025; however, following the granting of ODD in May 2025, the Applicant
withdrew the iPSP in June 2025. As dermatomyositis meets the criteria for orphan drug
designation, brepocitinib is exempt from the pediatric study requirements under the Pediatric
Research Equity Act pursuant to (21 CFR 314.55(b)), as the drug has been designated for a rare
disease or condition under (21 U.S.C 360bb), and therefore no pediatric studies are required at
this time.

Discussion and Conclusions From Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health-Maternal
Health Team
Pregnancy
Available data from pregnancies which occurred during the development program for
brepocitinib are insufficient to inform a drug-associated risk of major birth defects, miscarriage,
or other adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. Although the case of cleft lip reported during the
clinical development program is concerning given the timing of brepocitinib exposure early in
the first trimester, generalized conclusions cannot be drawn from a single case. As described
above, embryofetal development studies showed fetal skeletal malformations and postimplantation loss at exposures 1.3 and 3 times the exposure at the MRHD in rats and rabbits,
respectively. Labeling for another drug in this class (upadacitinib) describes embryofetal toxicity
at exposures 0.6 and 5.6 times the exposure at the MRHD in rats and rabbits, respectively.6
Reproductive toxicity labeling for JAKi drugs is based on exposure multiples observed in
reproductive toxicology studies and does not appear to be related to specific JAK selectivity.
Labeling for the other drug also includes embryofetal toxicity in Warnings and Precautions.
Because the exposure multiples for brepocitinib are similar to those observed for upadacitinib,
Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health (DPMH) recommends inclusion of embryofetal
toxicity in Warnings and Precautions for brepocitinib labeling as well.
Active DM during pregnancy is associated with an increased risk of preterm birth, low birth
weight, and spontaneous abortion. The Applicant proposes to include a Clinical Considerations

6

Prior qualitative interviews conducted in 2021 with two focus groups of patients with DM (n = 14) gave the basis
for relevant and most bothersome symptoms.
The labeling for upadacitinib was a source for comparison of exposure margins for reproductive toxicology data but
was not relied upon for the determination of reproductive safety of brepocitinib.

223
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
heading describing these disease-associated risks under labeling subsection 8.1 Pregnancy.
DPMH agrees with inclusion of this heading.
Although DM is a rare disease, and is uncommon in females of reproductive potential,
brepocitinib is being studied for a number of other indications. The cleft lip and the low exposure
multiples for embryofetal toxicity raises concerns that warrant collection of additional safety data
in the postmarket setting regarding the use of brepocitinib during pregnancy. Because DM is a
rare disease, and is uncommon in females of reproductive potential, including during pregnancy,
a pregnancy registry and complementary pregnancy safety study would be unlikely to enroll a
sufficient number of pregnant patients to provide interpretable data. As such, DPMH
recommends that the Division of Rheumatology and Transplant Medicine issue a PMR for a
DPSS to further characterize the potential risks with use of brepocitinib during pregnancy.
DPMH recommends the following PMR language:
Conduct a worldwide descriptive study that collects prospective and retrospective data in
women exposed to LISRAYA during pregnancy to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal
complications, and adverse effects on the developing fetus, neonate, and infant. Assess
infant outcomes through at least the first year of life. The minimum number of patients
will be specified in the protocol.

If brepocitinib is approved for other indications, the descriptive pregnancy safety study protocol
should be expanded to include these populations.
Lactation
There are no data on the presence of brepocitinib in human or animal milk, effects on the
breastfed infant, or the effect on milk production. Other drugs in the JAKi class have been found
to be present in animal milk and therefore are likely to be present in human milk. Like the other
JAKi drugs, brepocitinib is a small molecule (molecular weight <800 Daltons), and only 39%
bound to plasma proteins. As such, it is plausible that brepocitinib may also be present in human
milk. Treatment with JAKi drugs is associated with serious and potentially fatal adverse events,
including serious infections, GI perforation, and malignancy. Because of the potential for serious
adverse reactions in the breastfed infant, DPMH recommends that labeling advise women not to
breastfeed during treatment and for 3 days (i.e., 5 half-lives) after the last dose of LISRAYA.
DPMH notes that this recommendation is consistent with labeling with other products in this
class.
Based on discussions with the clinical and nonclinical teams, the exposure threshold for
brepocitinib for the serious adverse events typically associated with JAKi drugs is unknown. The
clinical team is also concerned that the safety profile of brepocitinib has not been sufficiently
characterized to definitively say that a low relative infant dose from a clinical lactation study
would be sufficiently reassuring to justify removing the recommendation not to breastfeed (email
communication from DRTM clinical reviewer dated 6/22/2026). Given these uncertainties,
DPMH does not recommend that DRTM issue a PMR for a clinical lactation study at this time
and recommends that outcomes from exposures via breast milk be monitored using routine
pharmacovigilance. If future approvals for new indications broaden the populations for which
brepocitinib may be prescribed, and more safety data become available, consideration should be
given to conducting a clinical lactation study to characterize the presence and amount of

224
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
brepocitinib in breast milk. The Applicant may also consider conducting a clinical lactation
study, even if such a study is not required by the Agency.
Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
Brepocitinib is not mutagenic and has no clinically relevant drug-drug interactions with
hormonal contraceptives. The Applicant’s proposed labeling includes subsection 8.3 Females
and Males of Reproductive Potential with a warning regarding potential embryofetal toxicity and
a recommendation to verify the pregnancy status of females of reproductive potential. This
recommendation is also present in subsection 2.1 Dosage and Administration/ Recommended
Evaluations and Immunizations Prior to Treatment Initiation. DPMH concurs with inclusion of
these recommendations in labeling. The Applicant’s proposed labeling also includes a statement
advising prescribers to consider pregnancy planning and prevention for females of reproductive
potential. DPMH recommends that this language be strengthened to advise females of
reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment and for 3 days following
the last dose of LISRAYA.
For further information, refer to the DPMH consult review dated June 23, 2026
(DARRTS ID: 5821078)

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 220106 with respect to
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable
standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such Office of
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
Priovant Therapeutics, Inc. submitted a new NDA 220106 for LISRAYA (brepocitinib) tablets,
30 mg via 505 (b)(1) regulatory pathway for the treatment of dermatomyositis in adults. This
NDA 220106 is based on the IND 158707.
The drug substance, brepocitinib tosylate, is a white to off-white solid which is classified as a

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
The Applicant provided attestation that the clinical studies were conducted in accordance with
the CFR governing the protection of human subjects (21 CFR part 50), institutional review
boards (21 CFR part 56), and the obligations of clinical investigators (21 CFR 312.50 to 312.70)
in accordance with good clinical practice (GCP). In general, the Office of Scientific
Investigations (OSI) found that the study was conducted adequately and the Sponsor's oversight
was acceptable.
During clinical site inspections, minor protocol deviations and documentation deficiencies were
identified, but these do not substantially affect the reliability of the overall safety or efficacy
review:
•

Site 10-139 (Dr. Ventura): OSI identified documentation deficiencies primarily concerning
AE logs, including discrepancies between paper source logs and electronic data capture
(EDC) entries regarding AE start/end dates, outcomes, and severities, as well as the use of
pencil for source documents. Of note, the efficacy results did not have pencil violations and
OSI verified the primary efficacy endpoints with no discrepancies found. Additionally, four
unreported AEs were discovered in subjects receiving brepocitinib (mild cutaneous fungal
infection, moderate constipation, acute bronchitis, and mild radiculopathy). From a clinical
perspective, the fungal infection and acute bronchitis are consistent with the known increased
risk of infection with brepocitinib, and the other events do not meaningfully impact the
broader safety profile or assessments for brepocitinib.

226
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5860011

NDA 220106
LISRAYA (brepocitinib)
•

Site 10-130 (Dr. Mangold): Minor protocol deviations were identified for two subjects. One
subject on brepocitinib 30 mg received a single 25 mcg dose of prohibited IV fentanyl during
a serious AE (appendicitis) without interruption of the study drug. A single dose of fentanyl
in this clinical setting does not impact the safety analysis. A second subject on placebo used
unreported topical clobetasol for scalp pruritus for 31 weeks. While prolonged topical
corticosteroid use could theoretically impact global disease activity scores, the Cutaneous
Dermatomyositis Area and Severity Index (CDASI) is not included in the proposed label,
making this isolated deviation negligible to the overall efficacy results and interpretations.

A review of financial disclosure records did not identify any concerns that have the potential to
impact the interpretation of safety or efficacy. The Applicant adequately disclosed financial
interests and arrangements with clinical investigators as recommended in FDA guidance, and
none of the disclosed interests raise questions about data integrity

III. Additional Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
On December 29, 2025, Priovant Therapeutics, Inc. submitted a new molecular entity NDA
application for brepocitinib. This NDA has an orphan drug designation and is part of “the
program” with a priority review designation on an 8-month clock with a Prescription Drug User
Fee Act goal date of August 29, 2026. Brepocitinib was developed under IND 158707.
A summary of key submission regulatory activities for brepocitinib is described below.

the amended ISS SAP. FDA generally agreed that the
proposed safety pooling strategy was reasonable and that
planned estimands SE2 and SE4 were acceptable, but
provided detailed recommendations regarding estimands SE1
and SE3, noting that the proposed censoring approach
reflected a hypothetical strategy rather than a true “while on
treatment” strategy. FDA also requested clarification on the
approach for identifying AESIs, noting that case definitions
were not provided in the ISS SAP and that it was unclear
which AEs would require full narratives.

case definitions, and data standards, and made a preliminary

17. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
PMR 5033-1
Conduct a worldwide descriptive study that collects prospective and retrospective data in women
exposed to brepocitinib during pregnancy to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal
complications, and adverse effects on the developing fetus, neonate, and infant. Assess infant
outcomes through at least the first year of life. The minimum number of patients will be
specified in the protocol.
Draft Protocol Submission:

10/2026

Final Protocol Submission:

04/2027

Interim Report Submissions:

05/2030 and 08/2033

Study Completion:

08/2037

Final Report Submission:

02/2038

PMR 5033-2
Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial evaluating the effect of repeat doses of brepocitinib on
the single dose pharmacokinetics of substrates (P-gp and BCRP). This trial should be designed
and conducted in accordance with the FDA guidance for industry M12 Drug Interaction Studies
(August 2024).
Draft Protocol Submission:

10/2026

Final Protocol Submission:

02/2027

Study Completion:

10/2027

02/2028

Bioanalytical Methods
A fully validated liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometric detection (LC-MS/MS)
assay was developed for quantification of brepocitinib in human plasma. The assay was initially
(b) (4)
developed by Pfizer (Validation Report 150640) and then transferred to
(b) (4)
(Validation Report B7939003 and addendum 1) and
(Validation Report V6162102P1). The
bioanalytical assays for quantification of brepocitinib in human plasma are acceptable.
A fully validated LC-MS/MS assay was developed for quantification of M1 in human plasma. The
(b) (4)
assay was initially developed by
(Validation
(b) (4)
Report B7939005) and then transferred to
(Validation Report V6162102P2). The bioanalytical assays for quantification of M1 in
human plasma are acceptable.

Reference ID: 5859282

In Vitro Studies
The brief summary of in vitro studies was listed in Table 2.
Table 2. Summary of in vitro studies using human biomaterials
ADME

Absorption

Distribution

Metabolism

Conclusions
Study/Report
Assessment of bidirectional permeability in MDCK-MDR1 cells showed that the
MDR1 efflux ratios of PF-6700841 at 0.3, 1, and 3 μM were 28.5, 23.5, and
015403
20.3, respectively. PF-06700841 is a substrate of human P-gp.
Assessment of bidirectional permeability in MDCK-BCRP cells with and without
the BCRP inhibitor (Ko143) showed that the BCRP efflux ratio of PF-06700841
042024
at 0.5 μM was 1.9. PF-06700841 is not a substrate of human BCRP.
The mean blood to plasma ratio for PF-06802530 (M1) was 0.89, 0.80, 0.681,
083657
0.77 and 0.82 in mouse, rat, rabbit, monkey and human respectively.
The mean blood to plasma ratio for PF-06700841 was 1.07, 1.08 and 0.84 in
104613
rat, monkey and human respectively.
The mean protein binding of PF-06700841 in rat, monkey and human plasma
173600
was 0.31, 0.27, and 0.39, respectively.
The mean protein binding of PF-06700841 in mouse and rabbit plasma was
164635
0.49 and 0.64 respectively.
The mean protein binding of PF-06802530 (M1) in mouse, rat, rabbit, monkey
034035
and human plasma was 0.46, 0.42, 0.38, 0.24, and 0.21, respectively.
Preliminary phenotyping of PF-06700841 showed that CYP3A seemed a major
013954
contributor (fmCYP3A=0.62).
PF-06700841 was incubated across a panel of recombinant human CYP
enzymes and formation of metabolites M1, M2, M3 and M4 was monitored.
M1 was observed in greatest abundance with CYP1A1 with comparatively
minor levels formed in CYP3A4, CYP1A2, CYP1B1 and CYP3A5 respectively. M2
112917
was observed in greatest abundance with CYP1A1 with comparatively minor
levels formed in CYP1B1 and CYP1A2 respectively. M3 was observed in
greatest abundance with CYP1A1 with comparatively minor levels formed in
CYP1A2. M4 was observed in greatest abundance with CYP1B1 followed by
CYP1A2, CYP2J2 and CYP2D6.
Reaction phenotyping experiments were conducted in HLM using CYP
inhibitors to determine the CYP450 isoforms involved in the metabolism of PF06700841 to three metabolites PF-06802530 (M1), PF-07084027 (M3), and PF06695739 (M4). Percent inhibition with selective CYP inhibitors was
determined in HLM incubations by monitoring metabolite formation of PF06700841. Selective CYP inhibitors include furafylline (1A2), montelukast
120720
(2C8), sulfaphenazole (2C9), n-benzylnirvanol (2C19), quinidine (2D6), and
ketoconazole (3A4). Results indicated that the fm value for CYP3A4/5 and
CYP1A2 was 0.77, and 0.14, respectively, and 0.09 for other CYPs. However,
the inhibitor of CYP1A1 was not evaluated in this study although Study 112917
indicated that M1 is mainly formed by CYP1A1. In addition, ketoconazole
inhibits both CYPs 3A4 and 1A1. See reviewer’s comments for additional
discussion.

Conclusions
Study/Report
PF-06700841 underwent minor to moderate metabolism in-vitro and
moderate to extensive metabolism in-vivo with a total of 9 metabolites
identified. There were no human-unique metabolites. The in vitro metabolic
profiles across rat, monkey and human were qualitatively similar, with
153827
oxidative modifications of the diazabicylco-octane ring representing the major
site of metabolism in all species. The in vivo metabolic profiles of PF-06700841
in rat and monkey plasma/urine revealed oxidative modifications of the
diazabicylco-octane and/or methyl pyrazole motifs as the major sites of
metabolism.
Based on assessment of plasma and urine samples from clinical study
B7931001, the primary metabolic pathways in both urine and plasma involved
091236
oxidative modifications the pyrazole region of the molecule.
PF-06700841 demonstrated little or no reversible inhibition of UGT1A1,
UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9 and UGT2B7 catalyzed enzyme activities (IC50
155254
>69.1 µM).
The IC50 values of PF-06700841 for the direct inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5 were all > 69 μM. PF084405
06700841 may have the potential to cause metabolism-dependent (timedependent and NADPH-dependent) inhibition of CYP3A4/5 enzyme activity at
69 μM.
PF-06700841 was a weak TDI of CYP3A4/5. The Kobs was above 0.01/min
when PF-06700841 concentration is 17.5 µM or 42.2 µM using testosterone or
102906
midazolam as prob substrate, respectively.
Based on the Kobs values, the potential of PF-06802530 (M1) as a TDI for CYPs
103817
2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 3A4/5 is low at clinically relevant concentration.
A >20% change in activity following a 30-minute pre-incubation was observed
085351
for CYP2B6, 2C8, 2C9, CYP3A4/5 at 100 μM PF-06802530.
PF-06700841 did not cause induction of CYP3A4, CYP2B6 or CYP1A2 in human
093836
hepatocytes at any of the concentrations tested (0.3-69µM).
PF-06700841 is an inhibitor of the BSEP efflux transporter.
18GR042
The IC50 values for inhibition of human SULT 1E1, 1A1, and 2A1 by PF014637
06700841 were all determined to be >100 μM.
The estimated IC50 values of PF-06802530 (M1) as inhibitors are >100 μM for
MATE1, MDR1, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B3, 53.6 μM for BCRP, 7.0 μM for MATE2K,
020653
75 μM for OATP1B1, 11.3 μM for OCT1, 32.1 μM for OCT2.
The estimated IC50 values of PF-06700841as inhibitors are 3.11 μM for MDR1,
5.29 μM for MATE1, 14.7 μM for MATE2K, 78.2 μM for OATP1B1, >207 μM for
192929
OATP1B3, 0.84 μM for OCT2.
The estimated IC50 values of PF-06700841as inhibitors are 10.2 μM for BCRP,
100621
88.5 μM for OAT1, 105 μM for OAT3, 0.4 μM for OCT1.

Abbreviations: MDCK, Madin-Darby canine kidney; BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; HLM, human liver microsomes; CYP, cytochrome
P450 enzyme; MATE, multidrug and toxin extrusion; OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptides; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; TDI, time dependent
inhibition; SULT, sulfotransferase; UGT, Uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyl transferases; OCT, organic cation transporter; OAT, organic anion
transporter; NADPH, nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate; IC50, half-maximal inhibitory concentration.

Reviewer’s comments
The applicant proposed that “The hepatic metabolism of brepocitinib is primarily mediated by
CYP3A4 with minor contribution from CYP1A2.” However, Study 112917 demonstrated that
brepocitinib was extensively metabolized in recombinant CYP1A1 preparation but not other
5

Reference ID: 5859282

CYPs (Figure 1). The pathway of brepocitinib metabolism through M1, the metabolite primarily
formed by CYP1A1, accounted for at least 60% of administered dose based on the mass balance
study.
Figure 1. UHPLC-UV chromatograms for PF-06700841 in a panel of recombinant CYP enzymes (λ=262
nm)

(Source: Figure 6.1 in Study Report PF-06700841_24Jan22_112917)

6

Reference ID: 5859282

In addition, the clinical drug interaction study with itraconazole revealed that CYP3A only played
a limited role in brepocitinib metabolism. Therefore, based on submitted information, CYP1A1 is
likely the major enzyme for brepocitinib metabolism instead of the proposed CYP3A4. On
4/17/2026 and 06/12/2026, FDA issued Information Requests recommending the applicant to
conduct additional in-vitro experiment using human liver microsomes with relatively specific
inhibitor of CYP1A1 to confirm the role of CYP1A1 in metabolism of brepocitinib and also
evaluate the impact of smoking on brepocitinib PK since cigarette smoking is known to induce
CYP1A1 and CYP1A2.
As requested by FDA, the applicant conducted in vitro experiments using pooled human liver,
lung, and intestinal microsomes but could not make definitive conclusion regarding the
contribution of CYP1A1 to brepocitinib metabolism due to the lack of specific CYP1A1 inhibitor
(Figure 2).
Figure 2. M1 formation in human microsomes [brepocitinib (2 μM), 120 minutes]*

*% M1 metabolite formation represents M1 formation at time = 120 minutes relative to parent at time = 0
(Source: Figure 1 in 1.11.3 Clinical Information Amendment (Response to 16 April 2026 IR))

Regarding the impact of smoking on PK, additional post hoc analysis showed that brepocitinib
AUC and Cmax were estimated to be 45% and 33% lower, respectively, in current smokers
compared to non-current smokers.
With the totality of evidence, FDA consider brepocitinib is mainly metabolized by CYP1A1 and
CYP1A2 with minor contribution from CYP3A4 to its metabolism.
For more details, refer to FDA Information Requests (dated 4/17/2026, 06/12/2026, 7/10/2026,
7/21/2026), the applicant’s responses (dated 4/24/2026, 6/18/2026, 7/16/2026), and
pharmacometrics review.
7

Clinical Studies
Study # B7931001
Title: A Phase 1, Within Cohort, Randomized, Double Blind, Third-Party Open, PlaceboControlled, Single- and Multiple Dose Escalation, Parallel Group Study to Evaluate the Safety,
Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics of PF-06700841 in Healthy Subjects and
Subjects With Plaque Psoriasis and Bioavailability of a Tablet Formulation Relative to
Suspension Formulation and the Effect of Food on a Tablet Formulation of PF-06700841
Study period: 27 November 2014 - 12 February 2016
Study design: This study was a phase 1 combination, within-cohort, randomized, double-blind,
third-party open, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study with single and multiple dose
escalation in healthy adult subjects, and multiple dosing in subjects with plaque psoriasis.
Subjects received PF-06700841 following an overnight fast of at least 10 hours. In addition, the
bioavailability (BA) of a tablet formulation relative the solution/suspension formulation, as well
as the effect of a high fat meal on the BA of the tablet formulation, was evaluated in a 3-way
crossover study design in 12 healthy subjects, in which study treatment administration was
separated by a period of at least 5 days. The high-calorie/high-fat breakfast consisted of 2 eggs
fried in butter, 2 strips of bacon or 50 g of meat or sausage, 2 slices of toast with butter, 4
ounces of hash brown potatoes and 8 ounces of whole milk. The breakfast should be consumed
over a 20-minute period with study drug administered within 5 minutes after completion of the
meal. No food was allowed for at least 4 hours post dose.
Figure 3. Study design (Study B7931001)

BID = twice daily; HVs = healthy volunteers; MAD = multiple-ascending; dose; QD = once daily; SAD = singleascending dose
(Source: Figure 1 of Study B7931001 CSR)

Figure 14. Forest Plot of Covariate Effects on Steady-State Brepocitinib Plasma Cmax and AUC0-24h

Colored diamonds: Median of the predicted relative change from the reference subject. Error bars: 90% CI associated with the medians. The
uncertainty was propagated by re-sampling 1,000 parameter sets from the variance covariance matrix obtained from NONMEM and simulating
1,000 exposure profiles for both the reference patient and each covariate variation. Effects are shown relative to the reference. The plotted
uncertainty reflects uncertainty in the estimated covariate effects while shared parameter uncertainty cancels out due to normalization. The
reference subject is a patient with DM administered a 30 mg brepocitinib dose, with a baseline body weight of 70 kg. The treatment effect is
presented as the relative difference to a dose level of ≤ 100 mg due to the estimated impact of higher dose levels on the relative bioavailability.
Cmax,ss: Maximum concentration at steady state. AUCss: Area under the concentration vs. time curve at steady state. CI: Confidence interval.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The submitted popPK model for brepocitinib represents an update to prior analyses,
incorporating PK data from 1,206 subjects across 10 clinical studies (12,124 observations). The
final two-compartment model with first-order absorption, lag time (tablet formulation), and
linear elimination is structurally consistent with previous models and is considered appropriate
for the studied dose range (1–200 mg).
The stepwise covariate modeling (SCM) was conducted per the pre-specified Model Analysis
Plan. Identified covariates, dose level (>100 mg) and high-fat meal on F and Ka, and body
weight on Vc/F are clinically plausible and consistent with prior analyses (Figure 14). Age (18-77
years) and Sex were not identified as significant covariate across the full population PK dataset.
Body weight affected only Cmax,ss through scaling of Vc/F, with predicted differences ranging
from −20.8% to +31% across the 40–120 kg range relative to a 70 kg reference (Figure 12). No
44

Reference ID: 5859282

effect of body weight on CL/F was identified. Because the drug is intended for chronic
administration, and because body weight across the full studied range (39–204 kg) had no
impact on CL/F, these findings support labeling language stating that body weight (39–204 kg)
does not have a clinically meaningful effect on brepocitinib exposure in the adult patient
population.
Race (White, Black, Asian, Native American, Pacific Islander, Other Race) was not identified as
significant covariate. The interindividual variability (ETA values) on the apparent clearance and
volume parameters by race (White, Black, Asian, Native American, Pacific Islander, Other Race)
did not show a significant trend. Asian race, previously identified as a covariate on CL/F, was
not statistically significant in the current SCM. Black race although not identified as a significant
covariate had higher median exposures (Figure 15, Figure 16).
An information request was sent to the Applicant to justify the significance of higher median
exposures in the Black patient population. In response, the applicant submitted additional
analyses (August 12, 2026). The sponsor provided individual treatment-emergent adverse
events (TEAE) and serious adverse events (SAE) for all 16 Black patients enrolled in PVT-2201301 (of which 11 were in the PK set). The sponsor explained that the FDA-cited 1.83-fold higher
exposure in Black vs. White patients was derived from a small subgroup (N=11) and is subject to
uncertainty. When analyzed in the full population PK dataset (N=48 Black vs. N=1,158 nonBlack), the AUC difference reduced to 1.17-fold, suggesting the 1.83-fold estimate was driven
by small sample size rather than a true PK difference. Consistent with this interpretation, ETA
distributions showed no meaningful trend by race, and no plausible mechanistic basis for a
race-related PK difference such as CYP1A or CYP3A polymorphisms was identified in the
literature. Safety data for the Black patient population did not show any exposure-response
relationship, and the safety profile appeared consistent with the overall population. The Agency
agrees with the applicant's assessment and no dose adjustment or race-specific labeling in the
USPI for Black patients is warranted.
A DM-specific reduction in CL/F (~27.8%), resulting in an estimated ~38% increase in AUCss
relative to the non-DM population with minimal change in Cmax,ss, could not be explained by
any evaluated demographic or clinical covariate (Figure 14). This is acknowledged by the
applicant.
Model diagnostics including goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 10), conditional weighted residuals
(Figure 11), VPCs (Figure 12, Figure 13), and ETA shrinkage, indicate adequate model
performance across doses and studies, including the Phase 3 DM population. A slight tendency
to underpredict concentrations in the Phase 3 DM population was noted but was not
considered to substantially impact the model's utility for exposure characterization. The
underprediction of population predictions for Study B7931009 (Japanese subjects, n=6) is
acknowledged. These finding warrants attention but does not preclude model use for NDA
support, as the effect is empirically captured in the final model.
A major limitation in the originally submitted popPK analysis (Version 1.0, dated November 24,
2025): smoking status was not evaluated as a covariate during model development, despite
smokers being included in the popPK dataset. This omission was flagged given the clinical
45

Reference ID: 5859282

relevance of smoking as an inducer of CYP1A enzymes and the potential impact on brepocitinib
clearance.
In response to the FDA's Information Request dated June 12, 2026, the applicant submitted
preliminary exposure data (June 22, 2026, Sequence 0030) demonstrating that exposure in
current smokers was approximately 50% lower than in non-smokers. The Agency subsequently
issued information request (June 24, 2026) requiring a formal update to the popPK model to
test smoking status as a covariate on CL/F. Provision of individual PK parameter estimates
(empirical Bayes estimates of CL/F, AUC, and Cmax) stratified by smoking status, along with a
discussion of clinical implications and whether dose adjustment in smokers is warranted. The
applicant's response, including the updated PopPK Report (Version 2.0/Amendment 1, Report
Number PVT-2201-301-PPK-1, dated June 30, 2026), has been reviewed by the Agency.
The updated popPK model incorporating smoking status as a covariate on CL/F is considered
acceptable. The analysis demonstrates that current smoking is associated with a statistically
significant and quantitatively meaningful increase in brepocitinib apparent clearance (~1.54fold) (Table 35), resulting in an estimated 45% and 33% reduction in steady-state AUC and
Cmax (Table 37) . The model diagnostics and parameter estimates are adequate, and the
analysis is consistent with the preliminary data submitted in Sequence 0030. The post hoc
evaluation of smoking as a covariate on CL/F was conducted appropriately, using the final
popPK model (Run 151) as the base. The large improvement in OFV (dOFV = −91.5) upon
inclusion of smoking status confirms that smoking is a statistically significant predictor of
brepocitinib clearance. The estimated 1.54-fold increase in CL/F in current smokers (95% CI:
1.39, 1.70) is estimated (Table 35) with acceptable precision and is clinically plausible given the
known role of smoking in inducing CYP1A enzymes.
The clinical significance of the smoking-related PK difference remains uncertain due to the very
small number of current smokers enrolled in the Phase 3 DM study (n = 7 in the 30 mg arm).
The updated popPK model and associated analyses are considered acceptable for NDA
submission purposes, and the model continues to provide an adequate quantitative framework
for characterizing brepocitinib PK in the DM population and for supporting labeling decisions.
Conclusion: The popPK model and associated analyses were successfully reproduced and are
considered acceptable for NDA submission purposes. Based on population (PK) analysis,
brepocitinib PK in DM population was estimated to be 38% higher AUCss relative to the nonDM population with minimal change in Cmax,ss. No dosage adjustment is recommended based
on body weight (39-204 kg), sex, age (18-77 years), race (White, Black, Asian, Native American,
Pacific Islander, Other Race). The exposure of brepocitinib in smokers was lower than in nonsmokers and therefore the effectiveness of brepocitinib may be reduced in smokers.
Exposure-Response Analysis:
Primary Efficacy Exposure-Response Analysis (TIS vs. AUC)

46

Reference ID: 5859282

A post-hoc exposure-response (E-R) analysis was conducted in the Phase 3 dermatomyositis
(DM) study (PVT-2201-301), relating individual population PK-estimated steady-state AUC (0–
24) to the primary endpoint, the Total Improvement Score (TIS), at Week 52.
Figure 17. Exposure-Response Relationship of Brepocitinib Steady-State AUC (0-24) (All Data, Left, and High AUC
Outliers1 Removed, Right) and Week 52 TIS from PVT-2201-301

Note: TIS in this figure represents the primary analysis value after accounting for intercurrent events and imputation rules (PVT-2201-301,
Listing 16.2.6.1.2). Post-hoc AUC estimates are from PVT-2201-301-PPK-1, Table A.3 and Table A.4. Participants randomized to placebo were

Reference: Summary of Clin-Pharm-Studies; Figure 6; Page no. 48
A positive exposure-response trend was observed (Figure 17), whereby higher AUC values were
associated with greater TIS improvement, with response plateauing at higher AUCs more
commonly achieved at the 30 mg dose. Considerable between-participant variability was
observed, and the loess trendline was influenced by high outlier PK estimates. A sensitivity
analysis excluding AUC values above the 95th percentile yielded consistent results.
In the primary efficacy analysis brepocitinib 30 mg QD met the primary TIS endpoint with an LS
mean difference of 14.2 points versus placebo (95% CI: 5.5, 22.9; p = 0.0013). The 15 mg QD
arm was numerically superior to placebo, with an LS mean TIS difference of 6.8 points,
consistent with a dose-response relationship; however, these results were not formally tested
for statistical significance. Overall, this exposure-response analysis was characterized as
supportive.
Conclusion: The E-R analyses collectively support brepocitinib 30 mg QD as the appropriate
therapeutic dose and demonstrate a positive exposure-efficacy relationship.

This review provides an addendum to the Supplemental review dated July 22, 2026.
Pharmacology
Nonclinical Assessment of Established Pharmacologic Class
In the supplemental review dated July 22, 2026, it was determined that the established
pharmacologic class (EPC) for brepocitinib is Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor.
However, the Applicant has argued that brepocitinib has a distinct dual TYK2/JAK1
inhibitor profile, with equipotent inhibition of both targets and substantially greater
potency at TYK2/JAK1-mediated signaling than at other JAK-mediated pathways,
making TYK2 mechanistically central to its activity in dermatomyositis, a disease driven
by type I interferon signaling. In the August 25, 2026, submission the Applicant
contended that labeling it simply as a "JAK inhibitor" understates the known
pharmacology, citing FDA’s MAPP 7400.13, regulatory precedent with deucravacitinib
(a TYK2 inhibitor), and 21 CFR 201.57(c)(13), which requires the mechanism of action
to be described to the extent known. Further, the Applicant claims potential
consequential confusion for prescribers, since labeling this as JAK inhibitor only could
lead practitioners to incorrectly assume other JAK inhibitors are also effective in DM.
Taking into consideration the Applicant’s response and in vitro data, specifically that in a
cell-free isolated enzyme assay, brepocitinib had greater inhibitory potency at TYK2 and
JAK1 than at JAK2 (≥ 3.4-fold) and JAK3 (≥ 286-fold), the EPC was ultimately
determined to be both a JAK and TYK2 inhibitor by the team. This is reflected in the
label.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
A GLP oral gavage embryo-fetal development (EFD) study of brepocitinib in pregnant
Wistar Han rats using doses of 0, 0.2, 0.5, and 1.5 mg/kg/day (Study #19GR061methods shown below) was conducted as a follow-up to the previously conducted EFD
study in rats (Study #15GR453 with brepocitinib doses of 0, 2, 5, and 15 mg/kg/day),
where a developmental NOAEL could not be determined and was <2 mg/kg due to
skeletal malformations observed at all doses.

Supplemental Pharmacology Toxicology Assessments and
Information
This review provides additional information relevant to the nonclinical assessment of
NDA 220106 for brepocitinib (referred to during development as PF-06700841 or PVT2201), a Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor indicated for dermatomyositis (DM).
The Applicant, Priovant Therapeutics, Inc., conducted a comprehensive program of
pharmacology, pharmacokinetic (PK), and toxicology studies to support clinical
development and the NDA submission of brepocitinib. The information included in this
review supports the nonclinical summary and regulatory decision for brepocitinib, found
in the Integrated Review Template under sections 5.1, 5.2, 7.1, 8.4 and 16. Detailed
reviews of all submitted studies were previously completed under IND 158707, unless
otherwise specified.
Pharmacology studies demonstrated that brepocitinib is a JAK inhibitor. Brepocitinib
inhibited human recombinant TYK2, JAK1, JAK2, and JAK3 activity with IC50 values of
22.7, 16.8, 76.6, and 6490 nM, respectively. Brepocitinib was found to be relatively
selective for TYK2, JAK1, and JAK2 compared to JAK3.
The risks identified in the toxicology studies are decreased lymphoid cellularity (in
thymus, spleen, lymph nodes, and sternal bone marrow), lung lesions (including
increased alveolar foamy macrophages, type II pneumocyte hyperplasia, and alveolar
edema), and hematological changes [decrease in leukocytes, RBCs, reticulocytes,
hemoglobin, hematocrit, total lymphocytes (such as T cells, Th cells, cytotoxic T cells),
NK cells, eosinophils, and decreased IgM and IgG responses to KLH].
In reproductive and developmental toxicity studies, reduced fecundity, fewer corpora
lutea, fewer implantation sites and increased pre-implantation loss, post-implantation
loss, and skeletal malformations were observed. These findings correlate with findings
observed with approved JAK inhibitors.
In a rat carcinogenicity study, Leydig cell tumors were observed.
The drug product does not contain any novel excipients for the oral route of
administration. Additionally, all impurities identified in the drug substance and product
are considered qualified based on the available nonclinical data.
In conclusion, NDA 220106 is recommended for approval from the pharmacology and
toxicology perspective. There are no outstanding nonclinical issues that would need a
post-marketing commitment or requirement.
Pharmacology
Nonclinical Assessment of Established Pharmacologic Class (EPC) and Potential
Effectiveness
2
Reference ID: 5839260

Brepocitinib is a small molecule inhibitor of the JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine
kinases. The JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine kinases, which include JAK1, JAK2,
JAK3, and TYK2, mediates signal transduction of cytokine receptors. Upon binding of a
cytokine to its receptor, the associated JAKs are activated and participate in the
regulation of numerous inflammatory cascades, including cytokine signaling and
production.
In in vitro studies, brepocitinib inhibited human recombinant TYK2, JAK1, JAK2, and
JAK3 activity with IC50 values of 22.7, 16.8, 76.6, and 6490 nM, respectively. Therefore,
brepocitinib was found to be relatively selective for TYK2, JAK1, and JAK2 compared to
JAK3. The IC50 values of M1 metabolite for all four enzymes were >14,000 nM,
indicating no pharmacological activity of the major metabolite against JAKs. The
Applicant claims that brepocitinib has the highest affinity for JAK1 and TYK2 and that
the most potent effects were observed with inhibition of TYK2/JAK1 signaling.
Therefore, the Applicant’s proposed pharmacologic class is selective TYK2/JAK1
inhibitor.
Brepocitinib inhibited IFNα-induced STAT3 phosphorylation mediated by the
TYK2/JAK1 signaling complex with an IC50 of 10 nM in human PBMCs, an IC50 of 1.7
nM in DM patient PBMCs, and an IC50 of 30 nM in human whole blood. Similarly, in
human whole blood, brepocitinib inhibited IL-12-induced STAT4 phosphorylation with an
IC50 of 65 nM and IL-23-induced STAT3 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 120 nM, both of
which are mediated by the TYK2/JAK2 signaling complex. In DM patient PBMCs,
brepocitinib inhibited IL-6-induced STAT1 and STAT3 phosphorylation (mediated by
TYK2/JAK1/JAK2) with IC50 values of 8.8 and 30 nM, respectively. Furthermore,
brepocitinib inhibited IL-4-induced STAT6 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 119 nM in
CD3+ T cells and 111 nM in CD14+ monocytes; in human PBMCs, it inhibited IL-15induced STAT5 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 65 nM. Both of the latter effects are
mediated by the JAK1/JAK3 signaling complex.
Although the sponsor claims that the most potent effects were observed with inhibition
of TYK2/JAK1, the IC50 values for TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and
TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 signaling reported above are all below 123 nM, which is the average
unbound plasma concentration observed in the clinical development program for DM at
a 30 mg once-daily (QD) dose. At 123 nM, brepocitinib appears capable of inhibiting
TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 signaling. Thus,
brepocitinib is not highly selective for TYK2/JAK1; rather, it has affinity for multiple JAK
kinases. Based on the totality of the data, it was determined that the established
pharmacologic class for brepocitinib is JAK inhibitor.
Cytokines implicated in DM in cell-based systems include IFNγ, IL-6, IL-12, and IL-23.
In in vitro studies, brepocitinib inhibited various TYK2- and JAK-dependent functions
across different cell types, including cytokine-induced phosphorylation of STAT proteins
in human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs, including those from DM
patients) and whole blood; differentiation of T helper (Th)1, Th17, and B cells; and IL12-induced interferon (IFN)-γ production in human PBMCs. In human skeletal muscle

3
Reference ID: 5839260

myoblasts, brepocitinib reduced IFN-I-induced myosin surface expression, and in
human dermal microvascular endothelial cells (HMEC-1), it attenuated IFN-I-induced
alterations to the structure of the microvascular network. In normal human
keratinocytes, brepocitinib decreased IFN-I- or IFNγ-induced STAT3 and STAT1
phosphorylation and reduced expression of cytokines such as IL-6, CCL2, and CXCL10.
While no animal models fully recapitulate all aspects of DM, the Applicant evaluated the
anti-inflammatory effects of brepocitinib using a rat adjuvant-induced arthritis (AIA)
disease model, which is broadly used in musculoskeletal inflammation research. In the
rat AIA model, brepocitinib reduced disease severity and hind paw swelling. The totality
of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology data submitted was considered sufficient to support
the Applicant's rationale for the use of brepocitinib as a treatment for DM.
Safety Pharmacology
A complete battery of safety pharmacology studies was conducted with brepocitinib. A
summary of the study designs and noteworthy findings is included in the table below.

4

Oral administration of PF-06700841 at 3 mg/kg produced no test
article-related changes in any parameter measured. Doses ≥10 mg/kg
produced increases in systolic and mean blood pressure, and PF06700841 dosed at 30 mg/kg caused decreases in HR and increases
in RR, QT and QTc-intervals. The NOEL was 3 mg/kg.
The PF-06700841 Cmax at 3 mg/kg was 201 ng/ml and at the 30
mg/kg was estimated to be approximately 2,000 ng/ml.
PF-06700841 at 8 mg/kg caused increases in systolic and mean
blood pressure and 36 mg/kg produced additional increases in
diastolic blood pressure as well as heart rate.
The PF-06700841 Cmax at 2 mg/kg was 182 ng/ml.

PF-06700841 at 15 mg/kg for 5 days produced higher mean blood
pressure and heart rate immediately post dose on days 1 and 3.
These effects were not observed on day 5.
The PF-06700841 Cmax at 15 mg/kg was 3050 ng/ml.

The neurofunctional assessment was conducted 30 minutes after
dosing, a time point which is approximately at the Tmax for PF06700841 in rats.
Locomotor activity was decreased in animals that received ≥20 mg/kg
PF-06700841. Mean horizontal movements decreased by 34% and
26% vs. controls in the 20 and 55 mg/kg groups respectively. Mean
vertical movements decreased by 35% and 29% vs. controls in the 20
and 55 mg/kg groups. No clear treatment-related effects on
respiratory parameters were observed. The Cmax at 55 mg/kg was
1170 ng/mL.

AUC = area under the curve; IC50 = concentration inhibiting 50% activity; NOEL = no observed effect level

ADME/PK/TK
Following a single oral administration at 3 mg/kg, brepocitinib was rapidly absorbed,
with Tmax of 0.4 h and an oral bioavailability of 110%. Following repeated oral
administration over 7 days, the mean Tmax for brepocitinib was 0.561 h, and the mean
Tmax for M1, a major human metabolite, was 1 h. The mean t½ for brepocitinib was 3.57
5
Reference ID: 5839260

h and 5.78 h for M1. Brepocitinib (2 μM) exhibited low to moderate protein binding in
plasma from mice, rabbits, rats, monkeys, and humans, with mean unbound fractions of
50.5%, 36.3%, 68.7%, 73.1%, and 60.9%, respectively. Distribution into the CNS is
expected to be limited because the partition coefficient (LogP) of brepocitinib is <2
(1.78).
Nine metabolites were qualitatively identified in rats, monkeys, and humans; no
metabolites were unique to humans. The primary mechanism of brepocitinib metabolism
was CYP-mediated oxidation (primarily hepatic CYP3A4 and CYP1A1). M1 was the
major metabolite identified in circulation (37.1%) and in urine (58.4%) in humans. No
other metabolites were present at greater than 10% of total brepocitinib exposure.
Urinary elimination is the major route of excretion, with 88% of brepocitinib excreted in
the urine and 8.7% in the feces. No studies on the excretion of brepocitinib in milk have
been conducted.
Brepocitinib and its metabolite M1 did not inhibit major CYP, UGT, or SULT enzymes,
nor did they induce CYP1A2, 2B6, or 3A4 enzymes. Brepocitinib is a substrate of
human MDR1; however, oral bioavailability was approximately 110% in a single-dose
rat study and 107% in a single-dose study in humans. It was therefore concluded that
the effect on the efflux transporter MDR1 is not clinically significant. M1 is a substrate of
OCT2 but is not a substrate of OAT1, OAT3, MATE1, or MATE2K. Brepocitinib inhibits
BCRP, MDR1, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2K. M1 is a weak inhibitor of
transporters compared to brepocitinib, with the exception of OATP1B1 and MATE2K.
The brepocitinib R values for systemic transporters exceeded the ICH M12 thresholds
for the OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2K transporters.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant conducted 4-week as well as 6-month and 9-month GLP toxicology
studies in rats and monkeys, respectively. A brief summary of the 4-week studies and a
summary of the chronic toxicology studies conducted to support clinical development is
presented below.
A 4-week study in Wistar Han rats (10/sex/group) using brepocitinib doses of 0 (0.5%
methylcellulose), 6, 20, or 55 mg/kg/day by oral gavage was conducted. Drug-related
8
Reference ID: 5839260

effects included thickened metaphyseal secondary trabecular bone in the stifle joint at
doses of ≥20 mg/kg/day. This was not considered relevant to adult human patients. In
addition, there was decreased cellularity in several lymphoid tissues (thymus, spleen,
mesenteric lymph node, inguinofemoral lymph node, and bone marrow). This is
considered a pharmacological effect of brepocitinib. The NOAEL was identified at 55
mg/kg/day.
A 4-week study in Cynomolgus monkeys (3/sex/group) using brepocitinib doses of 0
(0.5% methylcellulose), 7.5, 20, or 45 mg/kg/day by oral gavage was conducted. Drugrelated effects included decreased heart rate (10-15% versus control) that resulted in
prolonged QT intervals, and decreased cellularity in lymphoid tissues (spleen, thymus,
and bone marrow). Decreased cellularity in lymphoid tissues was attributed to
pharmacological effect of brepocitinib. The NOAEL was identified at 45 mg/kg/day.

Key Study Findings
• Brepocitinib dose-dependently decreased leukocytes, RBCs, reticulocytes,
hemoglobin, and hematocrit in males and females in all dose groups; most changes
resolved after recovery except for decreased RBCs and increased MCV/MCH in 45
mg/kg females.

were observed in males and females at ≥5 mg/kg/day.
• Decreased lymphoid cellularity of thymus, spleen, and lymph nodes occurred in both
sexes at ≥5 mg/kg/day, and decreased sternum bone marrow cellularity occurred in
males at ≥15 mg/kg/day and females at ≥5 mg/kg/day; all changes resolved after
recovery.
• NOAEL = 45 mg/kg/day. AUC0-24 = 101,000 ng*hr/ml (observed findings were
considered monitorable in a clinical setting).

Key Study Findings
• Decreased total lymphocytes (T cells, Th cells, cytotoxic T cells), NK cells, and

• High-dose males and females showed decreased IgM and IgG responses to
