considered monitorable in a clinical setting). Note that AUC value is female-only
from day 272.
Table 5 summarizes the nonclinical exposure data for brepocitinib (and its M1
metabolite) across repeat-dose toxicity studies conducted in rats and monkeys. Safety
margins were derived by comparing nonclinical exposures to clinical exposures at the
30 mg once-daily dose. The safety margins from the pivotal 6-month rat study and 9month monkey study are approximately 49.8-fold and 1.9-fold, respectively, compared
to the clinical exposure. Notably, the monkey 9-month safety margin is calculated using
AUC data from only female monkeys due to markedly decreased exposures after Day
92 in males and dose-limiting findings observed only in females. M1 is the major
metabolite and was not measured in pivotal GLP-toxicology studies. Its safety margin
was calculated from a rat 7-day PK study, yielding a 12-fold margin relative to the
clinical M1 exposure of 2,252 ng*h/mL.

Genetic Toxicology
Brepocitinib was assessed in a battery of genotoxicity studies, including the Ames test,
in vitro micronucleus assays, a fluorescent in situ hybridization (FISH) assay, and an in
vivo micronucleus assay. Brepocitinib was negative in the Ames assay but positive in
the in vitro micronucleus assays using human TK6 and Chinese hamster ovary (CHO)
cells after 24–27 hours without metabolic activation. Results from a follow-up FISH
study indicate that brepocitinib is an in vitro aneugen (whole chromosome loss).
However, brepocitinib did not induce micronuclei in vivo in rat peripheral blood cells.
The totality of data indicates that brepocitinib is negative for genotoxicity. A tabular
summary of the studies is presented below.

There was no evidence for treatment-related increase in micronuclei at the 4hour time point. The 27 hour incubation was conducted in the absence of S9
metabolic activation. PF- 06700841 doses tested included 4.27 – 18.0 μg/ml.
The highest concentration showed 59% cytotoxicity. 18.0 μg/ml PF-06700841
treatment resulted in significantly increased micronucleus formation. The 2.0%
mean micronucleus formation exceeds the historical control value of 0.38 ±
0.26% for 27 hr –S9 treatment.
A non-GLP fluorescent in situ hybridization (FISH) study (Study# ETK6201328 FISH) established that PF- 06700841 acts as an aneugen. Daughter cells
are observed with whole chromosome loss rather than chromosome breakage.
PF-06700841 induces micronucleus formation by disruption of spindle
apparatus formation during mitosis rather than interaction with DNA and strand
breakage.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant conducted a full battery of reproductive and developmental studies,
including male and female fertility in rats, embryo-fetal development in rats and rabbits,
and pre- and post-natal development in rats. A summary of each of these studies is
presented below.

Key Study Findings
• External malformations included cleft palate (one fetus), whole body subcutaneous

variations were detected.

and femur at ≥2 mg/kg; radius, ulna at ≥5 mg/kg; fibula and tibia at 15 mg/kg) and

sternebrae, a bent scapula, and a cleft palatine.

(≥2 mg/kg), and at 15 mg/kg, delayed skeletal ossification and structural rib and
sternebrae changes.

mg/kg.
• The developmental NOAEL could not be determined and is <2 mg/kg due to skeletal
malformations at all doses, lower embryofetal viability, and lower mean fetal body
weights at 15 mg/kg.

Key Study Findings
•

At 30 mg/kg/day, two females died (exact reason could not be determined).

•

At 30 mg/kg/day, brepocitinib caused impaired use and malrotation of hindlimbs,
both of which persisted into the recovery period.

•

At ≥5 mg/kg/day, brepocitinib caused dose-dependent decreases in the weights
of the thymus and spleen in both sexes. After recovery, thymus weights
remained lower at 30 mg/kg/day in both sexes, and spleen weights remained
lower in males but recovered in females.
In males, brepocitinib decreased femur length at all doses and femur width at 30
mg/kg/day. There were no femur changes in females. After recovery, lower femur
length remained at 30 mg/kg/day.
At 15 mg/kg/day, small femoral head was observed. After recovery, absence of
the femoral head and malrotated paws were observed at 30 mg/kg/day.
There was decreased primary spongiosa of femur and tibia and decreased
lymphoid cellularity in thymus at 15 mg/kg/day, abnormal morphology of femoral
head and decreased lymphoid cellularity in the splenic white pulp at 30
mg/kg/day and decreased extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen at all
doses. Most findings were absent after the recovery period.
The NOAEL was 5 mg/kg/day on PND 49.

1 Background
1.1 Regulatory History
Brepocitinib was initially developed by Pfizer Inc., and the worldwide development and
U.S. commercialization rights were subsequently acquired by Priovant Therapeutics Inc.
on September 13, 2021. A Type B pre-NDA meeting request was submitted to IND
158707 on April 15, 2025, with written responses provided on May 23, 2025. On May
25, 2025, brepocitinib received Orphan Drug Designation (ODD) for the treatment of
dermatomyositis (DM), thereby exempting the program from pediatric study
requirements under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA). Priovant submitted NDA
220106 under Section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act to market
brepocitinib for treatment of DM.

4 Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
The JAK family of cytoplasmic tyrosine kinases, that include JAK1, JAK2, JAK3, and
TYK2, mediate signal transduction of cytokine receptors (Figure 1). Upon binding of the
cytokine to its receptor, the associated JAKs are activated, and phosphorylate each
other and the receptor at specific tyrosine sites. The phosphorylated receptors serve as
docking sites for the signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) family
(STAT1, STAT2, STAT3, STAT4, STAT5a, STAT5b, and STAT6) of transcription
factors. The STATs are then phosphorylated by the co-localized JAKs, which stabilize
homo- or heterodimeric complexes that translocate to the nucleus where they bind to
specific binding sites and modulate transcription of a range of target genes.
Figure 1. Receptor Pairing Within the Janus Kinase Family

5
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

In the cell-free kinase assays, TYK2 was inhibited by 98% with 1 μM brepocitinib and 3
of 156 kinases were inhibited by >50% [JAK1 (96%), JAK2 (94%), TNK1 (64%)]. At 1
mM ATP, the IC50 of brepocitinib (PF-06700841) was 16.8 nM for human JAK1, 76.6 nM
for human JAK2, 6490 nM for human JAK3, and 22.7 nM for human TYK2. The IC50 of
brepocitinib for mouse TYK2 was 772 nM, 287 nM for the human TYK2 I960V mutant,
and 63.9 nM for the mouse TYK2 V953I mutant. These results indicate that brepocitinib
has a stronger interaction with isoleucine than valine at amino acid 960 and, as a
consequence, has lower affinity for TYK2 in nonclinical species relative to human (Study
#102201).
In the cell-based kinase assay (238 kinases) with 1 μM brepocitinib, TYk2 was inhibited
by 91%, JAK1 was inhibited by 90% and 5 kinases out of 238 were inhibited by >50%
[RSK1(88%), RSK3 (83%), QSK (75%), ERK5 (67%), and RSK2 (67%)].
Both the cell-free and cell-based kinase assays were performed with 1 μM brepocitinib,
which is about 10-fold higher than the human unbound Cav of a 30 mg QD dose (~123
nM) in the clinical development program for DM. Therefore in vivo off target
pharmacology or toxicity based on the above targets would be limited.
The selectivity of brepocitinib was evaluated in vitro using human PBMCs, and human-,
rat-, and monkey-whole blood. The activity of breprocitinib against interleukin (IL)induced phosphorylation of STATs in all cell types is summarized in Table 1 (Study
#090414, #102612, #101046, #103030, #102736, #PVT-2201-903).
TYK2/JAK1
In human whole blood, brepocitinib resulted in an inhibition of IFNα-induced STAT3
phosphorylation with an IC50 of 30 nM, and in human PBMCs, brepocitinib inhibited
IFNα-induced STAT3 signaling with an IC50 of 10 nM. Brepocitinib was less potent
against IL-10-induced STAT3 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 305 nM in human whole
blood.
TYK2/JAK2

6
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

Treatment of human whole blood with brepocitinib resulted in an inhibition of IL-12induced STAT4 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 65 nM and IL-23-induced STAT3
phosphorylation with an IC50 of 120 nM. However, brepocitinib inhibited IL-12 induced
IFNγ production in PBMCs with an IC50 of 261 nM.
JAK1/JAK2/TYK2
IL-6 and IL-27 signaling is mediated by JAK1 and JAK2 or TYK2. IL-6-induced STAT1
and STAT3 phosphorylation were inhibited by brepocitinib with IC50 values of 81 nM and
641 nM, respectively, in CD3+ lymphocytes of human whole blood. Similarly, IL-27induced STAT3 phosphorylation was also inhibited by brepocitinib with an IC50 of 86
nM.
JAK1/JAK3
Brepocitinib inhibited IL-4 induced STAT6 phosphorylation with an IC50 of 119 nM (in
CD3+ T cells), 111 nM (in CD14+ monocytes), and 295 nM (in CD20+ B cells). In
human PBMCs brepocitinib inhibited STAT5 phosphorylation induced by IL-15 with an
IC50 of 65nM. In human whole blood, brepocitinib inhibited STAT5 phosphorylation
induced by IL-15 with an IC50 of 238 nM and inhibited STAT3 phosphorylation induced
by IL-21 with an IC50 of 204 nM.

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

Cytokines implicated in dermatomyositis include IFNγ, IL-6, IL-12, and IL-23 in cellbased systems. Brepocitinib inhibited various TYK2- and JAK-dependent functions in
different cell types, such as cytokine-induced phosphorylation of STAT proteins in
human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs; including those from DM patients)
and whole blood; differentiation of T helper (Th)1, Th17, and B cells; and interleukin
(IL)-12-induced interferon (IFN) γ production in human PBMCs.
Signaling of thrombopoietin (TPO)-mediated production of platelets is mediated by the
JAK2 signaling cascade. TPO-induced STAT5 phosphorylation (a solely JAK2dependent process) and IL-6-induced STAT3 phosphorylation were inhibited by
brepocitinib with IC50 values of 114 nM and 78 nM, respectively. Brepocitinib
suppressed the expansion of CD34+ progenitor cells with an IC50 value of 3210 nM
(Study #094745). However, brepocitinib reduced TPO clearance by 10% in human
platelet-rich plasma with an IC50 of 85 nM from 1 of the 3 donors (Study #033455).
The sponsor claims that the most potent effects were observed with inhibition of
TYK2/JAK1 signaling and suppression of IFNα-stimulated phosphorylation of STAT3.
IL4-induced phosphorylation of STAT6 (mediated by JAK1/JAK3) is also observed at
119 (CD3+ cells) and 111 (CD14+ cells) nM. In the clinical development program for DM,
at a 30 mg dose QD, the average unbound plasma concentration is 123 nM. At this
concentration, it appears that brepocitinib can inhibit TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2,
JAK1/JAK3, and TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 mediated signaling. Thus, brepocitinib is not highly
selective for TYK2/JAK1.
Pharmacodynamic Inhibition of IFN-I Induced Damage in Human Cell Culture
Systems
Dysregulation of Type I interferon (IFN-I) signaling and other cytokines (IFNγ, IL-12, and
IL-23) is implicated in DM. Brepocitinib at both 130 nM and 1 μM prevented INF-Iinduced decrease in myosin surface in human skeletal muscle myoblasts (Study
#ROI22-ASSAY-001-ul-HSMM) (Figure 2). IFN-I-induced alteration to the structure of
the microvascular network in human dermal microvascular endothelial cells (HMEC-1)
was reversed by brepocitinib at 130 nM and 1 μM (Study #ROI22-ASSAY-001-ulHMEC) (Figure 2).
Figure 2. Effect of Brepocitinib Pretreatment on IFN-I Induced Damage in Myotubes and
Endothelial Cells (HMEC-1)

8
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

Brepocitinib significantly reduced STAT3 and STAT1 phosphorylation following
stimulation with either IFN-I or IFNγ, and also decreased expression of cytokines such
as IL-6, CCL2, and CXCL10 in normal human epidermal keratinocytes (Study #PRV-2405).
The pharmacodynamic activity of M1 and M2 metabolites of brepocitinib was also
assessed. The IC50 values for the M1 metabolite against four JAK isoforms were
>14,000 nM, which was approximately 77-fold higher than the human M1 Cav (181 nM
after brepocitinib 30 mg QD) (Study #014449). Similarly, the M2 metabolite suppressed
IFNγ-induced STAT1 phosphorylation with an IC50 >60,000 nM, IL-27-induced STAT3
phosphorylation with an IC50 of 20,500 nM, and IL-6-induced STAT1 and STAT6
phosphorylation with IC50 values of 9,770 nM and >60,000 nM, respectively. The M2
metabolite constitutes <0.5% of circulating brepocitinib (Study #033249). Hence it was
concluded that circulating metabolites do not contribute to any pharmacologic activity in
vivo.
The efficacy of brepocitinib was evaluated in a rat adjuvant-induced arthritis (AIA) model
(Study #103607). Rats were administered 3, 10, or 30 mg/kg/day or vehicle for 7
consecutive days after disease onset (hind paw swelling). Brepocitinib significantly
reduced hind paw swelling compared to the control group (Figure 3).
Figure 3. Dose Response Efficacy Study of Brepocitinib in the Rat AIA Model

Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

Pharmacokinetics
Absorption
Following a single IV administration of 1 mg/kg brepocitinib in rats, the mean CL was
approximately 31 mL/min/kg, Vss was approximately 2 L/kg, and the elimination half-life
(t½) was approximately 1.1 hours. Brepocitinib was rapidly absorbed following a single
3 mg/kg oral administration in rats with a Tmax = 0.4 hours. Oral bioavailability was
estimated to be 110%, indicative of no presystemic metabolism.
After repeated administration for 7 days, the mean brepocitinib Cmax was 9670 ng/mL,
and the mean M1 Cmax was 2650 ng/mL. The mean Tmax for brepocitinib was 0.561
hours, and for M1, the mean Tmax was 1 hour. The mean t½ for brepocitinib was 3.57
hours and was 5.78 hours for M1. The brepocitinib mean AUC(0-24) was 107,000
ng·h/mL and was 27,200 ng·h/mL for M1.
The potential of human MDR1 and BCRP to transport brepocitinib was evaluated by
measuring the bidirectional permeability in vitro in MDCK cells. The results indicated
that brepocitinib was a substrate of human MDR1 but not BCRP (Study #015403 and
#042024). However, the oral bioavailability of brepocitinib in rats was 110%, suggesting
a lack of effect in vivo (Study #085142).
Distribution
The plasma protein binding and blood-to-plasma ratios were calculated in plasma or
whole blood of humans and nonclinical species (Table 2). PF-06700841 (2 μM)
exhibited low to moderate protein binding in plasma from mice, rabbits, rats, monkeys,
and humans. Distribution into the central nervous system is expected to be limited
because the partition coefficient (LogP) of brepocitinib is <2 (1.78).
Table 2. Plasma Protein Binding and Blood:Plasma Partitioning

10
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

Metabolism and Excretion
Metabolism of brepocitinib was evaluated in rats, monkeys, and humans using liver
microsomes, plasma, and urine (Study #153827). Proposed metabolism routes are
shown in Figure 4. The primary metabolite detected was a hydroxylation at the C5
position of the pyrazole ring (M1, PF-06802530). Other metabolites include M2 (PF06769777 formed by loss of the N-methyl pyrazole ring), M4 (formed by Ndemethylation), M8 (hydroxylation followed by glutathione conjugation), M5 (opening of
the diazabicyclo-octane ring), and M6 (hydroxylation followed by glucuronidation on the
diazabicyclo-octane ring). All metabolites detected in nonclinical species were present
in humans. A glucuronide conjugate (M10, 0.3% in plasma) of a minor oxidative
metabolite (M4, 3.6% in plasma) was observed in human plasma but not in animal
plasma.
In humans, the total recovery of the orally administered radioactive dose was 96.7%,
with 88.0% in the urine and 8.7% in the feces, which suggests that urinary elimination is
the major route of elimination. M1 was the major metabolite identified in circulation
(37.1%) and in urine (58.4%). No other metabolites were present at greater than 10% of
total brepocitinib exposure. Following IV administration, the percent of dose excreted as
unchanged brepocitinib in urine and bile was approximately 2.2% and 0.86%,
respectively (Study #085142). No studies on the excretion of brepocitinib in milk have
been conducted.
In summary, the primary mechanism for brepocitinib metabolism was through CYPmediated oxidation (primarily hepatic CYP3A4 and CYP1A1).
Figure 4. Proposed Biotransformation Routes of Brepocitinib in Rat, Monkey, and Human Liver
Microsomes and Hepatocytes and Rat and Monkey Plasma and Urine (Study #153827)

(Excerpted from Sponsor’s submission)

Based on an AUC ratio, M1 exposure is approximately 12-fold higher in rat plasma
(Study #PVT-2201-901) after dosing rats to steady state at 45 mg/kg/day (the NOAEL
from the 6-month rat toxicity study 15MA023) compared to human plasma after a 30 mg
dose in humans (Study B7931033) [27,200 ng·hr/mL AUC(0-24) in rats and 2,231
ng·hr/mL AUClast in humans].
Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
The IC50 of brepocitinib and its metabolite M1 for the inhibition of CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8,
2C9, 2C19, 2D6, or 3A4/5 activities were >69 μM (Studies #084405 and #085351). This
concentration corresponds to 154-fold greater than the unbound clinical Cmax at 30 mg
QD (0.448 μM brepocitinib and 0.414 μM M1). Brepocitinib (up to 69.1 μM) did not
inhibit UDP-glucuronosyltransferase 1A1, 1A4, 1A6, 1A9, or 2B7 enzyme activity in
human liver microsomes (Study #155254), and at concentrations up to 100 μM, it did
not inhibit sulfotransferase (SULT) 1E1, 1A1, or 2A1 (Study #014637) in human liver
cytosol. Brepocitinib did not induce CYP1A2, 2B6, or 3A4 (Study #093836). Thus, it was
concluded that brepocitinib and its metabolite M1 did not inhibit major CYPs, UGT, or
SULT enzymes nor induce CYP1A2, 2B6, or 3A4 enzymes.
Brepocitinib is not a substrate for breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) (Study
#042024), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, or OATP1B3 (Study
#121752). However, brepocitinib is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp/MDR1/ABCB1)
(Study #015403). The oral bioavailability of breprocitinib was approximately 110% in a

12
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

single-dose rat study (Study #085142) and 107% in a single-dose study in humans
(Study #B7931014). Since dose proportionality was observed in Cmax and AUC in
humans, it was concluded that the effect on efflux transporter P-gp was not clinically
significant. M1 is a substrate of OCT2 but not a substrate of OAT1 or OAT3 and not of
MATE1 or MATE2K (Study #011116).
The potential inhibition of transporters by brepocitinib and the M1 metabolite was
assessed (Studies #192929, #100621, #020653) (Tables 3 and 4). To evaluate the risk
of transporter inhibition, R values were calculated as shown in Table 3 and 4.
Brepocitinib inhibits BCRP, MDR1, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2K. M1 is a weak
inhibitor of transporters compared to brepocitinib, except for transporters OATP1B1 and
MATE2K.
The brepocitinib R values for systemic transporters were above the ICH M12 thresholds
for the OCT1, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2K transporters.

Nonclinical Discussion of Established Pharmacologic Class
(EPC)
At 1 mM ATP, the IC50 of brepocitinib (PF-06700841) was 16.8 nM for human JAK1,
76.6 nM for human JAK2, 6490 nM for human JAK3, and 22.7 nM for human TYK2.
14
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

The sponsor claims that brepocitinib has the highest affinity to JAK1 and TYK2 and the
most potent effects were observed with inhibition of TYK2/JAK1 signaling. Therefore,
the Applicant’s proposed EPC is selective TYK2 inhibitor/JAK1 inhibitor.
TYK2 is a member of the JAK family of intracellular tyrosine kinases that relay signals
from specific cytokines to the nucleus, regulating immune and inflammatory responses.
Table 5 shows the comparison of IC50 ratios between various JAK- and TYK2-inhibitors
that are currently marketed in the US or have been submitted for a marketing
application. The IC50 ratios for brepocitinib for JAK1, JAK2, and TYK2 are less than 5fold.

Brepocitinib resulted in inhibition of IFNα-induced STAT3 phosphorylation mediated by
the TYK2/JAK1 signaling complex with an IC50 of 10 nM in human PBMCs, an IC50 of
1.7 nM in DM patient PBMCs, and an IC50 of 30 nM in human whole blood. Similarly, in

15
Reference ID: 5803877

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

human whole blood, brepocitinib also resulted in an inhibition of IL-12-induced STAT4
phosphorylation with an IC50 of 65 nM and IL-23-induced STAT3 phosphorylation with
an IC50 of 120 nM, both of which are mediated by the TYK2/JAK2 signaling complex. In
DM patient PBMCs, brepocitinib inhibited IL-6 induced STAT1 and STAT3
phosphorylation (mediated by TYK2/JAK1/JAK2) with IC50 of 8.8 and 30 nM,
respectively. Furthermore, brepocitinib also inhibited IL-4 induced STAT6
phosphorylation with an IC50 of 119 nM (in CD3+ T cells), and 111 nM (in CD14+
monocytes), and in human PBMCs, it inhibited STAT5 phosphorylation induced by IL-15
with an IC50 of 65nM, both of which are mediated by JAK1/JAK3 signaling complex.
Even though the sponsor claims that the most potent effects were observed with
inhibition of TYK2/JAK1, the IC50 values for TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3, and
TYK2/JAK1/JAK2 signaling above are all below 123 nM, which is the average unbound
plasma concentration in the clinical development program for DM at a 30 mg dose QD.
At 123 nM, it appears that brepocitinib can inhibit TYK2/JAK1, TYK2/JAK2, JAK1/JAK3
and TYK2/JAK1/JAK2. Thus, brepocitinib is not highly selective for TYK2/JAK1 but has
affinity for JAK kinases and can therefore be categorized as a JAK inhibitor. The EPC
assessment was discussed with Dr. Stephanie Leuenroth-Quinn from OND IO and she
agreed with JAK inhibitor as the EPC for this drug.

Reviewer Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Positive
ECAC Carcinogenicity Conclusion (negative/ positive): Positive
Tumor Findings: Administration of vehicle, 3, 10, or 30 mg/kg/day PF-06700841 daily
via oral gavage for up to 104 weeks resulted in increased mortality in males due to
infections secondary to immunosuppression and benign testicular interstitial cell (Leydig
cell) tumors in males. Leydig cell tumor incidence was significantly higher in HD males
(Trend analysis p=0.0026; Pairwise analysis p= 0.0104) with an incidence of 13%
compared to the control group (2%), historical controls (up to 5%), and published
literature (up to 10%). The sponsor identified the interstitial Leydig cell adenomas as
drug related. The Leydig tumors were also associated with increased mortality in the
HD males. This result was confirmed by the FDA statistical review. No brepocitinib
related mortality or neoplasms were observed in female rats.

Alveolar proteinosis was observed in HD males and MD and HD females compared to
the vehicle control group (Table 9). Alveolar proteinosis is characterized by the
accumulation of acellular, eosinophilic, amorphous to slightly granular material in
alveolar spaces, morphologically consistent with an accumulation of surfactant
protein/phospholipids normally produced in alveoli, sometimes with accumulations of
foamy macrophages.

A test article-related higher incidence of infection/sepsis-related deaths was observed in

Nonclinical Discussion
Administration of PF-06700841 by oral gavage for 104 weeks at 0, 3, 10, or 30
mg/kg/day resulted in a statistically significant increase in mortality in HD males.
Mortality in HD males was associated with bacterial infections and benign Leydig cell
tumors in the testis. The Leydig cell tumors are attributed to JAK2 inhibition and
alteration of the prolactin signaling. A similar dose-related increased incidence of
testicular Leydig cell hyperplasia and benign adenomas were noted with tofacitinib,
another JAK inhibitor [Chapin, 2017, drug label for XELJANZ® (tofacitinib) tablets].
Chronic inhibition of prolactin signaling can increase luteinizing hormone levels and has
been attributed to an increase in interstitial cell (Leydig cell) hyperplasia and adenomas
in rats (Prentice, 1992; Cook, 1999). Because prolactin receptors are either not
expressed or are expressed at very low levels in the testes in humans, the induction of
Leydig cell tumors in rats may not be relevant to humans (Cook, 1999).
Other neoplastic changes include benign follicular cell adenoma and carcinoma of the
thyroid gland in the HD female group. Due to the absence of a statistically significant
trend, it was deemed inconclusive.
Thymomas were observed in female rats in the LD and MD groups and thymomas are a
common background tumor in female Wistar rats (Taylor and Mowat, 2020). In addition,
due to lack of dose response and statistical significance, thymomas were not
considered related to the test article.
Non-neoplastic microscopic findings included decreased cellularity in the thymus in all
dose groups in both sexes; CPN in the kidney of males in all dose groups; alveolar
proteinosis in HD males and MD and HD females; and a higher incidence of
infection/sepsis-related deaths were observed in HD males. Decreased cellularity of
lymphocytes, immunosuppression, and alveolar proteinosis was attributed to
pharmacology of the drug and immunosuppression. A similar decrease in lymphoid

11
Reference ID: 5785500

IND # 158707

Reviewer: Swetha Rudraiah

tissue cellularity and immunosuppression was also evidenced in 6-month repeat dose
study in rats.

F0 Dams
Mortality
One female in the mid-dose group (MD) was found dead on GD18. On LD9, one female
in the high-dose group (HD) was euthanized due to head tilt and lack of righting reflex.
No abnormalities were observed during necropsy in either animal; therefore, the deaths
were considered unrelated to brepocitinib.
Clinical Observations
Clinical observations included dehydration, hunched posture, thin fur cover (cranium,
dorsal aspect, dorsal cervical region, left or right forelimb), thin appearance, head tilt,
salivation, and lack of or impaired righting reflex. These observations were not
considered to be test article-related because they were observed in control groups, did
not persist, and the incidence was low.
Maternal Body Weight and Body Weight Gains
No brepocitinib-related changes in body weights or body weight gains were observed in
gestating or lactating F0 generation females.
Maternal Food Consumption
No brepocitinib-related changes in food consumption were observed in gestating or
lactating F0 generation females.
Natural Delivery and Litter Observations
The pregnancy rates in the control, LD, MD, and HD groups were 95.5% (21), 95.5%
(21), 90.9% (20), and 95.5% (21), respectively. All pregnant dams delivered litters
except for one female in the MD group and one female in the LD group that had no
liveborn pups. Two females in the control group and one female in the HD group were
euthanized due to no surviving pups. There were 19 litters in all dose groups, including
the control group, for analysis. The natural delivery observations are shown in Table 1.
No brepocitinib-related changes were observed in any of the parameters evaluated. In
addition, no brepocitinib-related effects on maternal behavior observations during
lactation were observed.

The following clinical observations were observed in the HD group but with lower

Pup Body Weights
No brepocitinib-related changes in body weights were observed. A statistically
significant decrease in body weight was observed in males in the MD and HD groups
from PND 7 and in females in the HD group from PND 7. On PND 21, male and female
(combined) body weights were 100%, 94%, and 91% of the control group mean value in
the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively.
Pup Reflex and Physical Development
No statistically significant brepocitinib-related effects on pup reflex and physical
development parameters were observed.
Pup Macroscopic Pathology
On PND 21, one pup in the HD group had a hernia in the diaphragm and a
malpositioned middle lobe of the liver. Because of the low incidence (1/95), this finding
was considered unrelated to brepocitinib.
F1 Generation Postweaning observations
Mortality and Clinical Observations
No mortality was observed in postweaning F1 generation, and all animals survived to
scheduled euthanasia. Clinical signs observed were rare in occurrence and included:
red fur staining in the dorsal cervical area; thin fur cover (dorsal aspect [generalized],
dorsal cervical region, left axillary region, left or right forelimb, muzzle, abdominal area,
and ventral aspect [generalized]); scabs on the skin (cranium, right forelimb, right hind
paw, and lumbar area); and generalized shivering.
Body Weights and Body Weight Changes
In rats administered MD, body weights decreased significantly on PND 22 and
continued until PND 36 compared to the control group. Similarly, the body weights of
rats in the HD group also decreased significantly on PND 22 and continued until PND
64. Mean body weights in F1 generation males on PND 120 were 99%, 96% and 94%
of the control group in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. The change was less
than 10%; therefore, the body weight changes were not considered adverse.
In F1 female rats on PND 92, the mean body weights were 99%, 97%, and 98% of the
control group in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. On GD 13, the mean body
weights were 102%, 100%, and 101% of the control group in the LD, MD, and HD,
groups, respectively.
Food Consumption
No significant changes were observed in food consumption for F1 generation male or
female rats in any of the dose groups tested.

In males, a significant decrease in body weight was observed in the MD and HD groups

In MD and HD female rats, a significant decrease in the average response

No changes in mating and fertility parameters in male and female rats were observed.

No brepocitinib-related gross lesions were observed in the rats.
Ovarian and Uterine Examinations
The pregnancy rates were 17 (89.5%), 13 (68.4%), 17 (94.4%), and 18 (100%) in the
control, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. Ovarian and uterine examinations were
performed on all F1 generation females on GD 13, and no significant changes were
noted compared to the control group.
Toxicokinetics in Lactating Rats
The toxicokinetics of brepocitinib were evaluated in plasma samples collected from
lactating rats on LD 14. Following daily brepocitinib administration via oral gavage to
female rats, mean Cmax and AUC0-7hr values of brepocitinib increased with increasing
dose in an approximately dose-proportional manner. While tmax values were observed
by 0.5 hours, brepocitinib was quantifiable up to 7 hours post-dose in lactating rats at all
dose levels.

Summary
Pregnant female Wistar Han rats (F0 generation) were given brepocitinib at 0, 0.5, 2,
and 5 mg/kg (control, LD, MD, and HD) by oral gavage from gestation day (GD) 6 to
lactation day (LD) 20. No brepocitinib-related adverse effects were observed in the F0
generation. Therefore, the F0 NOAEL was determined to be 5 mg/kg/day. On LD 14,
the mean concentrations found in plasma increased with increasing dose in an
approximately dose-proportional manner. While tmax values were observed by 0.5 hours,
brepocitinib was quantifiable up to 7 hours post-dose in lactating rats at all dose levels.
In pups, a reduction in body weight was observed in male pups but was considered not
adverse. No effects on other developments parameters such as sexual maturation,
neurobehavioral, or reproductive function were observed. Therefore, the F1
NOAEL was determined to be 5 mg/kg/day. At 3 hours post dose on PND 14,

1. Summary
In this submission the sponsor included a report of carcinogenicity study in rats. These study
was intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of PF-06700841, when administered daily
via oral gavage to Crl:WI(Han) rats at appropriate drug levels for 104 weeks.
Rat Study:
Survival data analyses:
1.

For male rats, the survival analysis showed statistically significant dose response
relationships in mortality across the vehicle control group and the treated groups
(p-value for likelihood ratio test is 0.0177). The pairwise comparisons showed
statistically significant difference in mortality between the vehicle control group
and the high dose group.

2.

For female rats, the survival analysis showed statistically significant dose
response relationships in mortality across the vehicle control group and the
treated groups (p-value for likelihood ratio test is 0.0177). The pairwise
comparisons showed statistically significant difference in mortality between the
vehicle control group and the low dose group.

Tumor data analysis:
1.

For male rats, the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive
dose response relationship in the incidence of B-interstitial cell tumor, testis (pvalue=0.0026) across the vehicle control and the treated groups.

2.

For female rats, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
low dose group showed statistically significant increase in the incidence of Bthymoma benign, thymus (p-value=0.005). The pairwise comparisons between
the vehicle control and the mid dose group showed statistically significant
increases in the incidences of B-thymoma benign, thymus (p-value=0.0093).

2. Background
In this submission the sponsor included a report of carcinogenicity study in rats. These study
was intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of PF-06700841, when administered daily
via oral gavage to Crl:WI(Han) rats at appropriate drug levels for 104 weeks.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as the dose increases.

3. Rat Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Two
hundred and forty rats of each sex were randomly assigned to the four groups in equal size
of 60 rats per group. The dose levels for treated groups were 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day for
both males and females as shown in Table 1. The study for the rats was designed to
continue for up to 104 weeks.

Sponsor's analyses

3.1.1. Survival analysis
Tests to compare survival were conducted, with a 2-sided risk for increasing or decreasing
mortality with dose. Tests were conducted for dose response along with pairwise comparisons
for each dosed group against the control group using Kaplan-Meier productlimit estimates,
along with log-rank and Wilcoxon tests. These were conducted using the LIFETEST procedure
in SAS. The dose group was included as the stratum. Animals with a death or euthanasia

Reference ID: 5764018

NDA 220106

Page 6 of 23

status recorded as a scheduled terminal euthanasia or accidental death were censored in the
analysis.
Sponsor’s findings: Sponsor’s analysis showed the numbers (percents) of survival were 30
(50%), 25 (60%), 31 (52%), and 19 (32%) in vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose groups,
respectively in males, and 26 (43%), 42 (70%), 33 (55%), and 23 (38%) in vehicle control,
low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively in females.
The sponsor concluded that
1. For males, the trend analysis was statistically significant. Males administered 30
mg/kg/day had a total survival of 32% that was statistically significantly lower compared
with the control males which had a survival of 50%.
2. For females, the trend analysis was statistically significant; however, the pairwise
comparison was not. Therefore, there was no PF-06700841-related effect in females at
30 mg/kg/day compared with the control group.

3.1.2. Tumor data analysis
Tests to compare tumor incidence were conducted with a 1-sided risk for increasing
incidence with dose. Tests were conducted for a dose response along with pairwise
comparisons for each dosed group against the control group.
Unadjusted P-values were reported for tumors. An indication of a possible dose effect was
assessed on the basis of whether the tumor is a rare or common type, in line with the
current FDA guidelines (Food and Drug Administration, 2001) and Expanded Statistical
Decision Rules for Interpretations of Results of Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of
Pharmaceuticals (Lin and Rahman, 2018). The trend test was used for interpretation of the
results; the pairwise test results were only produced to assist in determining a no observed
effect level and were not utilized to determine the significance or non-sgnificance of any
tumor. The study pathologist determined whether a tumor type is rare or common.

Reference ID: 5764018

NDA 220106

Page 7 of 23

Sponsor’s findings: Interstitial cell tumor (Leydig cell tumor; adenoma) was statistically
significantly higher (trend p-value = 0.0025) in males administered 30 mg/kg/day compared
to the control group.

3.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses.

3.2.1. Survival analysis
The survival distributions of animals in five groups were estimated by the Kaplan-Meier
product limit method. The dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival
distributions were tested for the vehicle control, low, medium and high dose groups using the
Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in
Tables 6 and 7 in the appendix for males and females, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves
for survival rate are given in Figures 1 and 2 in the appendix for males and females,
respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals,
are given in Tables 8 to 9 in the appendix for males and females, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percents) of survival
were 30 (50%), 25 (60%), 31 (52%), and 19 (32%) in vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose
groups, respectively in males, and 26 (43%), 42 (70%), 33 (55%), and 23 (38%) in vehicle
control, low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively in females.
For male rats, the survival analysis showed statistically significant dose response relationships
in mortality across the vehicle control group and the treated groups (p-value for likelihood
ratio test is 0.0177). The pairwise comparisons showed statistically significant difference in
mortality between the vehicle control group and the high dose group.
For female rats, the survival analysis showed statistically significant dose response
relationships in mortality across the vehicle control group and the treated groups (p-value for
likelihood ratio test is 0.0177). The pairwise comparisons showed statistically significant
difference in mortality between the vehicle control group and the low dose group.

3.2.2. Tumor data analysis
The tumor data were analyzed for the positive dose response relationships and the pairwise
comparison between each of the treated groups with control groups for increased tumor
incidences. Both the dose response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons were
performed using the Poly-K method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and
Bieler and Williams (1993). In this method an animal that lives the full study period ( wmax ) or
dies before the terminal sacrifice but develops the tumor type being tested gets a score of sh
=1. An animal that dies at week wh without a tumor before the end of the study gets a score
k

 w 
of sh =  h  < 1. The adjusted group size is defined as Σ sh . As an interpretation, an
 wmax 
animal with score sh =1 can be considered as a whole animal while an animal with score sh <
1 can be considered as a partial animal. The adjusted group size Σ sh is equal to N (the
original group size) if all animals live up to the end of the study or if each animal that dies
before the terminal sacrifice develops at least one tumor, otherwise the adjusted group size is
less than N. These adjusted group sizes were then used for the dose response relationship (or
the pairwise) tests using the Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the

Reference ID: 5764018

NDA 220106

Page 9 of 23

choice of the appropriate value of k, which depends on the tumor incidence pattern with the
increased dose. For long term 104 week standard rat and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is
suggested in the literature. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis of this data. For the
calculation of p-values the exact permutation method was used. The tumor rates and the pvalues for the positive dose response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons are listed in
Tables 10 to 11 in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Adjustment for multiple testing: For the chronic study in rats, the adjustment of multiple
testing of the dose response relationship for a submission with one chronic rat study and
one transgenic mouse study, the more recently revised draft (January, 2013) FDA guidance
for the carcinogenicity studies suggests the use of test levels α =0.005 for common tumors
and α=0.025 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study. For pairwise comparisons for the
chronic rat study in the above type of submission with one chronic rat study and one
transgenic mouse study, the same guidance document suggests the use of test levels α
=0.01 for common tumors and α =0.05 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study.
It should be noted that the FDA guidance for multiple testing for dose response relationship
is based on a publication by Lin and Rahman (1998). In this work the authors investigated
the use of this rule for Peto analysis. However, in a later work Rahman and Lin (2008)
showed that this rule for multiple testing for dose response relationship is also suitable for
Poly-K tests.
Reviewer’s findings: The tumor types in Tables 4 and 5 below showed p-values less than or
equal to 0.05 in the tests for dose response (trend), and pairwise comparisons between the
vehicle control and the treated groups for males and females, respectively.
Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed above, we make the
following statistical conclusions:
1.

For male rats, the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive
dose response relationship in the incidence of B-interstitial cell tumor, testis (pvalue=0.0026<0.005)across the vehicle control and the treated groups.

2.

For female rats, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
low dose group showed statistically significant increase in the incidence of B-

Reference ID: 5764018

NDA 220106

Page 10 of 23

thymoma benign, thymus (p-value=0.005<0.01). The pairwise comparisons
between the vehicle control and the mid dose group showed statistically
significant increases in the incidences of B-thymoma benign, thymus (pvalue=0.0093<0.01).
